      - Intended retirement age 66 and date 2026-10-14 for spouse: Report asserts spouse intends to work until age 66 with retirement date 2026-10-14, but no evidence in passages specifies spouse's intended retirement age or date; spouse is stated as age 65 in P5 as at 08/12/2025

(2) The intended retirement date for the HGV driver (2027-10-14) is inferred in the canonical model from a statement made on 14/10/2025 saying 'expect to retire within the next two years', which would place retirement in 2025-2027; the extraction inferred this as October 2027, but the Fact Find contains no explicit confirmation of this date.   [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts: employment income £300/week net (C1 HGV driver); £20/week net (C1 Sea Cadets volunteer); £1,100/month net (C2 employment); State Pension £230/week net (C1 current); State Pension £230/week net (C2 future from 2026); household expenditure £1,700/month; monthly income total £4,486.67; monthly surplus £2,786.67; total assets £6,000; total liabilities £0.

    File:     Fact Find [P10] shows: employed income £1,200 gross basic monthly, £230/month State Pension; total monthly income £1,430. Fact Find [P1] shows: calculated total monthly household expenditure £1,718; total monthly disposable income -£288. Suitability Report [P17] confirms: £300/week typical employment income, £20/week Sea Cadets, £1,100/month Kim employment, £230/week State Pension current, £230/week State Pension future, £1,700/month household expenditure, £6,000 savings, no liabilities. Report [P9] states: £11,973 annual State Pension for C1 (approximately £230/week × 52), £11,973 annual State Pension for C2 from age 67, annual expenditure £1,700.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report derives monthly equivalent from £300/week as £1,300/month; Fact Find [P10] lists only £1,200/month gross basic employed income, creating a £100 discrepancy (8.3% difference) when comparing equivalents

      - Report states household expenditure £1,700/month; Fact Find [P1] states calculated total monthly household expenditure £1,718 (£18 or 1.1% difference)

      - Report monthly income total £4,486.67 (C1 HGV £1,300 + Sea Cadets £86.67 + State Pension £1,000 + C2 employment £1,100); this figure does not appear in evidence and sum cannot be verified against Fact Find [P10] which shows only £1,430 total monthly income

      - Report derives £230/week State Pension as £1,000/month; arithmetic check: £230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £999.33, rounds to £1,000, but Fact Find [P10] shows £230/month State Pension (not weekly), creating ambiguity about basis

    The report-first perspective requires that assertions in the suitability report be supported by evidence. The Fact Find [P1], [P10] and Suitability Report [P17], [P9] contain the primary income and expenditure data. Guard on weekly to monthly conversion: £300/week should annualise at 52 weeks, yielding £300 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300/month, not £1,200. The Fact Find [P10] shows £1,200 gross basic monthly employment income, but the report treats the £300/week as net, deriving £1,300 net monthly. The Fact Find lists gross, report lists net — this is a gross/net comparison issue covered by the false-positive guard, but the underlying employment income stated in both documents (£1,200 gross in Fact Find vs derived £1,300 net in report from £300/week) contains a material £100 discrepancy. The weekly to monthly conversion in the report is arithmetically correct (£300 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300), but the Fact Find gross figure of £1,200 does not reconcile to this. The State Pension figure of £230/week is stated consistently across Suitability Report [P17] and confirmed in [P9] as £11,973 p.a. (≈ £230/week). The report's derivation of £1,000/month (£230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £999.33 rounds to £1,000) is arithmetically sound. Household expenditure shows £1,700 in the report [P17] and £1,718 in Fact Find [P1], a 1.1% difference within rounding tolerance. The report's monthly income total of £4,486.67 is derived but not present in any single evidence document; it is calculated by the extraction system from component parts stated separately. The Fact Find [P10] shows £1,430 as total monthly income, which is substantially lower and appears to be incomplete (missing HGV £1,200 or derived £1,300, Sea Cadets £20, and C2 State Pension £230 future income not yet received). The report separates current income (£3,486.67 for C1 and C2 combined) from future income (C2 State Pension from 2026). No single reconciliation point exists in the evidence for the report's derived £4,486.67 total that excludes future C2 State Pension.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts two state pension positions: (1) the client is currently in receipt of full State Pension of £230 per week; (2) Kim will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age of 67, payable from 2027-10-14. The report also states Kim receives £1,100 per month, which equates to £11,973 per annum in state pension income from age 67 onwards.

    File:     Evidence shows: (1) [P9] quotes 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week' for the client; (2) [P9] states 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age'; (3) [P1] and [P5] identify State Pension Age as 66 for Kim, not 67; (4) [P5] states 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67' but [P1] shows State Pension Age is 66; (5) Planning documents show retirement projected at age 66 in 2026, with State Pension Age listed as 66 in [P1].

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Kim's State Pension age as 67 with payable from date 2027-10-14; evidence in [P1] shows State Pension Age as 66

      - Report projects Kim receiving state pension from age 67; planning documents [P1] and [P5] indicate State Pension Age of 66

      - Payable from date of 2027-10-14 implies age 67 (current age 65 + 2 years = 2027), but [P1] shows retirement 'age 66 in 2026' suggesting State Pension Age 66

    The report contains internal contradiction regarding Kim's State Pension age. The canonical model asserts State Pension age of 67 with a payable-from date of 2027-10-14. However, the intelliflo planning documents [P1] explicitly state 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' under KS (Kim). The evidence also shows retirement projected at age 66 in 2026, which is inconsistent with the stated State Pension age of 67. The £230 per week amount is corroborated by [P9]. The annual amount of £11,973 for state pension income is stated in [P5] but is mapped to age 67 in the report while evidence suggests age 66. The guard notes that state pension age and retirement age are routinely conflated; here the evidence clearly distinguishes State Pension Age as 66 but the report states 67. This is a material factual discrepancy that could affect retirement planning assumptions, cashflow projections, and the timing of income receipt modelled in the suitability analysis.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The extraction identified six internal inconsistencies: (1) People's Pension value £6,601.55 is consistent but recommendation to 'Retain' contradicts objective OBJ3 to consolidate pensions; (2) Risk rating shows questionnaire result of 4 vs agreed/confirmed rating of 5 with unclear sequence; (3) Aviva existing charge 0.50% vs recommended 0.44% (0.21% + 0.22%), but text claims new plan 0.06% more expensive, contradicting the calculation; (4) Fund allocations show same funds (HSBC 50%, CT Universal Map 50%) but with slight naming variance between pages 2 and 12; (5) Employment income stated as 'typically £300 per week net' on page 3 but assumed as deterministic '£300 per week' in cashflow on page 14; (6) Individual existing arrangement values sum to £116,998.47 but stated total transfer value is £110,185, a discrepancy of £6,813.47.

    File:     This check compares the report against itself and does not rely on external evidence. The internal inconsistencies are drawn from the extraction report's own analysis of the canonical model extracted from the suitability report. No supporting document passages are searched or cited; the comparison is internal to the report's stated figures, narratives and calculations.

    Discrepancies:

      - Aviva charge contradiction: page 5 states 0.50% existing; page 11 recommendation table shows 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total; page 6 text states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which contradicts the arithmetic showing new charge is 0.06% cheaper (0.50% - 0.44% = 0.06% reduction)

      - Transfer value arithmetic: sum of five existing arrangements (£6,601.55 + £211.32 + £3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002.00) = £116,998.47 but page 9 states total transfer value as £110,185, creating unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47

      - Employment income qualifier: page 3 describes income as 'typically earning around £300 per week net' suggesting variability, but page 14 cashflow assumptions section lists 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver' without the qualifier 'typically', treating it as deterministic input

      - Risk rating sequence ambiguity: page 4 contains two statements—'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and later 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'—creating unclear causal sequence between questionnaire result (4), adviser assessment/agreement, and client confirmation

      - Fund name consistency: page 2 recommendation summary lists 'CT Universal Map Balanced' and 'HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio' while page 12 lists 'CT Universal MAP' and 'HSBC' with allocations 50% each; the fuller names on page 2 appear to be the same funds but naming inconsistency exists across pages

      - Retention vs consolidation logic: recommendation states 'Retain your people's pension plan - £6,601.55' but objective OBJ3 directs consolidation of pensions; the retention of this one plan while consolidating/switching four others is not reconciled

    All six identified discrepancies are internal contradictions within the report itself and can be assessed without external evidence. Severity assessment: (1) The Aviva charge contradiction is material—page 6 asserts the new plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, but the fee table on page 11 and the arithmetic show the opposite (0.44% new vs 0.50% old = 0.06% cheaper). This is a high-severity self-contradiction on a figure that could drive the recommendation. (2) The transfer value shortfall of £6,813.47 is material and unexplained; it appears the report may have excluded one or more arrangements from the total without stating why, or there is an arithmetic error. This is high severity as transfer value is a driver of the recommendation. (3) The employment income qualifier ('typically' vs deterministic) is a moderate concern; the cashflow model treats £300/week as fixed but the narrative suggests it varies, which affects reliability of the financial projection. (4) The risk rating sequence is ambiguous but does not constitute a numerical contradiction—both statements conclude to risk rating 5, so the final position is clear even if the path to it is unclear; this is low-to-moderate concern affecting the documentation of consent. (5) Fund naming inconsistency (Universal Map vs MAP; full name vs abbreviation) appears to refer to the same funds and is a low-severity presentation issue. (6) The retention of People's Pension contradicts the stated consolidation objective, but the extraction notes this is a logical/strategic inconsistency rather than a data mismatch on the figure itself—still material and moderate severity. The core numerical contradictions are the Aviva charge statement (high severity) and the transfer value shortfall (high severity).

 

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts five income streams with the following net values and frequencies: (1) £300 per week (HGV employment); (2) £20 per week (Sea Cadets volunteer); (3) £1,100 per month (spouse Kim employment); (4) £230 per week (client State Pension); (5) £230 per week (spouse State Pension future). Report derives monthly equivalents: £1,300, £86.67, £1,100, £1,000, £1,000 respectively. Report states total monthly net income as £4,486.67. Report states monthly expenditure as £1,700 net. Single expenditure category listed with monthly frequency.

    File:     Fact Find (Test 1) [P2] shows Total Net Monthly Income: £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure: £1,718.00. Fact Find [P2] details income: Basic Income (CDH Recruitment) Monthly £1,200.00 net; State Pension Monthly £230.00. Fact Find [P1] and [P2] show expenditure totalling £1,718.00 monthly. Fact Find [P4] shows emergency savings of £6,000.00. Retirement Planning document [P5] states client "needs a household income of £1,700 net per month" in retirement. File notes [P9] discuss consolidation and pension switching.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts £300/week HGV income; Fact Find [P2] shows £1,200.00/month (equivalent to £276.92/week at 52-week annualisation), a 7.7% variance.

      - Report asserts £20/week Sea Cadets income; no corroborating figure found in fact find. Nearest is £25.00/month entertainment expenditure [P2], which is 25% away and is expenditure not income.

      - Report asserts £1,100/month spouse income; Fact Find [P2] shows £1,200.00/month basic income, a 9.1% variance.

      - Report asserts £230/week State Pension (client); Fact Find [P2] confirms £230.00/month, which at 52-week annualisation is £4.42/week, not £230/week. The report has scaled a monthly figure as if weekly.

      - Report asserts £230/week spouse State Pension (future); Fact Find [P2] confirms £230.00/month for spouse, not £230/week.

      - Report derives total monthly income of £4,486.67; recalculation from stated values (£1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 + £1,000) yields £4,486.67, but reconciliation against fact find fails: £1,200 + £230 = £1,430 monthly (actual from [P2]), not £4,486.67.

      - Report states monthly expenditure £1,700; Fact Find [P1] and [P2] show Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure £1,718.00, a £18 variance within tolerance but presented without qualification.

    Critical frequency/basis mismatch identified. The report asserts State Pension income at £230 per week for both client and spouse, but Fact Find [P2] evidences £230.00 per month. At 52-week annualisation, £230/month = £4.42/week net, not £230/week. This is a 52-fold error in the frequency conversion, not a rounding variance. The report's derivation of £1,000/month equivalent for each State Pension (implying £230/week × 52 weeks ÷ 12 months = £1,000) confirms the report treated £230 as a weekly figure when the source is monthly. The HGV employment income shows 7.7% variance (£300/week report vs £276.92/week equivalent from £1,200/month in fact find), within the 1% tolerance when expressed consistently. However, the spouse employment income asserts £1,100/month against fact find evidence of £1,200/month, a 9.1% variance exceeding the 1% tolerance. The £20/week Sea Cadets income has no corroborating evidence in the fact find; the only adjacent figure is £25 monthly expenditure, not income. The total monthly income derived by the report (£4,486.67) cannot be reconciled to the fact find's stated Total Net Monthly Income (£1,430.00), a 214% discrepancy. The monthly expenditure asserted (£1,700) differs from fact find by £18 (£1,718 actual), within rounding tolerance. The report's income calculations appear to use the wrong frequency for State Pension (treating monthly as weekly) and to include income streams not clearly evidenced in the fact find (Sea Cadets £20/week) or with materially different values (spouse employment). These errors cascade through the model's cashflow and affordability analysis, affecting the recommendation's foundation. The guard permitting gross/net reconciliation is not engaged here because all figures are asserted as net; the guard permitting weekly annualisation at 52 weeks is engaged correctly for HGV income but misapplied to State Pension figures that originate as monthly amounts.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts a retirement target date of 2027-10-14 (October 2027) with a term of 2 years and time horizons classified as 'LongTerm', 'MediumTerm', and 'MediumTerm'. The report also states an intended retirement age of 66.

    File:     The intelliflo planning proposed scenario [P10] shows a timeline with retirement ('Retire') for John at age 71 in 2027, with state pension age 66. The risk profile report [P11] specifies an investment period of 2 years with investment of £106,000. The Zurich Policy Info [P33] shows a retirement date of 7 June 2031 (age 75) for one arrangement. The Aviva Pension policy information [P12] references a default age 75 retirement unless client directs otherwise. The Pension Switch Report [P1] uses retirement age 75 as the illustration baseline for Aviva. The Policy Research document [P34] notes 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years' and references ATR 5.

    Discrepancies:

      - Retirement target date stated as 2027-10-14 in report but timeline in [P10] shows retirement age 71 in 2027, not age 66 as also stated in report

      - Retirement age stated as 66 in report canonical model but [P10] shows retire at age 71 in 2027 for John (born 1956, age 69 in 2025)

      - Zurich arrangement [P33] has retirement date 7 June 2031 (age 75), contradicting both the 2027 date and age 66/71 stated elsewhere

      - Multiple pension illustrations use age 75 as default retirement age ([P1], [P12]) rather than ages 66 or 71

      - 2-year term in report matches [P11] investment period but does not reconcile with 2027 retirement date if client is currently 69 years old in 2025, which would place retirement at age 71 in 2027 (not 2 years from now)

    The report presents internal inconsistencies and contradictions with evidence that prevent full reconciliation. The canonical model extracts retirement age as 66 and target date as 2027-10-14, yielding approximately 2 years. However, the intelliflo proposed scenario [P10] explicitly shows John retiring at age 71 in 2027, not age 66. This is consistent with his current age of 69 (born 07/06/1956 per [P10]) reaching 71 in 2027. The 2-year investment horizon in [P11] refers to the investment period for the current transfer, not the full remaining working life to retirement. The Zurich policy document [P33] states a retirement date of age 75 (7 June 2031), which contradicts both the 2027 target and the ages mentioned. The pension switch report baseline uses age 75 as the standard retirement illustration age, a common industry practice for defined-benefit-style projections. The Policy Research brief [P34] mentions 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years', which aligns with a 2-year planning horizon for the investment but does not indicate immediate retirement; rather, it suggests the decision-making and organization phase is 2 years. The guard 'A pension may have a long investment horizon while the income objective is short' applies here: the 2-year term appears to refer to the time to make the pension consolidation and investment decision, not the time to retirement. The guard 'The switch analysis states its own retirement age and term, and is routinely a different figure' is also relevant: [P1], [P33], and [P12] each embed their own retirement age assumptions (75, 75, and default 75 respectively) for illustration purposes, independent of the client's stated intention. The actual stated client objective per [P34] is to 'probably retire in the next 2 years', which suggests 2-year planning horizon, not a 2027 target per se. Reconciliation: The report's target date of 2027-10-14 is corroborated by [P10] retirement event in 2027; the retirement age of 71 (not 66) is evident from [P10] timeline and birth date; the 2-year term refers to the investment decision/consolidation horizon, not years to retirement. The assertion of age 66 as intended retirement age is not supported; [P10] shows 66 as state pension age eligibility, not retirement age. No evidence supports retiring at age 66.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts four foreseeable changes: (1) Client retirement expected within 2 years (by 2027-10-14), with financial impact of -£1,386.67/month net from loss of HGV employment income (£1,300/month) and Sea Cadets income (£86.67/month); (2) Kim's retirement at age 66 (2026-03-26), with financial impact of -£1,100/month from loss of employment income; (3) Kim's State Pension commencement at age 66 (2026-03-26), with financial impact of +£1,000/month; (4) Inheritance Tax regime change for pensions from 6 April 2027, with no quantified financial impact stated.

    File:     Evidence supports: Client (JS) aged 69, currently earning £300/week net as HGV driver (P6: 'typically earning around £300 per week net') plus £20/week from Sea Cadets volunteer role (P6), totalling approximately £1,386.67/month. Intelliflo planning scenarios show retirement events: Kim 'Retire' age 66 in 2026, John 'Retire' age 71 in 2027 (P1, P8). IHT rule change from 6 April 2027 mentioned in P3 and P6. Fact Find (P9) records retirement planning objective: 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.'

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states client retirement expected date as 2027-10-14 (age 71), but evidence shows client is currently 69 (born 07/06/1956 per P20), making retirement in 2 years approximately 2027 (age 71), which aligns. However, P1 and P8 intelliflo planning timelines state 'Retire' age 71 in 2027, consistent with report.

      - Report states Kim's retirement date as 2026-03-26 (age 66), and Kim's State Pension commencement as 2026-03-26. However, P10 states 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67', indicating State Pension age 67, not 66. P6 states she 'will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age' but does not explicitly state the age. Evidence does not clearly confirm whether Kim's State Pension starts at age 66 or 67.

      - Report quantifies Kim's State Pension financial impact as +£1,000/month. Evidence states £230 per week (P6, P10), which equals approximately £997/month (£230 × 52 weeks ÷ 12 months), close to £1,000 but not exactly stated as monthly figure in evidence.

      - Report states Kim's employment income loss as -£1,100/month. P6 states 'Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net', which aligns.

      - Report states client income loss of -£1,386.67/month combining HGV employment £1,300/month and Sea Cadets £86.67/month. P6 states 'typically earning around £300 per week net' (HGV) and 'additional income of £20 per week from your volunteer role with the Sea Cadets' (P6). £300/week = £1,300/month, £20/week = £86.67/month, totalling £1,386.67/month. This aligns with report.

      - Report includes IHT regime change as foreseeable change with date 2027-04-06 but states no financial impact. Evidence (P3, P6) confirms the change occurs from '6 April 2027' with description matching. However, a guard applies: legislative changes affecting everyone are not client-specific foreseeable changes and should not be counted as client-disclosed foreseeable changes for this requirement.

    The report's foreseeable changes are largely supported by evidence. Client retirement in approximately 2 years (2027, age 71) is supported by P6, P3, P9 stating 'expect to retire within the next two years'. Income figures for both client and Kim align with evidence. However, there is a material discrepancy regarding Kim's State Pension age: the report asserts Kim's State Pension commences at age 66 (2026-03-26), but P10 explicitly states 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67', suggesting State Pension age is 67. The £230/week cited in P6 does not include an age qualifier, creating ambiguity. The monthly equivalent of £230/week (£997) approximates the £1,000 stated by the report. The IHT regime change cited in the report is described in evidence but is a legislative change affecting all pension savers, not a client-specific foreseeable change, per the guard. The Intelliflo planning scenarios (P1, P8) show Kim retiring at age 66 in 2026 and John at age 71 in 2027, which aligns with the report's dates. The financial impacts (income losses and gains) are arithmetically consistent with the evidence-derived income figures.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report states three objectives with priority ranks 1, 2, and 3. One objective has a target date of 2027-10-14. The report asserts: (1) 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime'; (2) 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month'; (3) 'You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.' The retirement target is 2 years from report date (2025), placing retirement in 2027. The report recommends a long-term investment strategy in a balanced multi-asset portfolio with a 4.8% critical yield required up to age 75, implying a growth horizon extending approximately 6 years beyond the stated 2027 retirement date.

    File:     Evidence P4 confirms: 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime. You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month. You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement.' Evidence P11 (intelliflo planning proposed scenario) shows timeline events: '2025—69/65 [First Year], 2026—70/66 [Retire (Kim)], 2027—71/67 [Retire (John)]'. Evidence P9 and P5 (cashflow projections) extend projections to 2049 and beyond, showing planned expenditure and asset drawdown through advanced ages (89/85, 93/89). Evidence P8 states 'The retirement age used is 75' for critical yield calculations. Evidence P16 shows first-year household income assumptions of £28,613 (JS) plus £13,200 (spouse) = £41,813 total. Evidence P15 shows opening pension values totalling £116,997. The cashflow models assume £1,700 net monthly household requirement (£20,400 annually) and demonstrate surplus income being spent (0% saved per P8).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's investment strategy is projected to age 75 (P8: 'The retirement age used is 75'), which is 6 years beyond the stated retirement date of 2027 when clients will be ages 71/67. This creates a structural mismatch: the capital is committed to growth for 6 years after the stated retirement date, yet the objectives prioritise retiring in 2 years with a defined household income requirement.

      - The report recommends a balanced risk rating 5 portfolio (P3: HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%, both risk rating 5) designed for long-term capital growth. The critical yield analysis (P8) requires 4.8% mid-growth return up to age 75 to meet projected value of £115,000. However, the first stated objective is to 'ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime', which is a cashflow objective, not a capital growth objective. The strategy prioritises growth over income in the post-retirement period.

      - The report does not explicitly state target amounts for any of the three objectives. Objective 1 (lifetime affordability) has no quantified target. Objective 2 (retire with £1,700 net per month household income) is quantified only as an income flow, not a capital target required to generate it. Objective 3 (pension consolidation) is structural, not quantified. The extracted model shows /objectives[]/targetAmount is absent. This prevents verification that the recommended solution's projected values align with the capital requirements of each objective.

      - The cashflow evidence (P5, P9, P14) shows 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' consistently positive and at 0% savings assumption (P8), meaning all surplus is spent. The model shows stable liquid assets around £122,950-£122,997 through the projection period. This contradicts the implicit assumption in the growth-focused strategy that capital will accumulate to support future years: the model assumes all investment growth is consumed as lifestyle enhancement, not preserved.

      - The report recommends consolidating five existing pensions into one Aviva arrangement (P4 objective 3). However, the strategic recommendation to switch the Zurich arrangement (£103,439.24, the largest holding) into a balanced growth portfolio conflicts with the near-term retirement objective: the Zurich holding represents 88.5% of total pension capital, and switching it into a 6-year growth strategy (to age 75) contradicts a 2-year retirement timeframe. No alternative strategy (e.g., phased drawdown, annuity, or income-focused fund) is presented as aligned to the 2027 retirement date.

    The requirement tests whether stated objectives are consistent with each other and with the evidence, and whether the recommendation aligns to the stated time horizons and access dates. Three discrepancies emerge: (1) Temporal mismatch: The report asserts a 2-year retirement horizon (2027) but projects growth to age 75 (2031/2032), a 6-year extension beyond retirement. The critical yield calculation (P8) is framed as the growth rate 'required from the new solution to match the projected maturity value', implying capital growth is a primary metric, yet the retirement objective requires only £1,700 net monthly income, not maximum capital accumulation. (2) Strategic misalignment: The balanced risk rating 5 portfolio (both funds rated 5, P3) is justified as 'professional management and diversified investment solution that aligns with your financial goals' (P3), but no evidence links this strategy to the specific £1,700 monthly income requirement or demonstrates it is appropriate for clients retiring in 2 years. Instead, the strategy prioritises multi-asset diversification and cost efficiency (P3), which serve long-term growth, not near-term income. The guard permits this comparison: no false-positive guard applies. The objectives are structurally visible (P4, P11), the timelines are explicit (P11), and the strategy's horizon (age 75, P8) is stated. (3) Absence of quantified targets: The canonical model shows only one targetDate (2027-10-14) and no targetAmounts. Objective 1 (lifetime affordability) lacks any quantified metric. Objective 2's £1,700 monthly income is stated verbally but the report does not specify the capital required at retirement to generate this (under the drawdown or annuity model assumed). Objective 3 is administrative (consolidation) with no financial metric. Without targetAmounts, the comparison between strategy and objectives cannot be completed: it is unknown whether the projected £115,000 (mid-growth, P8) or £127,000 (overall position with retained plans, P20) is sufficient to support the income objective. This is a missing input preventing full assessment. (4) Cashflow inconsistency: The planning model (P5, P9, P14) shows constant liquid assets through 35 years of projection, with 0% surplus savings (P8). This implies the recommendation assumes all investment growth will be spent, contradicting the growth-focused strategy. If growth is not preserved, the strategy's critical yield of 4.8% (P8) serves only to offset charges, not to accumulate capital for longevity. This is internally inconsistent: the investment strategy prioritises growth, yet the cashflow model assumes growth is spent. Vulnerability overlay: The file notes (P10-P11) indicate no recorded vulnerability drivers or support needs affecting this assessment. However, the ambiguity in the extraction report about risk rating (4 vs 5) and the mismatch between stated 2-year retirement and 6-year growth horizon could indicate that the adviser's assessment of risk capacity and time horizon was not clearly resolved with the clients, particularly relevant given they are transitioning to retirement imminently.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report links six recommendations to three objectives: OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3. The first recommendation (Retain People's Pension - £6,601.55) is linked to OBJ3. The second recommendation (Switch Scottish Widows - £211.32) is linked to OBJ3. The third recommendation (Switch Aviva pension - £3,744.36) is linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3. The fourth recommendation (Switch Zurich - £103,439.24) is linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3. The fifth recommendation (Switch Standard Life - £3,002) is linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3. The sixth recommendation (Aviva Pension Portfolio with £110,185 transfer value) is linked to OBJ1 and OBJ2. The report states a selected retirement age of 71 for the main recommendation, with projections shown at age 75. The client needs £1,700 net per month in retirement. Flexi-access Drawdown is recommended as the withdrawal mechanism. The fund allocation is 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced.

    File:     The evidence establishes: (1) A stated retirement income need of £1,700 net per month (P1). (2) Current employment income of £300 per week net as an HGV driver, described as 'typically' £300 per week (page 3 of report per extraction), but modelled deterministically as £300 per week in cashflow (P10). (3) State pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67 for the client and spouse (P10). (4) Annual expenditure in retirement expected to be approximately £1,700 (P10), which aligns with the monthly figure stated. (5) Pension access available from age 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028, with the ability to take 25% tax-free lump sum under Flexi-Access Drawdown (P6, P7, P19). (6) Projections at age 75 under mid-growth scenario: existing pension £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease (P14, P15). (7) Critical yield of 4.8% required to match existing arrangement (P9, P14, P15). (8) Small pension pots under £10,000 can be taken as lump sums with first 25% tax-free (P19). (9) No explicit statement of client objectives by name (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) in the retrieved evidence. The objectives themselves are not defined or evidenced in the passages provided. (10) Cash flow runs to age 100 with no projected shortfall under baseline scenario (P10). (11) Employment income noted as variable ('typically') on page 3 but modelled as fixed £300/week.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report links recommendations to OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3, but the evidence does not define or corroborate these objectives by name or their specific content. The actual client goals are not stated with named objective identifiers in any retrieved passage.

      - The report states a selected retirement age of 71 for the recommended plan, but projections are shown at age 75 (P14, P15), creating ambiguity about the intended retirement date and whether the plan is timed correctly to the stated goal.

      - The report identifies employment income as 'typically' £300 per week (per extraction from page 3), suggesting variability, but the cashflow model applies this deterministically without adjusting for the stated variability or modeling downside scenarios.

      - The report's second recommendation states 'Retain your people's pension plan' valued at £6,601.55, but the extraction notes this contradicts advice to consolidate pensions (OBJ3) and switch other four plans to Aviva, creating internal inconsistency in whether consolidation is an objective.

      - The critical yield of 4.8% required to match the existing arrangement is substantially above expected mid-growth rate of 2.95%, yet the report concludes the recommendation is achievable based on past performance. This creates a material discrepancy between the yield required and the growth assumption used to justify suitability.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Definition and content of the three stated objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) - the objectives themselves are not articulated in any retrieved passage, Explicit corroboration that the named objectives are client-authored or client-confirmed rather than adviser-generated boilerplate, Clarity on the intended retirement date (71 vs 75) and whether the selected retirement age of 71 is a firm goal or planning assumption, Evidence of how the recommendations specifically serve each linked objective beyond generic statements about pension flexibility, Documentation of client priorities or hierarchy among the three objectives where they may conflict, Explanation of the material shortfall between the 4.8% critical yield requirement and the 2.95% mid-growth assumption used to project fund values, Evidence of any foreseeable life changes beyond the stated expectation of no change in expenditure (P24)

    This check requires that stated client goals and objectives be supported by evidence and that recommendations be explicitly linked to specific objectives rather than boilerplate. Three critical gaps emerge:

   

    1. Objectives Not Evidenced: The report asserts linkages to OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3, but the evidence does not retrieve or define these objectives. The fact-find (P24) records 'No' to expected expenditure changes, but does not articulate the three named objectives or confirm they are client-driven. The extraction's ambiguities section notes the report is internally inconsistent about whether to consolidate pensions, undermining the coherence of the objective framework itself.

   

    2. Retirement Age Ambiguity: The report selects a retirement age of 71 for the recommended plan but projects outcomes at age 75. The evidence does not clarify whether 71 is the stated goal or merely an intermediate planning assumption. At age 69 (current age per data), access to a pension fund carries no statutory restriction from age 55 onwards; the guard 'Check the access mechanism, not the wrapper name' is therefore satisfied. However, the mismatch between selected and projected retirement age suggests the recommendation may not be timed to the stated goal.

   

    3. Critical Yield vs Growth Assumption: The pension switch analysis (P14, P15) shows that the new solution requires 4.8% critical yield to match the existing arrangement, but the mid-growth rate used in recommendations is 2.95%. This 1.85 percentage point gap is material. The report states (P9) that past performance suggests 4.8% 'is achievable,' but the evidence does not support this claim with specific fund track records. The projection at mid-growth shows a £12,000 decrease (9.4%), which directly contradicts the suitability assertion unless the report explicitly accepts this as a cost of achieving other objectives—which it does not state clearly in the evidence retrieved.

   

    4. Boilerplate Linkage: The recommendation text (P16) states 'It is the most suitable solution given your circumstances and retirement objectives' and lists generic benefits of personal pensions (tax relief, flexibility, drawdown options, death benefits, IHT treatment). These are standard pension features, not specific to the client's named objectives. No passage states 'This recommendation serves OBJ2 because you stated that [specific goal].' The linkage is by category (all recommendations linked to pension consolidation, drawdown, or longevity) rather than by client-specific objective naming.

   

    5. Access Needs: The evidence confirms the client can access funds from age 55 (rising to 57) and prefers Flexi-Access Drawdown (P16). Retirement income need of £1,700 net per month is stated (P1, P10). The plan projects sufficient assets to age 100 without shortfall under baseline (P10). The access mechanism is available and appropriate to the stated need. However, whether the recommended fund allocation (50% HSBC, 50% CT Universal MAP, both risk rating 5) is aligned with a 71-year-old's ability to sustain volatility for 4+ years to retirement is not explicitly addressed.

   

    6. Foreseeable Changes: The fact-find (P24) records 'No' to expected expenditure changes. Employment income is noted as 'typically' £300 per week, suggesting variability, but no scenario modeling for income reduction is evident. No other foreseeable changes (health, family, housing, relocation) are mentioned in the retrieved evidence.

   

    Conclusion on Comparison: The comparison cannot be fully performed because the objectives themselves (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) are not defined in the evidence. The report asserts linkages but the evidence does not retrieve the definitions of what is being linked to. The recommendations are appropriate to the factual needs (retirement income £1,700/month, access from 55/57, tax-efficient drawdown) but are not demonstrably linked to client-authored objectives by name. The critical yield shortfall is a material concern: a 9.4% reduction in projected fund value at the assumed mid-growth rate, balanced only by generic claims about past performance, is not a secure basis for suitability when the client is age 69 and retirement is imminent.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.9, G3.10). The report labels the capacity for loss rating as 'Medium' but no document defines what 'Medium' means on a formal CFL scale or establishes a classification framework that includes 'Medium' as a discrete rating level. The extraction metadata identifies the CFL assessment provenance quote as 'PresentButUnparseable', indicating the source text cannot be cleanly extracted or parsed, suggesting ambiguity or unclear wording in the original document. The extraction metadata records an internal inconsistency: the report's natural risk level questionnaire shows rating 4 'Cautious Balanced' (P19) but the agreed risk level is stated as 5 'Balanced' (P11, P17), with inconsistent explanations of whether this was adviser-led assessment or client-initiated increase. This affects whether CFL is truly separately assessed from ATR or is merely restated as a consequence of the upward override. 12 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts a questionnaire-assessed risk rating of 4 ('Cautious Balanced') on a 1-10 scale, and an agreed risk rating of 5 ('Balanced') on the same 1-10 scale, also described as 'Balanced'. The report states the client agreed to increase from 4 to 5 and confirmed this preference.

    File:     The evidence corroborates both figures. P1 confirms questionnaire result 4 labelled 'Cautious Balanced' on a 1-10 scale. P2, P6, and P9 all confirm agreed risk level 5 labelled 'Balanced' on the same 1-10 scale. P4 states 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'. P10 confirms fund recommendations carry a risk rating of 5. P19 (file notes) lists existing plans with different risk ratings (Aviva 2, Zurich 7, Standard Life 4) providing context for the override decision.

    The report's assertion is fully supported. The questionnaire-assessed code (4) is corroborated in P1. The agreed code (5) is corroborated across multiple passages (P2, P6, P9, P4). Both are on the same 1-10 scale, so cross-scale contamination does not apply. The override from 4 to 5 is not a discrepancy but an intentional adviser-client agreement documented in P4, which explicitly justifies the increase: the adviser determined the questionnaire result did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite, and the client confirmed willingness to increase the rating to 5 to achieve growth objectives over a 10-year investment period with adequate financial capacity. The label 'Balanced' is consistently applied to the agreed rating 5 across all passages. No conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels exist. The extraction ambiguity concerning whether the final agreed rating is 4 or 5 is resolved by the evidence: the agreed rating is definitively 5, confirmed in P2, P6, P9 and P4. The questionnaire result of 4 remains recorded separately, which is appropriate and expected in an override scenario. No unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements exist regarding the risk rating itself.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts a capacity for loss rating of 'Medium'. The assessment basis comprises six stated elements: (1) State Pension income of approximately £230 per week; (2) employment income as HGV driver of around £300 per week net plus £20 per week from Sea Cadets role; (3) spouse Kim contributes £1,100 per month from employment and will receive State Pension of around £230 per week at State Pension age; (4) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (5) savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (6) confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living.

    File:     The evidence supports each of the six financial elements cited by the report. P11 corroborates: State Pension £230 per week, employment income £300 per week net, additional £20 per week from Sea Cadets, spouse contribution £1,100 per month and spouse State Pension £230 per week, household expenditure £1,700 per month, and savings £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities. The financial planning documents P1, P2, P4, P9, P12 show baseline and proposed scenarios with total assets of £122,997 (liquid assets £6,000, pensions £116,997). P4 and P9 project surplus income throughout the plan period with positive in-year surplus consistently shown as £20,000+ annually. P15 records stress testing at -30% market crash combined with 6% inflation showing no shortfall requiring pension funds to be used. P5, P14 and P20 document risk profile discussions but record 'No additional notes were recorded' for capacity for loss assessments. P19 shows the natural risk level questionnaire result as 4 'Cautious Balanced', but P11 and P17 show agreed risk level of 5 'Balanced'. The extraction metadata notes the capacity for loss assessment provenance quote is 'PresentButUnparseable', and describes ambiguity: 'Page 4 describes capacity for loss as medium but justification refers to income comfortably meeting expenditure with savings and pension funds as basis. Unclear whether medium is a formal classification or descriptive.'

    Discrepancies:

      - The report labels the capacity for loss rating as 'Medium' but no document defines what 'Medium' means on a formal CFL scale or establishes a classification framework that includes 'Medium' as a discrete rating level.

      - The extraction metadata identifies the CFL assessment provenance quote as 'PresentButUnparseable', indicating the source text cannot be cleanly extracted or parsed, suggesting ambiguity or unclear wording in the original document.

      - The extraction metadata records an internal inconsistency: the report's natural risk level questionnaire shows rating 4 'Cautious Balanced' (P19) but the agreed risk level is stated as 5 'Balanced' (P11, P17), with inconsistent explanations of whether this was adviser-led assessment or client-initiated increase. This affects whether CFL is truly separately assessed from ATR or is merely restated as a consequence of the upward override.

      - The Risk Profile Report (P5, P14, P20) records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the Capacity for loss questionnaire responses and for Capacity for loss for the goal, suggesting no documented discussion or assessment rationale beyond the questionnaire itself.

      - While the financial metrics supporting CFL (income, expenditure, surplus, savings, liabilities) are corroborated, the leap from 'combined income comfortably meets expenditure with savings and pension funds as basis' to a 'Medium' CFL rating lacks explicit reasoning about what threshold, percentage, or duration of loss would trigger detriment to day-to-day living.

    The report's six-point assessment basis for capacity for loss is factually corroborated by the evidence. Each financial element—State Pension, employment income, spouse income, household expenditure, liquid savings, and absence of liabilities—matches corresponding figures in P11 and is consistent with the financial planning models in P1, P2, P4, P9, P12. The financial stress testing in P15 (showing resilience to -30% loss plus 6% inflation) and the baseline and proposed scenario cashflow projections showing persistent annual surplus support the adviser's conclusion that potential underperformance would not trigger detriment to day-to-day living.

   

    However, two substantive concerns arise:

   

    1. The 'Medium' CFL rating is not mapped to any defined scale or framework in the evidence. Unlike the risk rating which is explicitly mapped to a 1-5 scale (P17, P19, P20 show ratings 4 and 5 with textual definitions), the CFL rating 'Medium' appears descriptive rather than formally classified. The extraction metadata flags this ambiguity explicitly. No document provides a CFL classification system (e.g., Low-Medium-High, or a numeric scale) against which 'Medium' can be validated as a chosen point.

   

    2. The assessment appears to rest primarily on financial position (income suffices, savings exist, no liabilities) without explicit quantification of actual capacity for loss—i.e., what percentage or absolute loss could be tolerated before day-to-day living is affected. The guard instructs that 'A CFL rating with no supporting financial reasoning is not evidenced merely because the words appear.' Here, the financial position is evidenced but the interpretive step from position to 'Medium' rating is not. The Risk Profile documentation (P5, P14, P20) records no additional notes on CFL assessment, and P5 shows the CFL questionnaire section as blank with 'No additional notes were recorded'.

   

    3. The extraction metadata records the CFL assessment provenance quote as 'PresentButUnparseable', which indicates that the source text in the original report could not be cleanly parsed. This suggests the CFL assessment in the report may be ambiguously worded or embedded in a way that made extraction uncertain. This technical flag, combined with the noted internal inconsistency between questionnaire result (rating 4) and agreed risk level (rating 5), raises questions about whether CFL was assessed independently or inferred as a restatement of the agreed ATR override.

   

    4. The guard cautions: 'Capacity for loss restated as a consequence of attitude to risk (medium CFL, consistent with your ATR of 5) is a weak assessment—flag it as such rather than as a value mismatch.' The evidence does not show the report explicitly linking CFL to the ATR 5 override as a consequence, but the timing, absence of CFL questionnaire notes (P5, P14), and the lack of documented CFL discussion separate from the ATR discussion (which P11 shows was detailed) raises the inference risk. The CFL conclusion may be an unstated consequence of the agreed ATR 5 rather than a standalone assessment.

   

    5. Vulnerability overlay: The evidence does not identify actual or potential vulnerability drivers, support needs, or communication adjustments that would materially affect this CFL assessment. The financial position and stress testing do not indicate material vulnerability affecting capacity for loss.

   

    The financial basis is sound and corroborated. The CFL rating label, framework, and independence from the ATR decision are not.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts tolerance for loss through two statements: (1) 'You are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' (page 4, Capacity for loss section); (2) 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' (page 4, Attitude to Risk & Capacity for loss section). No quantified maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is stated. No separate tolerance for loss classification or figure is provided. The report bases capacity assessment on financial position: client receives £230/week state pension, £300/week as HGV driver, £20/week from Sea Cadets role; spouse contributes £1,100/month; underperformance 'would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living'; client 'is not reliant upon pension funds' and confirmed 'you wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period'. Risk rating agreed at 5 'Balanced'. Stress test conducted for -30% immediate market crash with 6% inflation shows 'no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used'.

    File:     Evidence confirms awareness statements in Suitability Report. P18 quotes: 'You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value' and 'you are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall'. Risk profile documents (P1, P14) describe risk level 5 'Balanced' as suitable for investors who understand risk and can accept combination of investments with differing risk levels. P11 Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses show: question 'If you invested £20,000 and the value fell temporarily, how much of a fall, over a 12 month period, would make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?' was answered 'More than £4,000'. This is the only quantified loss figure in the pack. No separate need-level risk classification exists in the evidence. P26 shows ATR questionnaire response to 'In seeking a return on my investment, in the short-term I'm willing to accept:' was answered 'A slight risk of capital loss'. No formal tolerance for loss quantification or classification documented beyond the ATR questionnaire response and the £4,000 reference.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states tolerance for loss through general awareness statements only; no quantified maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is stated in the report itself

      - Report does not reference or explain the £4,000 loss threshold that appears in the Capacity for Loss questionnaire (P11)

      - Report characterises capacity for loss assessment as 'medium' in extraction notes but this term does not appear in the actual suitability report text provided

      - Report provides no separate tolerance for loss classification or metric distinct from attitude to risk and capacity for loss

    The guard specifies that many firms do not quantify tolerance for loss separately; absence is a documentation gap, not a contradiction. The report asserts client awareness of downside risk through two quoted statements on page 4, and both statements are corroborated verbatim in the evidence (P18). The stress test on page 23 models a -30% market crash scenario and shows no shortfall, demonstrating the plan was tested for material loss. The agreed risk rating of 5 'Balanced' is supported by the Risk Profile Report (P1, P14, P19), which confirms the client's agreed risk level. The Capacity for Loss questionnaire (P11) contains a quantified response of 'More than £4,000' to a temporary fall scenario on £20,000 invested over 12 months, but the suitability report does not cite or incorporate this threshold. The report's capacity assessment relies instead on stated income (£230+£300+£20+£1,100 monthly spouse contribution) meeting expenditure comfortably with pension funds as buffer—a backward-looking financial position assessment rather than a forward-looking loss tolerance quantification. The extraction's note that capacity for loss appears as 'medium' cannot be located in the canonical report text; this may reflect extraction interpretation rather than explicit statement. No objective need-level risk metric is documented in either the report or the evidence pack. The absence of quantified tolerance for loss is consistent with the guard: this is a documentation practice gap, not a factual contradiction between what the report claims and what the evidence supports.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Investment term: 10 years. Objectives time horizons: Long Term, Medium Term, Medium Term.

    File:     Risk Profile report (P3) states 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the investment goal details table. SIPP research report (P9) references an agreed risk level of 5 'Balanced' but does not specify a term. Intelliflo planning scenarios (P4-P8) project cash flow through 2060, with retirement events at age 66 (2026) and age 71 (2027), extending approximately 30+ years from 2025. Key Features (P13) advises that initial costs mean investments should be viewed as 'medium to long term (typically more than five years)'.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts 10-year investment term; Risk Profile report evidence shows 2-year Investment Period in the projections table (P3)

      - Report objectives include 'Long Term' horizon; Risk Profile document shows 2-year investment period, which is neither long term nor consistent with a 10-year assertion

      - No evidence in SIPP research report (P9) or other passages explicitly confirms a 10-year term; the longest documented horizon is the planning projection to 2060 (30+ years)

      - Mismatch between stated 10-year term and 2-year Investment Period used in risk projection modelling

    The canonical model asserts an investment term of 10 years. The Risk Profile report (P3), which is the primary document evidencing how the agreed risk level was determined, explicitly states 'Investment Period: 2 years' within the 'Risk level justification' section where projections are modelled. This is a material discrepancy: the report grounds its risk analysis on a 2-year projection, not a 10-year term. The objectives contain mixed horizons (Long Term, Medium Term, Medium Term), none explicitly labelled as 10 years. The Intelliflo planning scenarios project to age 100 (2060), representing a 35-year horizon for comprehensive financial planning, but this is distinct from the investment term used for risk profiling. The Key Features document notes that initial costs mean investments 'should be viewed as medium to long term (typically more than five years)', which neither aligns with 2 years nor clearly supports 10 years. The guard permits a longer investment horizon post-retirement, but the report must state this; no such explanation is provided. The 10-year term in the canonical model is not corroborated by the evidence, and the 2-year Investment Period used in the actual risk projection contradicts it. This is a material inconsistency: the risk level was justified using a 2-year horizon in the stochastic projections, yet the report asserts a 10-year term without explanation for the discrepancy or rationale for why the agreed risk level remains suitable under a longer horizon.

      · Risk profile report.md [D] (P3)

        "Investment Period   | 2 years"

      · Risk profile report.md [D] (P2)

        "To project the potential outcomes, each risk level is mapped to a collection of asset classes designed to expose you to an appropriate level of risk. Projections are produced by passing the asset class breakdown for the selected risk level to a stochastic engine that uses modelling provided by Hymans Robertson to project the future performance of the combination of asset classes."

      · Key Features (1).md [H] (P13)

        "Initial costs will have an immediate effect on the value of those investments, so you should view such investments as medium to long term (typically more than five years)."

 

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report recommends a target risk rating of 5 (Balanced). The investment strategy comprises two funds in a 50/50 allocation: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, 0.18% OCF) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, 0.29% OCF). Both funds are stated to be risk-rated at level 5, matching the client's agreed risk rating of 5. The strategy is described as a Model Portfolio approach.

    File:     [P10] confirms the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced). [P11] confirms the fund allocation: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 50% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C at 50%, with risk ratings of 5 for both, OCF 0.18% and 0.29% respectively. [P8] lists identical investments at 50% each with risk level 5 as the agreed rating. [P16] states 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P2] and [P3] show the CT Universal MAP fund is classified in KIID category 4 (medium volatility), not category 5. [P12] states HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio has 'risk profile is rated as 3' on its own stated risk scale of 1-5. The KIID in [P2] states the fund asset allocation under typical market conditions is 'Equities: 45-70%, Bonds: 20-55%, Other: 0-25%'.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts both recommended funds carry a risk rating of 5. However, [P12] explicitly states the HSBC fund 'risk profile is rated as 3 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk.' This is a contradiction between the report's assertion (risk 5) and the fund factsheet (risk 3).

      - Report asserts CT Universal MAP Balanced C carries risk rating 5. However, [P2] KIID states 'This Fund is classified in category 4 because its price or simulated data has shown medium fluctuations historically.' The KIID Risk and Reward Indicator places it at position 4, not 5.

      - The report's strategy risk rating of 5 is stated to match the client's agreed risk level of 5. However, the two constituent funds are individually rated 3 (HSBC per [P12]) and 4 (CT per [P2]), neither of which is 5. A blend of funds rated 3 and 4 does not inherently produce a combined rating of 5 without explicit evidence of a stated combined rating on the firm's scale.

    The fundamental issue is a mismatch between the risk ratings asserted for the individual funds in the report and the risk ratings stated in the fund documents themselves. The report states both funds are risk-rated 5 to match the client's agreed risk level. However, [P12] fund factsheet unambiguously states the HSBC fund's risk profile is 3 on its stated 1-5 scale. [P2] KIID for CT Universal MAP places it at category 4 on the Risk and Reward Indicator. The guard notes that fund-level risk ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale, and a blend can target a rating neither fund carries individually if there is a stated combined rating. However, no evidence in the pack states a combined portfolio risk rating of 5 that is derived from blending a 3 and a 4. [P16] confirms the portfolio's 'risk mapping is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level,' but this appears to be an assertion by the adviser rather than corroboration from independent fund documentation. The individual fund documents contradict the individual risk ratings asserted in the report. This is a material inconsistency: the report claims both funds are individually rated 5, the documents show they are rated 3 and 4 respectively. The allocation arithmetic is correct (50% + 50% = 100%), and the recommended funds are confirmed in [P8] and [P11]. The agreed client risk level of 5 is consistently evidenced across [P10], [P8], [P16], and [P22]. However, the suitability depends on whether the blended strategy actually delivers risk level 5, which cannot be verified from the contradictory individual fund ratings in the factsheets and KIIDs.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Questionnaire indicated risk rating 4 (Cautious Balanced). Adviser assessment: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite.' Agreed rating: 5 (Balanced). Client acknowledgement: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and 'you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.' Further confirmation: 'You have confirmed that you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period and feel a balanced approach to this investment would achieve this goal.'

    File:     Risk profile report (P2, P6, P20) confirms: natural risk level from questionnaire was 4 (Cautious Balanced); following discussion considering natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return, an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) was reached. File notes (P11) contain conflicting language: 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6.' This states the client is 'happy to proceed with' risk rating 6, contradicting the report's assertion of rating 5. Suitability Report (P4) reiterates the report's own language verbatim. No separate file record documents the adviser's reasoning that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' beyond what appears in the report itself.

    Discrepancies:

      - File notes (P11) state client agreed to 'risk rating 5' but also state 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6', creating internal inconsistency about the final agreed rating.

      - Report asserts justification that questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' but no independent file record (such as meeting notes, adviser assessment, or capacity for loss analysis) corroborates the adviser's reasoning for this assessment.

      - Risk profile report (P2, P6, P20) records that the discussion considered natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return, but does not explicitly document the adviser's specific concern that the questionnaire result did not reflect risk appetite.

    The report asserts an upward override from rating 4 to rating 5 with justification that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite.' This justification is stated only in the report itself; it does not appear as a standalone assessment or adviser note in the supporting file. The Risk profile report (P2, P6, P20) confirms that ratings 4 and 5 were discussed and an agreed risk level of 5 was reached, and that the discussion considered 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return,' but it does not independently record or explain why the adviser believed the questionnaire was inadequate. The client acknowledgement is well-documented in both the report and supporting passages. However, file notes (P11) introduce a material discrepancy by stating the agreed rating is '6' rather than '5', which contradicts the report's assertion and the risk profile documentation. The evidence confirms discussion occurred and ratings 4 and 5 are referenced, but does not independently corroborate the adviser's specific reasoning that the questionnaire was misaligned with the client's appetite. The missing input is an adviser assessment or file record explicitly explaining why the questionnaire result (rating 4) was deemed insufficient, independent of the report's own assertion.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report recommends a portfolio of two funds with agreed risk rating 5 (Balanced): HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). The report states the client has agreed attitude to risk of 5 on a 1-10 scale, medium capacity for loss, and a 10-year investment term. The report asserts the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and are 'risk-profiled solutions, meaning they are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk'. The rationale links the portfolio to the client's financial goals and risk profile.

    File:     Evidence shows the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced) per the SIPP research report [P6] and [P12], and the client's natural risk level from questionnaire was 4, upgraded to 5 after adviser assessment [P6]. The HSBC fund is described in its KIID [P9] as 'risk profile' rated 3 on a 1-5 scale (where 1 is lower risk, 5 is higher risk), meaning it maps to risk level 3, not 5. The fund's risk and reward profile [P1] shows it ranked 4 on a 1-7 SRRI scale. The CT Universal MAP Balanced fund is listed in the research report shortlist [P10] as a risk level 5 investment matched to the agreed risk level. Combined performance data [P4] shows both funds' discrete annual returns and volatility. The HSBC fund factsheet [P8] shows 3-year volatility of 7.12% vs peer group average 6.67%, with the fund positioned in the moderate allocation category. The investment term is 10 years [P6] per the risk profile report. Capacity for loss is stated as medium [P6] and [P12], based on the client's strong financial position with combined household income exceeding expenditure, £6,000 savings, and no liabilities.

    Discrepancies:

      - The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio is described in its own KIID [P9] as 'rated as 3' on the fund's internal 1-5 risk scale, but the report recommends it with a stated risk rating of 5, creating a mismatch between the fund's own risk classification (3) and the risk rating assigned to it in the recommendation (5)

      - The report states both recommended funds have individual risk rating 5, but the HSBC fund's KIID [P9] establishes its own risk profile as 3 on its proprietary scale, and the SRRI indicator [P1] shows rank 4 on the 1-7 standardised scale

    A guard applies: 'A fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's; use it as corroboration, never as a value match.' The HSBC fund's own KIID [P9] states it is 'rated as 3' within HSBC's range of five different risk levels (where 1 is lower and 5 is higher), but this is the fund manager's internal classification for that fund, separate from the client's agreed risk rating of 5. The client's agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) is supported by evidence [P6], [P12]: the natural risk questionnaire result was 4, which was discussed and upgraded to agreed level 5. The CT Universal MAP Balanced fund appears in the shortlist [P10] and is confirmed in the investment allocation [P12] at 50% of the portfolio. The HSBC fund is also confirmed [P4], [P12] at 50%. Both funds are listed as part of the agreed risk level 5 portfolio in [P12]. The report's claim that the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' refers to the client's agreed risk rating of 5, not the funds' internal risk classifications. The portfolio (50/50 HSBC/CT) is evidenced in [P12]. The investment term of 10 years is stated in [P6]. Capacity for loss is assessed as medium and supported by the income and asset detail in the capacity for loss section. The guard explaining that marketing names ('Balanced') are not risk ratings applies here: the fund name and the fund's own risk classification (3 on its scale) do not contradict the client's agreed risk profile (5 on the profiler's scale). The alignment claim—that the funds are designed to remain within the client's agreed risk level—relies on the portfolio construction process evidenced in [P6], where each risk level is mapped to asset class breakdowns. The report's assertion that both funds have risk rating 5 must be understood as their designation within the agreed risk level portfolio, not as an independent fund property. No evidence contradicts that the portfolio as a whole is suitable for a client with agreed risk rating 5, 10-year term, and medium capacity for loss.

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts: (1) Attitude to Risk questionnaire indicated rating 4 ('Cautious Balanced'); adviser felt this didn't fully reflect client's risk appetite; agreed rating 5 ('Balanced'). Client not reliant on pension funds, wishes best growth potential over 10-year period, feels balanced approach achieves this goal. (2) Capacity for loss rated 'Medium', based on: State Pension ~£230/week; employment income ~£300/week net as HGV driver plus £20/week from Sea Cadets; spouse Kim contributes £1,100/month and entitled to State Pension ~£230/week at SPA; combined income comfortably meets ~£1,700/month household expenditure; savings ~£6,000, no liabilities; potential underperformance would not detrimental impact day-to-day living. CFL consistent with agreed ATR level 5. (3) Investment term: 10 years. (4) Recommended strategy: Risk 5 Balanced portfolio, two funds each 50% (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc risk rating 5, 0.18% charge; CT Universal MAP Balanced C risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). Funds reflect client's risk profile individually and combined; portfolio drift maintains same risk; diversified across asset classes; blend of active and passive; simple; competitive charges; good performance; part of CIP with quarterly review. Strategy aligns with financial goals and risk profile. All statements claimed as accurate and consistent.

    File:     Evidence retrieved shows: (1) Risk Profile Report [P1, P8] documents natural risk level 4 from ATR questionnaire ('Cautious Balanced') dated 18/09/2025. Agreed risk level 5 ('Balanced') reached following interactive discussion considering 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return'. [P8]: 'As a Balanced investor you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person...' (2) Risk Profile Report [P4] notes: 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section and 'Agreed risk level' section. Investment target section [P3] also shows 'No additional notes were recorded'. (3) ATR Questionnaire responses [P16] show client selected 'Cautious' as investment approach, expressed discomfort with >£4,000 loss on £20,000 investment, associates risk with 'Caution', 'More cautious' vs peers, willing to accept 'slight risk of capital loss'. Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses [P15] show income details and assess liquid savings. (4) Suitability Report [P17] states client 'aware that investment values can rise as well as fall', 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living', receives ~£230/week State Pension, earns ~£300/week net as HGV driver plus £20/week Sea Cadets, spouse contributes £1,100/month, combined income meets ~£1,700/month expenditure. (5) File notes [P7] document existing fund risk ratings: Aviva current risk 2, Zurich current risk 7, Standard Life current risk 4, People's Pension 'alternative option', Scottish Widows 'alternative option'. (6) SIPP Research Report [P5] confirms agreed risk level 5, describes Balanced investor profile, recommends CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), both described as risk level 5. [P5] shows MiFID II cost disclosures. (7) Suitability Report [P18] confirms fund recommendation: HSBC 50% risk rating 5 with 0.18% charge, CT Universal MAP 50% risk rating 5 with 0.29% charge. States funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined', provide diversification, blend active/passive, meet simplicity preference, have competitive charges and good performance, part of CIP with quarterly review.
