(9) Capacity for loss assessment basis appears in report [P17] but Risk Profile Report sections [P4] show 'No additional notes were recorded' for Capacity for Loss questionnaire discussion and Agreed risk level justification.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states client's investment term is 10 years ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'); Risk Profile Report [P8] shows 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the projections used for risk level justification, creating a 8-year discrepancy in stated investment horizon.

      - Report asserts CFL assessment rationale spans State Pension, employment income, spouse income, surplus income, liquid assets, and no detriment statement, all attributed to capacity for loss discussion section [P4]; but Risk Profile Report [P4] states 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section, suggesting no documented discussion notes beyond questionnaire responses.

      - Report states 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire' and client 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5'; the extraction metadata flags ambiguity: unclear whether final agreed rating is 4 or 5, whether adviser's assessment moved it to 5 or client chose to increase, and extraction identifies candidate readings 'Risk rating 4 per questionnaire' vs 'Risk rating 5 agreed and confirmed'.

    The report asserts a 10-year investment term while Risk Profile Report [P8] shows the projections table used to justify the agreed risk level models a 2-year investment period. This is a material discrepancy: investment term is a core component of suitability assessment and capacity for loss calculation. The discrepancy is not explained by a temporal guard (e.g., client changed horizon between assessments) because both documents are dated within the same file. The extraction metadata itself acknowledges this as an ambiguity at path '/replacementAnalysis', noting the internalInconsistency in stated investment periods.

   

    The report's capacity for loss assessment basis—State Pension £230/week, HGV driver income £300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week, spouse £1,100/month, expenditure £1,700/month, savings £6,000, no liabilities, no detriment to day-to-day living—is cited verbatim from the report and corroborated in passages [P17] (Suitability Report) and [P15] (Questionnaire responses). However, Risk Profile Report [P4] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' under the Capacity for Loss questionnaire section and under Agreed risk level, which suggests the detailed discussion documented in the suitability report was not recorded in the source risk profile document. This is not a contradiction of the facts stated (income figures, savings, expenditure are consistent across documents) but rather a documentation gap: the justification exists in the report but the risk profile document shows no notes recorded for discussion of capacity for loss or agreed risk level. The extraction identified this as an ambiguity: capacity for loss described as 'medium' but justification refers to strong financial position.

   

    On attitude to risk override: The report asserts questionnaire indicated rating 4, adviser felt this didn't reflect appetite, agreed rating 5, client confirmed wish to increase to 5 and was happy to do so. This is corroborated by [P1, P8] (Risk Profile Report showing natural risk 4, agreed risk 5, and methodology for reaching agreed level through discussion) and [P17] (Suitability Report repeating the same). The override direction and justification ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') is stated and acknowledged by client ('wish to increase your Risk rating to 5', 'aware this is higher', 'happy to take on this risk'). Extraction metadata flagged ambiguity on whether final rating is 4 or 5, but passages and report are consistent: agreed rating is 5. No contradiction stands.

   

    On recommended strategy and risk alignment: Report asserts both funds carry risk rating 5, portfolio designed to remain within risk level 5. [P5] (SIPP Research Report) and [P18] (Suitability Report) confirm both HSBC and CT Universal MAP are risk-profiled solutions rated level 5. No contradiction on fund risk ratings or strategy-to-risk alignment.

   

    On tolerance for loss: Report includes statements that client is 'aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' and 'higher risk...potential there is for larger drops in value'. These are corroborated by [P17]. No quantified maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is asserted or required; extraction shows 'maximumAcceptableLossPercentage: null' and 'maximumAcceptableLossAmount: null'. Severity guidance notes 'Low: tolerance for loss not quantified where no large-loss scenario is modelled', but this is guidance for cases where such quantification is missing and material; no assertion of a specific tolerance threshold was made to contradict.

   

    The one material finding is the investment term discrepancy: 10 years vs 2 years. This directly affects the suitability assessment because investment horizon is a core input to risk capacity and strategy selection. The discrepancy stands and is unsupported/unexplained across documents.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). Report states or implies 'Moderate' knowledge level; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' to direct question 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' (P10). Report describes suitability for Balanced investing based on 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P15); questionnaire records zero experience in stocks, shares, unit trusts, or pension products, with responses limited to cash savings only (P1). Report's characterization of client as comfortable with moderate knowledge contradicts questionnaire's recorded assessment of no understanding on stocks and shares (P1), unit trusts (P1), and pension planning (P1). 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts the client has 'Moderate' knowledge and experience. The suitability report states: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing' and describes the client as suitable for a Balanced (risk level 5) approach, implying moderate investment understanding.

    File:     The risk profile questionnaire responses (P1, P10) record the client's self-assessed understanding as follows: 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself? No understanding / knowledge' (P10). On specific product knowledge: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge' (P1). On recent investment experience: 'Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years? No' (P1). The only area where experience is recorded is cash savings: 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years? Yes, by myself' (P1).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states or implies 'Moderate' knowledge level; questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' to direct question 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' (P10)

      - Report describes suitability for Balanced investing based on 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P15); questionnaire records zero experience in stocks, shares, unit trusts, or pension products, with responses limited to cash savings only (P1)

      - Report's characterization of client as comfortable with moderate knowledge contradicts questionnaire's recorded assessment of no understanding on stocks and shares (P1), unit trusts (P1), and pension planning (P1)

    The report asserts a knowledge level of 'Moderate' and uses language consistent with moderate investment understanding ('some experience', 'degree of understanding of financial matters'). However, the questionnaire responses form a direct, objective assessment completed by the client on 18/09/2025 (same date as the advice meeting per P1 and P10). The questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' as the client's answer to the explicit question 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' This is not self-deprecation but a recorded response to a standard questionnaire item. Furthermore, the detailed product-specific questions show no knowledge of stocks and shares, unit trusts, or pension planning — only cash savings experience. The only investments in the last 5 years were cash deposits, checked 'Yes, by myself' (P1). The report's narrative describes the client as suitable for a Balanced (risk 5) approach based on having 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P15), but the questionnaire evidence contradicts this entirely. The guard notes that 'self-assessed comfort is not the same as assessed knowledge', and that 'No understanding / knowledge' recorded against a direct question is an assessed answer, not a casual remark. The report's assertion of 'Moderate' knowledge is not supported by the evidence and is contradicted by the questionnaire record of 'No understanding / knowledge' across investment categories. The risk profile report (P5) does note the client's natural risk level as '4 Cautious Balanced' based on the questionnaire, then records agreement to risk level 5, but this does not alter the fact-find assessment of knowledge, which remains 'No understanding / knowledge' on the questionnaire. The suitability report's description of the client as having moderate knowledge and being suited to a Balanced approach on the basis of investment understanding is not corroborated and is contradicted by the contemporaneous questionnaire evidence.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report states client has: (1) previously taken out cash savings products; (2) NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by himself; (3) held group pensions for some time. No years of experience quantified for any product type.

    File:     Risk profile questionnaire [P1] shows: (1) 'Yes, by myself' for Current Accounts/Savings/Deposit Accounts/Cash ISA within last 5 years—corroborating cash savings experience; (2) 'No' for stocks and shares within last 5 years—corroborating no direct stocks & shares experience; (3) 'No' for pension planning and income in retirement products within last 5 years—contradicting report claim of holding group pensions 'for some time'. Fact Find [P5], [P15], [P17] lists five existing pension arrangements (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) with no dates of acquisition documented. Questionnaire [P1] records 'No understanding / knowledge' for 'stocks and shares', 'Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts', and critically 'pension planning and income in retirement'. Client confirmed checking portfolio valuation 'Yes, by myself' annually.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts client 'have held your group pensions for some time' but questionnaire [P1] response to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' is 'No', indicating no recent pension investment activity recorded in risk assessment

      - Report states client has 'previously taken out cash savings products' supported by questionnaire 'Yes, by myself' for savings accounts, but does not quantify years of experience

      - Report does not cite or reference the questionnaire finding that client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement, despite recommending pension consolidation and transfer

      - Client's understanding level stated as 'no understanding' for pension planning in questionnaire [P1] but report does not acknowledge this material gap when recommending complex pension restructuring

    The report's assertion that the client 'have held your group pensions for some time' is directly contradicted by the risk profile questionnaire [P1], which records a response of 'No' to the question 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?'. This is not a matter of passive workplace pension experience versus active self-directed investing—the questionnaire specifically asks about pension investment activity, and the client's answer was negative. The Fact Find documents five existing pension arrangements but provides no dates of commencement, so the file cannot corroborate the 'for some time' narrative. More critically, the questionnaire [P1] explicitly records that the client has 'No understanding / knowledge' of 'pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?'. This material knowledge gap is not acknowledged in the report's narrative on Knowledge & Experience. The cash savings experience is supported: questionnaire response 'Yes, by myself' for Current Accounts and Savings within last 5 years aligns with report claim. The stocks & shares statement is also supported: questionnaire 'No' to stocks & shares within last 5 years matches report's assertion that client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself'. The core discrepancy is the pension experience claim combined with the undisclosed finding of no understanding of pension planning. This affects the suitability assessment because the report recommends consolidating five separate pensions into a single Aviva Platform arrangement with a balanced fund portfolio (50% HSBC Global Strategy, 50% CT Universal MAP)—a complex restructuring that the evidence shows the client does not understand.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that the Aviva Pension Portfolio Personal Pension with Flexi Access Drawdown is 'Complex' and identifies five complexity drivers: 'Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options', 'Multiple fund choices within platform', 'Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required', 'Death benefit options and nominations', and 'Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027'. The report states the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension'. The recommended solution transfers £110,185 into this product with two balanced funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50% and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund 50%).

    File:     The file records the client's knowledge and experience at page 3 as follows: the client is an HGV driver earning £300 per week net (page 3, described as 'typically earning'), has 'limited investment knowledge' (page 3), and the risk profile report (page 35 [P35]) classifies the client as 'Cautious Balanced' investor who 'tend[s] to see yourself as quite a cautious person' with 'limited or moderate experience of investments'. The file notes (page 38 [P38]) indicate the adviser considered but discounted alternative providers (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) because their available funds did not match the agreed risk level or had higher charges. The suitability report itself (page 31 [P31]) explains the pension product in detail and pages 9 and 19 describe flexi-access drawdown mechanics. Product literature (pages 1, 20 [P1], [P20]) sets out retirement benefit choices and drawdown options. The file contains fund factsheets (pages 8, 9, 26, 28, 29 [P8], [P9], [P26], [P28], [P29]) and KIIDs (pages 21, 23, 24, 25, 26 [P21], [P23], [P24], [P25], [P26]) for both recommended funds.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms' but the file records 'limited investment knowledge' on page 3, creating tension between stated understanding and recorded knowledge level.

      - The report classifies the recommended product as 'Complex' with five drivers, but the client is recorded as a 'Cautious Balanced' investor with 'limited or moderate experience of investments' (page 35 [P35]). No evidence in the file records the client as having received prior detailed explanation of pension freedoms, drawdown mechanics, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies, or IHT planning consequences before the recommendation was made.

      - The report provides no record of the client asking questions about or demonstrating comprehension of the complex features (pension freedoms, multiple fund choices, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies, death benefit nominations, IHT treatment from April 2027) beyond stating the client 'prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown'.

    The comparison can be made. The report recommends a product explicitly labelled 'Complex' by the report itself, citing five distinct complexity drivers: pension freedoms, multiple fund choices, tax-efficient withdrawal strategies, death benefit options, and IHT planning. The suitability report text (page 31 [P31]) confirms the client will access 'Flexi Access Drawdown' and states 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension, allowing you control over how and when benefits are taken.' However, the file records the client's knowledge as 'limited' (page 3) and the risk profile report (page 35 [P35]) classifies the client as 'Cautious Balanced' with 'limited or moderate experience of investments'. The stated income is £300 per week net as an HGV driver, with expenditure comfortably covered by income and modest savings. There is no evidence in the file of: (1) a detailed discussion with the client about pension freedoms mechanics; (2) the client demonstrating understanding of flexible drawdown, tax consequences at different ages, or IHT treatment changes; (3) questions from the client about these features; or (4) the adviser documenting the client's comprehension of any unusual features. The Key Features document (pages 1, 20 [P1], [P20]) and product literature set out these features in standard form, but there is no evidence the client engaged with or understood them before advice was given. The discrepancy between 'limited investment knowledge' recorded in the fact find and the assertion that the client 'understands the pension freedoms' is material. The report provides the standard product explanation but does not corroborate that a client with limited knowledge comprehended the complexity of the recommended product before accepting the advice. Under COBS 9.2.1R-9.2.2R and 9A.2.1R-9A.2.2R, the adviser must have regard to the client's knowledge and experience and ensure recommendations are suitable. A 'Complex' product with five material drivers (including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies and IHT planning) recommended to a client recorded as having 'limited investment knowledge' requires documentary evidence of comprehension. The file does not provide such evidence.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the client's knowledge level is 'Moderate' and recommends a 'Complex' product. The suitability report asserts (P5): 'In addition to your attitude to investment risk and capacity for loss, I have also considered your investment objectives as well as your knowledge and experience, and the length of time you wish to invest for.' The report describes the recommended funds as multi-asset funds providing 'a professionally managed, diversified investment solution' and states 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple.' The report also states (P7): 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.'

    File:     The fact find and risk profile record contradictory evidence of the client's actual investment knowledge. The questionnaire responses (P9) show: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them? No understanding / knowledge'; 'Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts within the last 5 years? No'; 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years? No'. The only affirmative investment response is 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years? Yes, by myself'. The risk profile document notes (P10): 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience section, and 'No additional notes were recorded' under Capacity For Loss questionnaire notes. The report itself states (P7): 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5' and notes client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements.'

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states knowledge level is 'Moderate' but questionnaire responses record 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, their risks, pension planning, and income in retirement risks

      - Report states client has 'some experience in investments' (P7, reproducing description requiring such experience) but file shows no investment experience beyond basic savings accounts and workplace pension

      - Report asserts 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' but file explicitly records no understanding of stocks and shares or pension planning

      - Report describes investment as keeping strategy 'fairly simple' yet recommends two actively managed multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced) with layered complexity via 'CIP' approach, contradicting simplicity claim

      - Report states client awareness 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' (P7) yet ascribes 'Moderate' knowledge level to justify recommending Complex product

    The core issue is a material contradiction between the report's stated knowledge level ('Moderate') and the file's recorded knowledge. The questionnaire data (P9) demonstrates no understanding of equity investments, their risks, or pension planning. The client's only investment experience is basic savings products. The report acknowledges this limitation ('experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements') but then assigns a 'Moderate' knowledge classification to justify recommending a 'Complex' product. The precondition for the description quoted in P7 ('If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach') is explicitly not met: the file records no such experience or understanding. The guard applies here: the risk-profile description carries preconditions about experience that the file contradicts, and the report is using this description as evidence of knowledge it does not establish. The report's own assertion that the investment keeps the strategy 'fairly simple' is contradicted by the recommended structure: two actively managed funds (HSBC at 0.18% and CT Universal MAP at 0.29%) plus platform and adviser overlay, which the report itself describes as a layered, actively monitored 'CIP' approach requiring ongoing committee oversight (P8). This is not a simple solution for a client with no equity experience. The vulnerability overlay is relevant: the client is an HGV driver with limited income (£300/week net plus £20/week from Sea Cadets role), receiving State Pension of £230/week. Combined with spouse income of £1,100/month, the household is modestly positioned. The client's lack of financial knowledge, combined with modest income resources, creates a vulnerability that heightens the suitability concern when a Complex product is recommended without clear evidence the client comprehends its constituent parts or their interactions.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The client states they are 'comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards'. The report recommends a complex investment solution comprising a Carefully Curated Portfolio (CIP) with multiple funds across different asset classes, regions and sectors, described as actively monitored by an investment committee. The client is recorded as a Balanced investor (Risk Rating 5) with limited investment experience confined to workplace pension arrangements. The report emphasises the CIP's complexity relative to alternative investment pathways but provides no documented evidence of client questions about product features, structure or risk mechanisms.

    File:     File notes record that the client's experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and that they 'understand that returns are not guaranteed'. The Risk Profile Report contains a section titled 'Investor Experience' with a note stating 'No additional notes were recorded'. The Fact Find completed 19/09/2025 contains no documented questions from the client about investment complexity, product structure, fund composition or risk management approach. The file notes confirm the client agreed to Risk Rating 5 and understood that 'your funds may go up as well as down'. No evidence appears of any discussion of the CIP approach, its active oversight mechanism, or how it differs from simpler alternatives beyond the brief mention that investment pathways are 'discounted' in the suitability report.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts client is 'comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' but file records experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' — comfort level and sophistication implied by 'reasonable understanding' appear overstated relative to documented limited experience

      - Report recommends a complex multi-fund CIP structure with active investment committee oversight but Risk Profile Report section 'Investor Experience' shows 'No additional notes were recorded', indicating no documented discussion of client comprehension of this complexity

      - Report states investment pathways were 'discounted' because client 'require[s] a more tailored solution' and 'a more robust and flexible framework' but provides no evidence the client understood or questioned why the CIP's complexity was necessary versus simpler options

      - File notes record client questions and explanations on risk rating and capacity for loss but contain no documented client questions regarding product features, fund selection, CIP structure, or investment mechanism

    The client's stated comfort with investments and 'reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' appears in the suitability report at [P1] and is supported by the client's self-assessment in the report narrative. However, the file evidence shows the client's actual documented experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements, with no record of prior investment activity or questions demonstrating sophisticated understanding. The risk profile document [P14] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience, meaning no discussion of investment knowledge was documented beyond the self-assessment. The recommended product is materially complex: it comprises a carefully curated multi-fund portfolio with active oversight by an investment committee, described in the report as offering 'enhanced oversight, risk management, and adaptability' compared to static multi-asset funds. This is substantially more complex than the investment pathway alternatives the adviser rejected. Per the guard 'An absence of recorded questions is not proof the client did not understand', an evidence gap exists but the gap is material here: the product's complexity and the client's limited documented experience are at odds, and the file contains no questioning or comprehension evidence to bridge the gap. The report's description of the client as having 'reasonable understanding' and being comfortable with investments, while the file records experience limited to workplace pensions and no documented discussion of CIP complexity, represents a meaningful discrepancy. The severity is elevated because the product complexity is substantial (multi-fund CIP with active governance oversight) and the documented client experience baseline is limited (workplace pensions only), with no recorded evidence of client comprehension of the specific complex features being recommended.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report states the client has: (1) previously taken out cash savings products (confirmed); (2) NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by themselves; (3) held group pensions for some time. The existing arrangements show five pension plans with varying fund holdings: People's Pension (Global Investments up to 85% shares, risk rating 9); Scottish Widows (Portfolio Four CS1 & Portfolio Three CS1, risk rating 4); Aviva (My Future Focus Consolidation, risk rating 2, lifestyled); Zurich (Managed AP, risk rating 7); Standard Life (At Retirement Universal 10-year, risk rating 4, lifestyled).

    File:     The case file evidences: (1) Five existing pension arrangements with confirmed values and fund holdings as at September-October 2025: People's Pension £6,601.55 in Global Investments (up to 85% shares); Scottish Widows £211.32 in mixed portfolios; Aviva £3,744.36 in My Future Focus Consolidation (lifestyled); Zurich £103,439.24 in Managed AP; Standard Life £3,002 in At Retirement Universal (lifestyled). (2) Holdings span risk ratings from 2 (lifestyled, conservative) to 9 (aggressive equity). (3) No evidence in the file of direct stocks & shares ISA, unit trust, or investment trust holdings outside pension wrappers. (4) Two of the five arrangements are documented as lifestyled (auto-switching), indicating passive rather than active investment decision-making.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' but the existing holdings include People's Pension with Global Investments (up to 85% shares, risk rating 9) and Zurich with Managed AP (risk rating 7), both equity-heavy portfolios. These represent direct equity market exposure within pension wrappers, which contradicts the claim of no stocks & shares investment experience.

      - The report describes the client as having 'held group pensions for some time' but the file shows the client holds four personal pensions (Aviva, Scottish Widows, Zurich, Standard Life plan numbers P10026-719-DL and D2301334000) and only one occupational pension (People's Pension). Three of the four personal pensions (Aviva started 2017-11-01, Zurich started 2013-05-01, Standard Life started 1991-02-01 per P21) have lengthy histories, but the assertion of group pension experience is not clearly corroborated as primary experience.

      - Two of the five existing arrangements (Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation and Standard Life At Retirement Universal) are documented as having lifestyling/auto-switching investment approaches (P2 states 'auto-switching investment approach' and 'Lifestage Investment Approach'; P14 names 'SL At Retirement Universal (PP10 Year)Pension' as the invested fund). Lifestyled arrangements indicate the investments were not selected by the client but are automatically managed by the provider, which raises a question about whether these holdings evidence the client's own investment decision-making or experience.

    The report's assertion that the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' is directly contradicted by the existing holdings. The client's People's Pension and Zurich pensions both hold equity-based funds (Global Investments up to 85% shares and Managed AP respectively), which constitute stocks & shares investments. These holdings are not merely cash savings; they are recorded as existing arrangements with named equity funds. The extraction summary notes an internal inconsistency regarding whether the client's risk rating was upgraded based on adviser assessment or client choice, but the existence of equity holdings is not ambiguous. Under the guard that 'holding a product is not the same as understanding it', holdings corroborate experience but do not prove knowledge; however, the report's explicit denial of stocks & shares investment experience contradicts the objective fact of such holdings in the file. The client's experience with group pensions is supported by the People's Pension (occupational), but the report's characterisation is imprecise given that the majority of holdings are personal pensions. Two of the five arrangements are auto-switching/lifestyled, which means they were not actively selected by the client, though this does not invalidate that the client has held such products. The core discrepancy is the report's negation of stocks & shares investment experience when the file records active holdings in equity funds.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Report asserts that recommended products are Complex, driven by: (1) Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options; (2) Multiple fund choices within platform; (3) Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies required; (4) Death benefit options and nominations; (5) Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027. Report provides rationale across 10 topics: EmployerContributions, SmallPotsEncashment, LowerCosts, LifestylingRisk, GreaterInvestmentChoice, AdviserChargingFacilitation, OngoingMonitoring, MultiAssetFundAccess, EnhancedRetirementFlexibility, and FlexibilityAndChoiceInRetirement. Rationale addresses consolidation objectives, fund switching justifications, and platform selection benefits.

    File:     File evidence records client as risk rating 5 (Balanced investor). File notes on ATR state: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' [P8]. Investor Experience section in risk profile notes 'No additional notes were recorded' [P14]. Capacity for loss notes record client awareness that 'investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed' [P8]. File records natural risk level as 4, selected ATR as 5 [P12]. Fact Find completed 19/09/2025 with ATR/CFL fully completed with detailed notes [P12]. Risk profile report Investor Experience section shows no recorded discussion notes [P14]. File evidence does not record any discussion of pension freedoms, drawdown options, death benefit nominations, or IHT planning considerations from April 2027.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report identifies complexity drivers including 'Pension freedoms and flexible access drawdown options', 'Death benefit options and nominations', and 'Inheritance Tax planning considerations from April 2027' but file evidence contains no recorded discussion of these topics in Investor Experience section [P14] which shows 'No additional notes were recorded'

      - Report describes client as requiring 'Tax-efficient withdrawal strategies' but file records client experience as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' [P8], suggesting no prior experience with complex withdrawal strategy planning

      - Report rationale emphasizes 'Multiple fund choices within platform' and 'Greater Investment Choice' but file notes record client objective as simply 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' [P12], indicating preference for simplification rather than expanded choice

      - File records natural ATR as 4 (Cautious Balanced) with selected ATR as 5 (Balanced) [P12], but risk profile report Investor Experience notes show no recorded discussion explaining why client can handle complex product features despite limited investment experience [P14]

    The report asserts complexity and provides rationale, satisfying the structural requirement for a complex product recommendation. However, material gaps exist between the rationale offered and the file evidence of client knowledge and experience. The report identifies five complexity drivers, three of which (pension freedoms/drawdown, death benefit nominations, IHT planning from April 2027) are significant pension-specific features. The file records the client's experience as limited—explicitly stated as 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' [P8]—yet contains no recorded discussion of the client's understanding of these complex features. The Investor Experience section of the risk profile report contains no notes whatsoever [P14], despite the fact find recording ATR/CFL as 'Fully completed with detailed notes' [P12]. This is a material inconsistency: if the client's knowledge was assessed and found adequate to support a complex product recommendation addressing drawdown options, death benefits, and IHT planning, that assessment should be documented in the risk profile. The client's stated objective was consolidation and simplification ('get his head around what he has') [P12], not expanded choice. While the report's rationale includes 'Greater Investment Choice' as a benefit, this appears to conflict with the recorded client objective. The discrepancy is not explained by any guard: the comparison can be made; both the report assertion and file evidence are present; and there is no temporal or contextual reason why a rationale addressing complex features should exist in the report but no corresponding record of client comprehension or agreement should exist in the file. The severity is High because the report's description of client knowledge (implicitly capable of handling complex withdrawal, death benefit, and IHT features) contradicts the fact file record of experience limited to workplace pensions, with no documented discussion bridging that gap.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report states expenditure 'approximately £1,700 per month'; Fact Find shows exactly £1,718.00 (1.1% difference). Report asserts 'combined income comfortably meets' expenditure; Fact Find shows net monthly income of £1,430.00 against expenditure of £1,718.00, yielding a deficit of -£288.00 monthly, not surplus. Report states pension funds 'totalling approximately £106,000'; five arrangements sum to £116,998.47; discrepancy of £10,998.47 (10.4%). 26 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Initial commitment: £3,305.55 (3% advice fee on £110,185 transfer value). Ongoing commitment: £1,101.85 per annum (1% on current values and expected contributions, minus initial fee, for first year). Affordability basis: 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, you hold savings of around £6,000, and have no outstanding liabilities. You also hold pension funds totalling approximately £106,000.' Client confirmed underperformance would not detract from day-to-day living.

    File:     Fact Find (P1): Total net monthly income £1,430.00; total monthly expenditure £1,718.00; resulting monthly disposable income is -£288.00 (a deficit, not surplus). Savings held: £6,000 (corroborates report assertion). Intelliflo planning scenarios (P2, P5, P8): Income sources include employment to 2026–2027 (CDH Recruitment £15,600 p/a, Sea Cadets £1,040 p/a, Kim's income £13,200 p/a). State pension £11,973 from age 66. Cashflow projections show positive in-year surplus/deficit of £0 throughout retirement years (P2, P9, P11). Existing pension funds total £116,998.47 (sum of five arrangements), not £106,000. Report states transfer value is £110,185; calculated from report figures yields £116,998.47.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states expenditure 'approximately £1,700 per month'; Fact Find shows exactly £1,718.00 (1.1% difference)

      - Report asserts 'combined income comfortably meets' expenditure; Fact Find shows net monthly income of £1,430.00 against expenditure of £1,718.00, yielding a deficit of -£288.00 monthly, not surplus

      - Report states pension funds 'totalling approximately £106,000'; five arrangements sum to £116,998.47; discrepancy of £10,998.47 (10.4%)

      - Report states transfer value £110,185; sum of five stated transfer values in canonical model is £116,998.47; unexplained shortfall of £6,813.47

      - Report grounds affordability in income meeting expenditure; Fact Find shows persistent monthly deficit of £288, not surplus coverage

      - Ongoing commitment £1,101.85 stated as 'based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee'; figure depends on post-transfer fund value, which is uncertain given transfer value discrepancy

    The core affordability assertion rests on two claims: (1) income comfortably covers expenditure, and (2) the household holds adequate reserves. The evidence directly contradicts the first claim. The Fact Find (P1) is dated contemporaneously and documents the client's stated income (£1,430 net monthly) against stated expenditure (£1,718 monthly), yielding a calculated deficit of -£288 per month. The report's language—'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure'—asserts a surplus condition that the Fact Find does not support. The report rounds expenditure to 'approximately £1,700' (1.1% below actual); the rounding itself does not explain away a £288 monthly shortfall. The Intelliflo scenarios (P2, P5) model retirement cashflow and show in-year surplus/deficit of £0 in most years after state pension and employment income cease, but these projections assume the pension transfer proceeds and funds are deployed. They do not validate the claimed affordability at the point of the recommendation when employment income is still in receipt yet insufficient to cover outgoings. The report's second basis—that 'you hold savings of around £6,000'—is corroborated by the Fact Find. However, savings of £6,000 provide a buffer for a single month's deficit and are inadequate to sustain a £288 monthly shortfall over time, particularly before pension access becomes available. The pension fund values are stated inconsistently: report claims 'approximately £106,000'; the five existing arrangements shown in the canonical model sum to £116,998.47. The report also cites a transfer value of £110,185, but the sum of five transfer values in the data is £116,998.47, a £6,813.47 discrepancy not explained in any document. The ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 is quoted from the report itself and is linked to 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee.' The fee figure depends on the actual transfer value received, which is uncertain given the documented shortfall. The recommendation document (P7, P22) contains the quoted fees and the affordability statement but does not explain how a household with a documented monthly deficit can afford an initial £3,305.55 fee (from transferred pension) and an ongoing 1% per annum charge. The initial fee is stated to be 'paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer,' so it reduces the amount invested, not client's current income; this is a valid structural guard. However, the ongoing annual charge must be met from the fund's performance or client's own resources. The fact-find shows no discretionary income available to cover fees from cashflow. The report does not quantify or evidence the assertion that capacity for loss is 'medium' or explain how, given the monthly deficit and limited liquid reserves, the client can afford market downturns without encroaching on emergency savings. In stress tests (P28), the report claims no shortfall under -30% market crash and 6% inflation scenarios, but these projections assume the transfer completes and assumes state pension and continued employment, and do not reconcile with the current documented deficit. No document reconciles the Fact Find deficit with the recommendation's affordability narrative.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts that the client holds £6,000 in liquid capital and has confirmed this is sufficient to cover unexpected emergencies. The report states: 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies, giving you reassurance as you progress with your retirement planning' (page 3). The report also notes 'You are comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds' (page 8). For emergency access to pension funds, the report indicates 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8). The report does not quantify a required emergency fund amount.

    File:     The Fact Find (P6, P14, P18) confirms the client holds £6,000.00 in a Cash/Savings account as at 22/09/2025. The monthly cashflow analysis (P14) shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, resulting in a monthly deficit of £(288.00). The intelliflo planning proposed scenario (P7) shows opening Liquid Assets of £6,000 in 2025, declining to £5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027, and continuing to decline throughout the projection period. The planning document (P23) shows Total Regular Expenditure of £24,001 in the first year. The Fact Find (P18) itemises monthly expenditure totalling £1,718, comprising rent £600, council tax £200, electricity £120, water £60, food & personal care £300, car/travelling expenses £150, building insurance £25, entertainment £100, and other items. Income sources include employment at £1,200 net monthly plus State Pension at £230 monthly (expected from age 66), totalling £1,430 currently. The projected cashflow tables (P5, P8) show liquid assets remaining relatively stable throughout the projection (around £122k-£185k) but this reflects accessible pension values combined with liquid assets, not liquid reserves in isolation.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts sufficiency of the emergency fund ('You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital') but provides no quantified requirement against which sufficiency can be assessed.

      - The monthly cashflow in the Fact Find shows a deficit of £(288) monthly (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), yet the report does not address how this deficit is to be funded or how the £6,000 emergency fund will be preserved.

      - The report references access to pension funds in 'the future' (10-15 working days) but does not clarify whether this constitutes adequate liquidity for emergencies given the current monthly shortfall.

      - The projected cashflow models show Liquid Assets declining from £6,000 in 2025 to £5,956 by 2026 and further declining, indicating drawdown rather than preservation of the emergency fund.

    The requirement tests whether the affordability of the recommendation is fully supported by and consistent with evidence on financial resilience and liquidity. The report asserts that the client 'has confirmed' sufficient liquid capital for emergencies, but this assertion rests on a guard: An asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified — it must be recorded as unquantified rather than as a contradiction. The £6,000 held is corroborated by the Fact Find. However, critical inconsistencies emerge when the emergency fund is examined against cashflow reality. The Fact Find shows current monthly income of £1,430 against expenditure of £1,718, creating a monthly shortfall of £(288). This deficit cannot be sustained from a £6,000 reserve without rapid depletion (approximately 21 months to exhaustion if no other source funds it). The intelliflo projections show liquid assets declining from £6,000 to lower levels, consistent with drawdown. The report does not address this tension: it asserts the emergency fund is 'sufficient' for unexpected emergencies while the baseline cashflow model demonstrates ongoing monthly shortfalls that will necessarily draw on available liquid resources. The report also does not quantify what 'sufficient' means in relation to monthly expenditure (typically 3-6 months' expenses). At current expenditure of £1,718 monthly, a prudent emergency fund would be £5,154–£10,308; the £6,000 held falls at the lower end of this range and leaves minimal margin. Crucially, once the recommendation is implemented and retirement income begins (State Pension at age 66, currently at £230/month), the income/expenditure position may improve, but the report does not reconcile the current shortfall with emergency fund sufficiency. The report states the client is 'comfortable with the concept' of liquid access, but comfort is not the same as demonstrable affordability. The assertion of sufficiency remains unquantified and the cashflow contradiction (current deficit vs. claimed adequate reserves) is unresolved in the evidence.

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts affordability. Initial commitment: £3,305.55 (3% adviser fee). Ongoing commitment: £1,101.85 annually (1% per annum based on current values and expected contributions).       - Report references 'approximately £1,700 per month' expenditure; Fact Find calculates precisely £1,718.00 monthly expenditure

    The Fact Find establishes a clear monthly deficit of £288 (income £1,430 minus expenditure £1,718). The report's derived figure of £2,786.67 monthly surplus cannot be reconciled from any evidence in the file and contradicts the actual Fact Find position. The client has £6,000 in savings (emergency reserves), but at a monthly deficit of £288, these would be depleted in approximately 21 months if drawn upon. The initial advice fee of £3,305.55 would be approximately 55% of existing liquid reserves (£6,000), severely depleting emergency funds in a household already running at a monthly deficit. The ongoing commitment of £1,101.85 per annum (approximately £92 per month) cannot be afforded from the stated monthly deficit without further drawdown of savings. The report's claim that affordability is supported by a surplus income of £2,786.67 is unsupported by any document in the file and directly contradicted by the Fact Find figure of a £-288.00 monthly deficit. The statement that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living (page 4) cannot be validated given the existing deficit position.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity and access needs are funded from Pension (extracted as /financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/fundedFrom: 'Pension'). The report does not explicitly state the quantum of the emergency fund, its sufficiency, or whether it will be depleted. The extraction found the path /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall absent, meaning the report is silent on emergency fund shortfall analysis.

    File:     The case file evidences £6,000 in liquid savings (Savings/Cash Account) as the only readily accessible emergency reserve at the start of the plan [P9]. This is held separately from the £116,997 in pension assets. The financial planning scenarios show a Surplus Account starting at £0 [P8, P9, P13]. In the proposed scenario, the Liquid Assets column shows values declining from £6,000 (2025) to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), and continuing to decline to approximately £5,500 by 2035, before stabilising around £5,400 thereafter through 2038 [P10, P12]. The cashflow shows consistent small annual deficits being met from liquid assets, with surplus income percentage saved set to 0% [P8]. No explicit emergency fund amount is quantified or discussed in the evidence as a distinct reserve separate from the general savings.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts liquidity needs are funded from Pension, but the evidence shows the only accessible liquid capital at plan inception is £6,000 in a Cash Account (Savings), not pension funds [P9].

      - The report does not quantify or explicitly discuss an emergency fund, whereas the evidence shows liquid assets being drawn down annually to cover shortfalls, contradicting an implicit assertion of maintained liquidity reserves.

      - The proposed scenario shows Liquid Assets declining from £6,000 to £5,912 by 2027 and continuing to erode over time [P10, P12], indicating the emergency fund is being spent down, yet the report does not acknowledge this depletion or assess its sufficiency.

    The guard rule states: 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it, whatever the report says about retaining liquid capital. Read the liquidation order in the scenario before accepting an assertion of sufficiency.' The proposed scenario [P1, P10, P12] shows that Liquid Assets are being liquidated second (after Surplus Account, which starts at £0), declining year-on-year from £6,000. The In Year Surplus/Deficit column shows £0 is being saved in every year [P1, P12], meaning all surplus is consumed. The cashflow mechanics reveal that the £6,000 cash reserve is being drawn upon to meet annual shortfalls. By 2035, Liquid Assets have declined to approximately £5,573 [P10]. The report's assertion that liquidity needs are 'funded from Pension' is inconsistent with the evidence that the modest liquid reserve is the first non-zero asset to be called upon. The second guard applies: the report has not explicitly addressed whether the emergency fund is sufficient, retained, or depleted. The evidence shows it is being eroded. No document in the case file contains an explicit quantified emergency fund assessment or a statement that the £6,000 is adequate for foreseeable needs. The plan assumes income continues until age 66/71 for retirement, but the employment income depends on continued employment (£300/week 'typically' for HGV driving, £1,100/month for Kim's employment [P1, P5]). Income interruption is not explicitly stress-tested against the liquid reserve. The recommendation involves consolidating five pension plans into one Aviva arrangement [P13], which is a transfer between pension wrappers and does not deplete the emergency fund per the first guard. However, the ongoing erosion of the £6,000 liquid asset base to meet cashflow deficits in the proposed scenario is not addressed in the suitability report's affordability narrative.

    Report:   Recommendation: Personal Pension wrapper with selected retirement age 71. The suitability report asserts this wrapper is appropriate for the client. Age at first access: 71.

    File:     Client's current age: 69 (born 07/06/1956, per [P12] and [P19]). Minimum pension age: 55 (per [P3], [P4]). Client already over minimum pension age. At age 71, client will be able to access pension via income drawdown or cash lump sum (per [P4], [P14]). Flexi-access drawdown available from age 55 onwards, with ad hoc and regular withdrawals permitted (per [P2], [P3]). Client's liquid assets: £6,000 in savings (per [P12], [P19], [P21]). Proposed pension value post-consolidation: £110,185 (per [P19]). Cashflow analysis shows no shortfall projected throughout lifetime to age 100 (per [P25]). Income sources: employment (£300 per week, per [P25]), state pension (£11,973 per annum from age 71, per [P25]). Annual expenditure in retirement: approximately £1,700 (per [P25]).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report specifies retirement age 71; client is currently age 69, meaning 2 years elapse before recommended access point. No evidenced access need requiring liquidity before age 71 is cited in the suitability report. However, an access need between now (age 69) and age 71 is not explicitly ruled out or addressed. The report asserts 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' ([P25]), suggesting income adequacy from state pension onset, but this occurs at age 71 for this client, not immediately.

      - Client has £6,000 liquid savings currently ([P12], [P19], [P21]). The cashflow assumes this can be drawn if needed ([P25]). However, no explicit emergency fund quantum or reserve-adequacy statement appears in the suitability report itself regarding affordability and resilience.

      - Recommended wrapper (Personal Pension) permits access from age 55 onwards via flexi-access drawdown ([P4]). Client is currently age 69, already past minimum pension age. No mismatch exists between wrapper restrictions and client age. However, the report does not explicitly confirm that the client is aware of or has agreed to defer access until age 71, nor does it evidence any need to lock capital until that date.

    The check examines whether recommended wrapper access restrictions prevent the client from meeting evidenced access needs. Client is aged 69, already above minimum pension age (55). Recommended wrapper is Personal Pension, which permits access from age 55 via flexi-access drawdown (per [P4]). No liquidity mismatch arises from wrapper restriction, because the wrapper does not lock capital beyond the client's age. Retirement age 71 is selected; at that point, income drawdown or lump sum withdrawal becomes possible within the same wrapper ([P4], [P14]). Client has £6,000 liquid savings outside the pension ([P12], [P19], [P21]), and cashflow projects no shortfall to age 100 ([P25]). No evidenced access need before age 71 is cited in the suitability report or the planning documents. The planning assumes 0% of surplus income is saved ([P27]), meaning all surplus is spent, yet no shortfall is projected. However, the suitability report does not explicitly document that the client has no access need before age 71, nor does it quantify an emergency fund or resilience reserve. The assertion that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' ([P25]) is accurate for retirement income from age 71 onwards, but does not address 2-year pre-retirement period between now (age 69) and retirement (age 71). The report is silent on how liquidity and resilience are managed in that interim period. Per the decision logic guard: pension access restrictions are irrelevant where the client is already over minimum pension age. This guard applies here: client is 69, minimum age 55. Wrapper does not create a liquidity mismatch on age grounds. However, the absence of explicit documentation of access needs, emergency reserves or interim-period resilience means the affordability assessment is incomplete from a liquidity and resilience perspective, even though no actual mismatch is triggered by the wrapper choice itself.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four foreseeable changes: (1) Client retirement in Oct 2027 with net income loss of £1,386.67/month (loss of HGV employment £1,300/month and Sea Cadets income £86.67/month); (2) Kim's retirement in Mar 2026 with net income loss of £1,100/month from employment; (3) Kim's State Pension commencement in Mar 2026 adding £1,000/month net income; (4) IHT regime change for pensions from 6 Apr 2027 with unquantified financial impact. The report quotes client expectations to retire within two years and Kim to work until age 66. Emergency access to pension drawdown is stated as requiring 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking.

    File:     Fact Find [P1] states total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 with no expected changes in foreseeable future, and total net monthly income of £1,430 creating disposable income deficit of £288/month. Fact Find [P14] records State Pension entitlement of £230/month and employed basic income at £1,200/month with start date 22/09/2025. File notes [P15] confirm client 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' intelliflo planning scenarios [P12] show retirement events: Kim retire age 66 in 2026, John retire age 71 in 2027. Baseline and Proposed scenarios [P6][P12] show opening liquid assets of £6,000 (cash savings) plus accessible pension values totalling £116,997. Planning report [P5] shows Summary Cashflow with Required Withdrawal lines indicating drawdowns from pensions and investments where expenditure exceeds income in retirement years.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states employment income loss of £1,300/month on client retirement; Fact Find [P14] shows employed basic income of £1,200/month, a difference of £100/month unaccounted for.

      - Report states State Pension £230/week when Kim reaches State Pension age (derived as £1,000/month net); Fact Find [P14] records State Pension as £230/month, not weekly, creating inconsistency in quantum and basis.

      - Report asserts no change in household expenditure upon retirement; Fact Find [P1] states current expenditure £1,718/month but planning scenarios [P5] show expenditure line changing post-retirement, contradicting the assertion of no expected changes.

      - Report states current monthly disposable income as unspecified; Fact Find [P1] calculates current disposable income as negative £288/month (£1,430 income minus £1,718 expenditure), indicating current affordability constraint not reflected in retirement planning narrative.

      - Report provides IHT regime change event dated 6 Apr 2027 with null financial impact; no evidence in file quantifies or addresses the implications of this change for the pension recommendation.

    The report addresses planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs by identifying four material events with income impacts. However, significant inconsistencies between the report's stated figures and the supporting evidence create material uncertainty about affordability. First, the employment income quantum differs by £100/month between report narrative (£1,300 HGV) and Fact Find (£1,200 total employed). The Sea Cadets component (£86.67/month) is not separately itemised in any supporting document, making it impossible to verify this element of the income loss calculation. Second, State Pension is expressed as £230/week in the report quote but recorded as £230/month in the Fact Find [P14], a seven-fold discrepancy that fundamentally changes the income projection from retirement. If the report intends £230/week (approximately £996/month), this is consistent with the derived £1,000/month figure; if the Fact Find is correct at £230/month, the report overstates retirement income by approximately £766/month. Third, the report asserts that household expenditure will not change upon retirement, but the Fact Find [P1] explicitly records current expenditure of £1,718/month and the planning scenarios [P5] model expenditure lines that extend through retirement years without showing the flat continuation the report claims. The current financial position shows a £288/month deficit before retirement (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), yet the affordability narrative in the report does not address how this is currently being funded or how the retirement income projections resolve this structural shortfall. The planning scenarios show Required Withdrawals from pensions and investments [P5], indicating drawdown-based retirement funding, but the suitability report does not detail the sequencing, quantum or sustainability of these withdrawals. The IHT regime change from 6 Apr 2027 is cited but unquantified, and no evidence addresses the report's treatment of this known future tax regime shift. The emergency access provision (10-15 working days for pension drawdown) is stated but not connected to the client's actual liquidity needs post-retirement. The file notes [P15] record the client's retirement income need as £1,700 net/month, which is £18/month above current expenditure of £1,718, but no justification is provided for why this stated need differs marginally from current expenditure while the cashflow analysis shows ongoing deficits requiring pension withdrawals. The conjunction of: (a) current cash position of only £6,000 against current monthly deficit of £288; (b) income quantum discrepancies of £100-£766/month depending on which statement is correct; (c) assertion of unchanged expenditure contradicted by planning model; and (d) unquantified future tax regime change—creates material obscurity about whether the recommendation's affordability has been fully demonstrated from a financial resilience perspective.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report provides 28 distinct rationale texts across multiple recommendations. The canonical model marks 22 of 28 as isClientSpecific=true and 6 as isClientSpecific=false. Rationales include: (1) workplace scheme rationale linked to OBJ3; (2) small pot tax treatment linked to OBJ3; (3) lower costs (0.06% more expensive) linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (4) lifestyling risk drift linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (5) greater investment choice linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3; (6) adviser charging facilitation linked to OBJ1 and OBJ2; (7) ongoing monitoring linked implicitly to OBJ2; (8-17) repeated variations of Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, and Ongoing Monitoring; (18) portfolio drift control linked to risk profile; (19-28) generic statements about diversification, cost competitiveness, fund performance, CIP review, risk-profiled solutions, and multi-asset fund benefits. The report asserts rationale is present for each recommendation and linked to stated objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, and OBJ3.

    File:     Evidence shows: (P4, P8) Policy Research document records Overall File Strategy stating 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement. Will be recommending to consolidate where necessary and provide cashflow planning around his retirement.' Alternative Strategies noted as 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things.' Investment Choice Goals listed as 'ATR 5 CIP funds'. (P4, P8) Policy Research for individual arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows) shows column 'Which objective/ goal is this achieving?' with all cells blank except notation of rationale (Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing). (P10) Suitability Report states 'You have been provided with a copy of our Client Agreement and Important Information about our Investment, Protection and Pension Services... This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives'. (P9, P10) Report recommends switching Aviva pension into new advised Aviva pension £3,744.36 but rationale linking this specific action to a numbered objective is not quoted in evidence. (P7) SIPP research report provides MiFID II cost disclosures showing fund-level charges (HSBC 0.19% total, CT Universal MAP 0.55% total, aggregated 0.37%) but no narrative explaining why these specific funds meet client's risk profile OBJ2 or OBJ3. (P12) Risk profile report documents agreed risk level process but does not quote specific rationale connecting fund selection to risk level maintenance post-switch. (P5) SIPP research report recommends £110,185 investment but provides no narrative explanation of why this amount or fund allocation meets stated objectives. Evidence contains no passages quoting or paraphrasing rationale texts extracted from the report for 8 of the 28 rationales; no evidence corroborates the claim that 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (rationale [3]) or 'the plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (rationale [13]); no research document explains why Greater Investment Choice or Adviser Charging Facilitation is suitable for this client's specific circumstances beyond generic template language.

    Discrepancies:

      - Rationale [3] states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but derived figure analysis shows Aviva existing charge at 0.50% and recommended Aviva Platform at 0.44%, a difference of 0.06% in favour of the new plan (not more expensive); text contradicts its own arithmetic claim.

      - Rationale [13] states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but no supporting charge comparison is provided in evidence for the arrangement this applies to, and the derived figures show charge discrepancies suggesting calculations were performed on wrong arrangements (EA5 rows computed on Zurich £103,439 not SL £3,002).

      - Policy Research document (P4, P8) shows blank cells in 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' column for Aviva, Zurich, and Scottish Widows recommendations, indicating objectives were not linked to individual arrangement rationales at research stage, yet report extraction shows linkedObjectiveIds present.

      - Rationale texts [19-28] marked isClientSpecific=false in extraction are generic statements ('The charges are competitive', 'The funds have performed well', 'diversification and reduced risk') that are repeated identically or near-identically across multiple recommendations, violating the boilerplate guard.

      - Report asserts adviser charging facilitation as a switching advantage (rationale [6], [11], [17]) but evidence (P4, P8) notes existing arrangements show 'No' or blank for 'Can adviser charging be applied', suggesting existing arrangements did not permit adviser charging; this is not explained in the rationale as a switch benefit requiring explanation of current constraint.

      - Suitability Report (P9, P10) provides initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) and ongoing 1% per annum but does not quote client-specific rationale explaining why fee structure is suitable for this client's means, linking it to OBJ1 or confirming client acceptance; P2 (Fact Find) does not record explicit client agreement to fee levels in the passages provided.

      - Transfer value discrepancy: report recommends £110,185 transfer (P5) but derived figures show existing arrangements total £116,998.47, a gap of £6,813.47 unexplained in any rationale or analysis; no rationale addresses why certain existing arrangements are excluded from consolidation objective OBJ3.

      - Fund allocation reported as HSBC 50% and CT Universal MAP 50% (P7 cost table) but rationale [9] explains Zurich blended allocation (risk 7 + risk 2 due to lack of risk 5 funds available) as a compromise; evidence does not clarify which fund allocation applies to which arrangement or whether both allocations were recommended, creating ambiguity about which rationale applies to which action.

    The extraction identifies 28 rationale texts but marks only 6 as non-client-specific (false). However, applying the boilerplate guard reveals substantial repetition: 'Greater Investment Choice' appears identically in rationale [5] and [8] and [15]; 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' appears in [6], [11], and [17] (three times); 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' appears in [7], [12], and [18] (three times); 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' appears in [10] and [16]; 'Lower Costs' and 'Lifestyling' each appear twice. These repeated bullets violate the guard and should be counted once per unique recommendation, not restated. The extraction marks [19-28] as generic ('provide exposure', 'professionally managed', 'regularly reviewed', 'cost competitive', 'performed well') and flags isClientSpecific=false for these, correctly. However, rationales [1-2] are genuinely client-specific: they name the workplace scheme, the £200 small pot, £170 net estimate, and reference 'your employer' and 'your retirement'. Rationale [3] contains a factual contradiction: it claims Aviva is '0.06% more expensive' when derived figures show 0.50% existing vs 0.44% new, making the new plan 0.06% cheaper. This contradiction between what the report states and what its own numbers show is a severity-highest finding. Evidence (P4, P8) shows Policy Research with blank objective linkages, suggesting contemporary research did not document which objective each recommendation addressed; the report's linkedObjectiveIds may be post-hoc assignment. Rationale [9] concerning Zurich's lack of risk 5 funds necessitating a 7+2 blend is explained but the evidence does not show whether this blend recommendation contradicts the client's agreed risk level or is a defensible workaround. The report asserts alternatives were considered (P4 notes 'Alternative Strategies' section) but provides minimal detail on why alternatives were rejected beyond 'too much information' and 'expensive'. The regulation COBS 9.4.1R requires clear explanation of suitability; the report provides rationale text but evidence does not corroborate that each recommendation was discussed with the client or that client agreed objectives are those documented in the report. Guard on vulnerability overlay: No evidence of vulnerability characteristics is cited in the pack; the overlay does not apply to this finding. The core issue is that rationale is present but generic in bulk, contradicted in specific claims (0.06% cost), unsupported by objective linkage documentation at research stage (P4, P8 blank cells), and lacks evidence of client agreement to the stated objectives or alternatives rejection logic.
