  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that Aviva Pension Portfolio (Personal Pension wrapper) is recommended. For provider selection, it lists 8 rationales: Financial strength (A rating, £407bn AUM); Choice (6,900+ funds); Expertise (Aviva Investors award-winning strategies); Value (transparent, competitive charges); Convenience (online tools); Governance (regulatory oversight); Innovation (digital investment); and Lower Costs (lower cost structure vs existing pensions). For investment strategy, it recommends HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%), justified by: risk profile alignment; diversification; blend of active/passive; simplicity; competitive charges; strong performance; CIP membership with quarterly review; and multi-asset fund rationale. The report states the new scheme has lower overall cost structure and greater investment choice than existing arrangements.

    File:     The research documents show: (P1, P9) Aviva Platform was identified through a research process with 4 products shortlisted (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime), with no DNA analysis applied and no filters used. (P5) A separate SIPP research identified 1 product from 118 matched (Pension Portfolio - Choice by Aviva). (P10) Fund research shows HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio with ongoing costs of £99.17 (0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund with ongoing costs £159.77 (0.29%), giving total aggregated costs of £406.74 (0.37%). Performance data (P12) shows both funds' discrete returns over multiple years. (P13) Policy research notes for Aviva: 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' as reasons it isn't suitable, yet the same Aviva is recommended as the new solution. For Zurich: 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'. (P14) Pension switch report recommends consolidation to Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice on Aviva Platform. (P2) Discussion of investment pathways notes they offer 'potentially lower costs; reduced complexity' but were rejected because client requires tailored solution, not generic pathways. (P8) Key Features describe the Platform and Pension Portfolio as flexible SIPP offering consolidation benefits. No direct comparison of Aviva Platform against the 3 other shortlisted platforms (Elevate, ARC, Wealthtime) is evidenced.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts provider selection based on financial strength, choice, expertise, value, convenience, governance, and innovation as absolute strengths. Evidence shows research identified 4 shortlisted platforms (Aviva, Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) but no comparative analysis or DNA benchmarking was performed (P1: 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out'; P9 shows only Aviva's record).

      - Report claims lower costs than existing arrangements. However, P13 notes on existing Aviva arrangement state 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' yet the recommended solution is also Aviva (albeit Pension Portfolio platform variant). The claim of lower costs versus existing Aviva is not explained.

      - Report recommends Aviva despite P13 research noting existing Aviva as 'expensive'. The contradiction between calling existing Aviva expensive and recommending new Aviva is not resolved in rationale.

      - Report lists provider rationales as generic industry-wide attributes (financial strength rating, fund choice quantity, regulatory oversight, innovation) rather than client-specific comparisons explaining why Aviva was chosen over Elevate, Aegon ARC, or Wealthtime, which were equally shortlisted.

      - Investment strategy rationales are partially generic: 'diversification', 'blend of active and passive', 'competitive charges', 'strong performance', 'governance' apply to many multi-asset funds and are not fund-specific justifications. Some rationales (CIP membership, quarterly review) are adviser-specific monitoring claims rather than product-specific features.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Comparative analysis between the 4 shortlisted platforms (Aviva Platform vs Elevate vs Aegon ARC vs Wealthtime) showing why Aviva was selected. Research document (P1) shows 4 products shortlisted but states 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' and 'No filters were applied', indicating no discriminating analysis was performed., Explanation for the contradiction between P13 research labeling existing Aviva as 'expensive' and the recommendation to switch to new Aviva (Pension Portfolio on Aviva Platform)., Comparative fund analysis explaining why HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (0.18% ongoing) and CT Universal MAP Balanced (0.29% ongoing, 0.55% total MiFID II cost per P10) were selected over alternatives in the 6,900 fund universe., Investment pathway rejection rationale specific to this client's circumstances, beyond generic statements that client needs tailored solution., Client-specific justification linking provider selection (Aviva Platform) to stated objectives. Objectives referenced are OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3 but provider rationales do not name which objectives each provider strength addresses.

    The report presents two classes of justification: generic provider attributes and adviser-facilitation claims. Guard 1 applies: the provider rationales (financial strength, fund choice, expertise, value, convenience, governance, innovation) are absolute marketing-style claims, not comparative statements explaining selection over the 3 other shortlisted platforms. The research evidence (P1, P9) shows Aviva Platform was one of 4 shortlisted with no discriminating analysis applied ('No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', 'No filters were applied'). This means the comparison cannot be shown to have been performed; the evidence reveals shortlisting but not selection reasoning. Guard 2 (panel/CIP membership) partially applies to investment strategy: the report states funds are 'part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition)' regularly reviewed by investment committee, which is legitimate documented-panel justification, corroborated by P2's discussion of CIP monitoring. However, this explains adviser-driven governance, not why these specific two funds (rather than other CIP members) suit this client's risk and objectives. A material contradiction appears in P13: the policy research document evaluates the existing Aviva arrangement as 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' as reasons it is unsuitable, yet the recommended solution is Aviva Pension Portfolio (still Aviva as provider). The report does not explain how switching from an expensive Aviva product to a different Aviva product resolves the cost objection. The fund rationales mix generic quality claims ('competitive charges', 'strong performance', 'diversification') with client-specific framing ('your risk profile', 'your financial goals'). However, no evidence demonstrates that these specific funds were compared against alternatives, or that their costs (0.37% total MiFID II per P10) are competitive relative to other Balanced/risk-rated funds in the 6,900-fund menu. The lower-cost claim versus existing arrangements lacks quantified support: existing Zurich is noted as 0.93% (P13), Aviva existing 0.50%, so new Aviva at 0.44% platform + fund charges would be lower, but this calculation is not explicit in the report's narrative rationale.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances

  N/A — severity High

    Report:   The canonical model asserts isClientSpecific across 28 rationale elements, with 22 marked as true and 6 marked as false. The report claims to provide personalised rationale linked to client objectives for the majority of recommendations.

    File:     The suitability report excerpt [P13] states 'This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives - whilst at the same time highlighting any potential disadvantages and risks'. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The Policy Research file [P14] notes 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement.' The same file's recommendation table [P15] shows recommendations for Aviva (Switch), Zurich (Switch), People's Pension (Retain), and Scottish Widows (Switch), with reasons cited as 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' (Aviva); 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' (Zurich); 'Active workplace pension' (People's Pension). The Suitability Report excerpt [P13] provides only one visible rationale: 'Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension - £3,744.36' with no explanation of why this meets the client's objectives.

    Discrepancies:

      - The canonical model reports 28 rationale elements with 22 marked isClientSpecific=true, but only one recommendation rationale is visible in the suitability report excerpt [P13], making it impossible to verify that 22 distinct personalised rationales exist in the document.

      - Six rationale elements are marked isClientSpecific=false in the canonical model, but no evidence is provided showing which recommendations these are or what non-personalised rationales appear.

      - The Policy Research file [P15] shows recommendation reasons that are generic descriptors (e.g. 'Expensive, no adviser servicing') rather than client-specific rationales linking to the stated objective of consolidation and simplification.

      - The visible recommendation in [P13] ('Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension - £3,744.36') contains no rationale text at all explaining why this switch meets the client's objectives of consolidation and understanding.

    The requirement demands that the suitability report contain personalised, client-specific rationales linked to individual circumstances for each recommendation. The canonical model asserts that 22 of 28 rationale elements carry isClientSpecific=true and 6 carry false. The evidence pack does not contain sufficient text from the suitability report to verify this claim. Only a single recommendation excerpt appears in [P13], which lacks any rationale text whatsoever. The Policy Research file [P15], which may document the adviser's working notes, shows recommendations paired with brief, generic reasons ('Expensive', 'Lifestyle', 'no adviser servicing', 'Active workplace pension') rather than personalised explanations linking each recommendation to the client's stated needs. The client's stated objective is clear in [P5] and [P14]: consolidation of multiple pension arrangements to simplify management, with retirement in approximately 2 years and a target household income of £1,700 net per month at ATR 5. A client-specific rationale for, for example, switching Aviva or Zurich pensions would explain why consolidation of those specific plans into an advised Aviva Platform meets this client's specific need for simplification and how the recommended structure supports the £1,700 retirement income goal. The evidence does not support that such rationales appear in the suitability report. The guard on standard regulatory wording is satisfied (none is confused with personalisation). The absence of the full suitability report text prevents confirmation of whether isClientSpecific assertions are accurate. The visible excerpt and working notes suggest personalisation is limited or absent. This is not evidence that the report fails the requirement; it is evidence that the comparison cannot be completed from the pack provided.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts four alternatives considered for the recommendation: (1) 'Leave pot to grow until retirement' (Do-Nothing) — rejected because encashing now is preferred to simplify arrangements, though this is marked non-client-specific; (2) 'Internal fund switch within Zurich' (In-Situ Switch) — rejected because client's main priority is to consolidate pensions, gain better control, and be well-positioned for retirement with all available drawdown options, and multi-asset funds not available within risk range and blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options (client-specific, linked to OBJ3); (3) 'Stakeholder pension with single multi-asset fund' — rejected because stakeholder pensions have maximum charge of 1.5% per annum for first 10 years then 1% thereafter, but recommended solution is more cost effective, and stakeholder pensions typically do not allow flexible access and have limited fund ranges (non-client-specific); (4) 'Investment Pathway solutions' — rejected because client requires more tailored solution than the four generic solutions offered, does not reflect individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile as accurately, client requires regular review and adjustment as part of ongoing advice service, and has not performed as well as recommended investment strategy (client-specific, linked to OBJ1, OBJ2). The research file lists five options considered: OPT1 'Leave the existing pension scheme untouched' (Do-Nothing, not recommended, no rejection reasons listed); OPT2 'Leave the existing pension scheme in place and switch investment funds' (In-Situ Fund Switch, not recommended, no rejection reasons listed); OPT3 'Switch the existing pension scheme into another existing pension arrangement' (Consolidate Into Existing, not recommended, no rejection reasons listed); OPT4 'Switch your existing schemes into your employer's pension arrangement' (Employer Scheme, not recommended, with three rejection reasons: loss of employer contribution benefit, funds do not meet risk profile and plan is 0.06% more expensive with £4.50 annual admin charge, no multi-asset funds available and limited range); OPT5 'Set up a new pension plan to move your existing pension schemes into' (New Plan, recommended). For individual existing arrangements, the report recommends: People's Pension — Retain (do-nothing); Scottish Widows — Encash under small pots rules; Aviva — Switch to Aviva Platform; Zurich — (implied switch based on overall recommendation); Standard Life — (implied switch based on overall recommendation).

    File:     [P6] shows five existing pension arrangements with retirement options: People's Pension (FAD, Full and partial UFPLS), Scottish Widows (Full and partial UFPLS, noted 'Retain and Encash'), Aviva (FAD, Full and partial UFPLS), Zurich (Full and partial UFPLS), Standard Life (FAD). [P18] states recommendations for existing schemes: 'People's Pension - 6197175/PP | Retain' and 'Scottish Widows - ZU4225575 | Encash'. [P22] documents research notes: Scottish Widows discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing; Aviva alternative option shows only mixed asset Aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet client's ATR, so discounted; Zurich alternative option notes Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, blend of 7 and 2 has been used, no multi-asset funds in risk range, blend consists of managed AP (client already invested in this) and asset specific fund, wouldn't recommend singular asset types, blend of funds have not performed as well as offered on Aviva marginally, main priority for client is to consolidate, get better handle on pensions and be ready for retirement with all options; Standard Life discounted as all funds within same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what can be offered at Aviva. [P20] shows pension switching summary recommending consolidation of Aviva plan, Zurich pension, and Standard Life pension into Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice on Aviva Platform. [P1], [P2], [P3], [P4], [P5], [P10], [P19] show comparative pension switching analyses with effect of switching, critical yield, and reduction in yield data, demonstrating analysis of existing vs new solutions. [P13] shows fund cost disclosure for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund selected. Research documents ([P7], [P8], [P11], [P12], [P15]) show SIPP research with Aviva Platform recommended after platform selection process.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report cites 'Leave pot to grow until retirement' as alternative considered, marked as 'Inferred' assertion, with rationale about 'encashing now is preferred to simplify arrangements'; but [P22] notes this alternative (small pots encashment of Scottish Widows) was 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing', not because encashing is preferred to simplify — the Scottish Widows rationale differs from the general rationale stated in report

      - Report's rationale for rejecting Internal fund switch within Zurich cites 'blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options', but [P22] uses more measured language: 'blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally' — 'marginally' qualifier present in research but not in report assertion

      - Report states for OPT4 (Employer Scheme): 'Funds do not meet your risk profile; plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution with £4.50 annual admin charge', but this refers to comparison with Aviva existing plan (charge 0.50%), not the new recommended solution (Aviva Platform charge 0.44%); the 0.06% difference calculation is unexplained in context of whether this compares old to new or old to old

      - Report's Investment Pathway rejection reason states 'It has not performed as well as the recommended investment strategy' but [P13] shows fund costs only: HSBC at 0.19% total and CT Universal MAP at 0.55% total, aggregated at 0.37%; no comparative performance data provided to support this assertion against Investment Pathways

      - Report states 'Stakeholder pension' alternative rejected partly because 'the solution recommended is more cost effective than a typical stakeholder pension' but [P1] shows Reduction in Yield of 0.6% for new solution; no stakeholder pension RIY comparison provided in evidence to corroborate this cost comparison claim

      - Research file lists OPT1, OPT2, OPT3 with 'wasRecommended: false' and empty 'rejectionReasons' arrays, but report does not present these three options to client with any reasons for rejection — significant silence on three alternatives

      - Research file shows individual arrangement alternatives (EA1_REC, EA2_REC, etc.) with recommendations but no rejection reasons listed for Aviva and Zurich switches, whereas report's overall recommendation section does not detail rationale for switching these two beyond consolidation objective

      - Report's guidance on People's Pension retention states rationale is 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement' but does not state whether any alternative (e.g., switch to Aviva) was considered and rejected for this arrangement specifically

    The suitability report does present alternatives considered with reasons for rejection across four categories plus individual arrangement recommendations. The research file corroborates the existence of comparative analysis (pension switching reports P1-P5, P20 show detailed existing vs new solution comparisons). However, material inconsistencies arise: (1) The report marks the 'Leave pot to grow' rationale as 'non-client-specific' ('SimplificationPreference', isClientSpecific: false) and cites a generic simplification preference, but the evidence in P22 and P18 shows the actual reason was specific to Scottish Widows small pots encashment being problematic ('no servicing and reduced flexibility'), not a general simplification strategy. (2) The 'Internal fund switch within Zurich' rejection reason includes performance comparison language ('have not performed as well') but the performance comparison is modal ('marginally') in the research file and no actual performance data appears in the evidence pack — the assertion of underperformance is not corroborated by comparative performance metrics. (3) For the employer scheme (OPT4), the cost comparison states the plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but this figure appears to come from comparison with the existing Aviva arrangement (0.50% vs 0.44%), not with other alternatives, and the evidence shows this calculation is computed on the wrong underlying plan value per the derived figures audit (EA5 rows are computed on Zurich fund value £103,438 not Standard Life £3,002). (4) Three alternatives (OPT1, OPT2, OPT3) are listed in research with no rejection reasons and do not appear mentioned in the suitability report at all — this is a report gap: options listed in research but not presented to client. (5) The Investment Pathway rejection reason claiming performance underperformance is asserted in the report but no fund performance data comparing Investment Pathways to the selected funds (HSBC and CT Universal MAP) appears in the evidence; only cost data (MiFID II) is present. (6) The stakeholder pension cost comparison claims the recommended solution is more cost effective but evidence shows only RIY of 0.6% for new solution; no stakeholder RIY is provided for comparison — the claim is unsupported. (7) Aviva and Zurich individual switches are presented as recommendations without detailed alternative rejection reasons documented for those two specific arrangements in the suitability section, though research notes exist. (8) The report's rationale structure is inconsistent: some alternatives have 'isClientSpecific: true' (Zurich fund switch, Investment Pathways) with linked objectives, others marked 'false' (Leave pot to grow, Stakeholder, Initial performance claim on Investment Pathways), but the actual evidence shows all are informed by client objectives (consolidation, risk rating 5, withdrawal flexibility). Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability characteristics identified in the evidence would materially affect this check's assessment.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies 5 existing arrangements: (1) People's Pension - Retain; (2) Scottish Widows - Encash; (3) Aviva pension my future focus consolidation - FullTransfer; (4) Zurich Managed Pn AP - FullTransfer; (5) SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund - FullTransfer. Each has a stated rationale. People's Pension rationale: 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement' and 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' Scottish Widows rationale for encashment: 'I would recommend you withdraw the full amount of this pension under the small pots rules and put the money into your savings account. This would not detract from your financial retirement plan' with tax calculation detail. Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life transfer rationales cite: Lower Costs, Lifestyling risk concerns, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments, and Multi-asset fund availability.

    File:     The case file contains policy documents for each arrangement. [P3], [P5] and [P21] show a policy research table listing 5 arrangements: Aviva (SP50163762), Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL), Standard Life (D2301334000), Peoples Pension (6197175/PP), and Scottish Widows (ZU4225575). Individual policy documents [P7] for Aviva, [P9] for Zurich, [P4] and [P6] for Standard Life, and [P13] for Peoples Pension confirm these arrangements exist with specified values. [P21] contains a recommendation table stating 'People's Pension - 6197175/PP' has 'Other Considerations: Current workplace scheme' and 'Scottish Widows - ZU4225575' shows 'Retain and Encash'. [P23] explicitly states 'Standard Life - D2301334000' advice as 'Switch to Aviva Platform' with detailed rationales for Lower Costs, Lifestyling concerns, Greater Investment Choice, Enhanced Retirement Flexibility, Adviser Charging Facilitation, and Ongoing Monitoring. No specific evidence document states the Aviva and Zurich transfer recommendations or their rationales verbatim.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report rationale on Standard Life states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' [P23] but the canonical model extract for Aviva shows 0.44% ongoing charge against Standard Life's 0.18% or 0.26% (depending on row interpretation in derived figures), creating an arithmetic contradiction: 0.18% or 0.26% is not 0.08% more expensive than 0.44%.

      - Report cites 'Lower Costs' as rationale for Standard Life transfer, but [P23] states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', which contradicts the rationale that costs are lower in the receiving scheme.

      - Aviva arrangement (EA3) current value £3,744.36 appears in policy document [P7] but the recommendation to 'FullTransfer' lacks explicit textual support in the retrieved passages; the canonical model asserts this action but no passage contains the specific recommendation statement for Aviva's transfer.

      - Zurich arrangement (EA4) current value £103,439.24 appears in policy document [P9] but no passage retrieved contains an explicit recommendation to fully transfer Zurich; the rationales in canonical model reference Zurich's fund limitations but do not appear verbatim in evidence.

    All five existing arrangements have stated recommended outcomes in the canonical model: Retain (People's Pension), Encash (Scottish Widows), and FullTransfer (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life). Each outcome is supported by client-specific rationales in the extraction. However, the evidence base for verification is partial. [P21] and [P23] corroborate the People's Pension retention (workplace scheme, ongoing contributions), Scottish Widows encashment (small pots rules with tax relief potential), and Standard Life transfer (lifestyling concern, investment choice, lower costs framing). The rationales for Aviva and Zurich transfers are present in the canonical model but not retrieved in the searched passages; the file shows policy documents confirming these arrangements exist, but the specific advice to transfer them is asserted by the extraction rather than directly quoted. A critical arithmetic discrepancy emerges: the report claims Standard Life charges are 'lower' in the receiving scheme (Aviva Platform) yet [P23] simultaneously states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.' The derived figures show this contradiction: if Standard Life carries 0.18% or 0.26% and Aviva Platform carries 0.44%, Standard Life is not more expensive. This is a material internal inconsistency within the report's own rationale. The report also applies shared boilerplate rationales ('Lower Costs', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Lifestyling', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring') across multiple different arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), reducing specificity to individual arrangement circumstances. The Scottish Widows and People's Pension rationales are sufficiently specific (workplace scheme continuation, employer contributions, small pot tax relief). No vulnerability factors identified in the evidence materially affect this assessment.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome

  Potential Concern — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report makes six recommendations, each with: (1) an action type (Transfer, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch, Rebalance); (2) a summary describing what is to be switched or encashed; (3) an advised status of 'Recommended' for all six; (4) an intended outcome for each (e.g. 'Consolidate pension arrangements while retaining employer contributions...', 'Withdraw small pension fund under small pots rules...'); and (5) implementation details stating the adviser will implement all six, with a timescale of '10-15 working days' for administration. The report states 'Expected Timescale: 10-15 working days' and lists next steps including completion of beneficiary nomination form, review of cancellation notice (30 days), and acknowledgment of out-of-market risk. For all six recommendations, /recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement is true and /recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty is 'Adviser'.

    File:     The case file contains terms and conditions for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension (P1-P8), a risk profile report showing agreed risk level 5 Balanced (P9, P12, P15), policy information for People's Pension and Zurich (P14, P13), a SIPP research report (P12) detailing the fund allocation (CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%), a client agreement (P10), and investor experience notes showing no additional notes recorded (P11). The file does not contain a document explicitly stating the adviser's recommendation status, implementation responsibility, or intended outcome for each of the six changes. The risk profile document (P15) states 'we agreed a risk level' and projection details, but does not detail the specific pension switching actions or their rationale. No passage in the file directly addresses whether the adviser recommends each change, who will implement each one, or the intended outcome of each specific action.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts six discrete recommendations each with Recommended status, intended outcome, and adviser implementation responsibility. The file contains no corroborating passages that explicitly state this advice pattern for any of the six recommendations.

      - The report states implementation timescale of '10-15 working days' and lists next steps. The file contains terms and conditions describing how the Aviva platform operates (P1-P8) but does not evidence that these timescales or next steps were communicated in advice or agreed with the client.

      - The report's intended outcome for recommendation 1 is to 'Consolidate pension arrangements while retaining employer contributions and current workplace scheme benefits' but the file does not evidence this specific rationale or outcome.

      - The report's intended outcome for recommendation 2 is to 'Withdraw small pension fund under small pots rules and deposit into savings account' but the file does not evidence this specific outcome or reference to small pots rules.

    The requirement G6.6 limb on Consistency requires that where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome. The canonical model extract shows all six recommendations carry the fields /adviceStatus='Recommended', /implementation/willAdviserImplement=true, and /implementation/responsibleParty='Adviser'. These fields are present and consistent in the report's own assertion. The /intendedOutcome field is populated for all six recommendations with specific, differentiated statements of purpose (consolidation, withdrawal under small pots, flexibility improvements, cost reductions, etc.). The /solution/implementation section specifies a timescale ('10-15 working days'), lists next steps (beneficiary nomination, cancellation notice review, out-of-market risk acknowledgment), and includes quotes from the report's pages 8 and 16. The requirement is fundamentally about whether the suitability report itself contains these statements; it is not a requirement that external evidence corroborate them. The method of assessment is PresenceOnly. On this method, the comparison cannot fail on the basis that the file's passages do not repeat or corroborate the report's statements. The file's silence about recommendation status, implementation responsibility, or intended outcome is not evidence that the report omitted these elements. The extraction metadata indicates expectedButAbsent and ambiguities relating to other fields (risk rating, capacity for loss) but not to the /recommendations[] fields assessed here. The report's own canonical model shows these fields are present and populated. On a PresenceOnly method, this constitutes a valid statement by the report that each change is recommended, who will implement it, and what the intended outcome is. The report states these facts internally consistently: all six are Recommended, all will be implemented by the Adviser, all have stated outcomes. There is no internal contradiction. The guard 'An implementation step handled by the provider rather than the adviser is still a stated step; the finding is silence, not who happens to act' does not apply here because the report explicitly names the Adviser as responsible party. The guard 'A change the client instructed and the adviser is merely executing should say so' does not apply because there is no evidence in the file of a client instruction overriding adviser recommendation. The assessment is whether the report itself states these elements, not whether the file corroborates them. The report does state them.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts six recommendations with the following implementation details: (1) Transfer - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (2) Encash - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (3) Switch - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (4) Switch - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (5) Switch - 10-15 working days, Adviser; (6) Rebalance - Adviser. All are attributed to 'Adviser' as responsible party. Overall solution timescale stated as '10-15 working days'. Next steps include: (a) Complete nomination of beneficiaries form 'after the transfer process is begun' (client-specific, page 16); (b) Review cancellation notice with '30 days to review and consider options' after plan setup (boilerplate, page 16); (c) Stock market movements disclosure stating client 'are comfortable with this concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access to funds' (client-specific, page 8). Out-of-market risk disclosed as true. Implementation and timing are stated at solution level and individual recommendation level.

    File:     Evidence retrieved comprises provider terms and conditions (Aviva Pension Portfolio Terms and Conditions, Key Features), fund factsheets, and planning scenario documents. The Aviva terms state at [P1] that 'If You want to cancel Your regular income payments once they have started, then Your Adviser will need to do so at least 10 working days before Your existing regular income payment date.' Terms at [P5] specify adviser charges are deducted from Payment(s) before investment or from Pension Portfolio depending on type. Terms at [P7] state 'Any instruction to withdraw Your Investment will not take place on a non Business Day and will wait until the next Business Day. This could mean that You may not receive Your withdrawal proceeds straight away.' Terms at [P3] address fund closure: 'We will, where possible, notify Your Adviser or You and request reinvestment instructions 30 days in advance.' The Scottish Widows policy info [P10] notes 'Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission.' No evidence retrieved specifies timescales for transfer execution, implementation responsibility allocation across multiple recommendations, or confirmation of 'Adviser' as the identified responsible party for each discrete action.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states timescale '10-15 working days' applies to recommendations 1-5 (Transfer, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch) but evidence does not corroborate this specific timescale for these actions; Aviva terms reference '10 working days' only in context of cancelling regular income payments, not for initial transfer or encashment

      - Report identifies 'Adviser' as responsible party for all six recommendations but evidence does not specify adviser responsibility for all discrete actions, particularly for Rebalance or for actions dependent on provider-initiated processes

      - Report states next step requires completion of beneficiaries nomination form 'after the transfer process is begun' but evidence does not specify when this action should occur relative to transfer completion or whether adviser or client is responsible for obtaining and submitting the form

      - Report asserts client 'are comfortable' with out-of-market risk based on possession of liquid cash, but no evidence in the file corroborates that the client was asked about or confirmed comfort with this risk

      - Report at page 8 quotes disclosure of stock market movements but does not cite evidence showing client agreement to bear this risk or that liquid cash holdings were assessed as sufficient buffer

    The report states timescales and responsibility for implementation but the evidence base does not include the suitability report itself or client correspondence confirming these allocations. The Aviva provider terms and conditions ([P1], [P5], [P7]) govern certain operational timescales (e.g., 10 working days for cancelling regular payments, non-Business Day delays) but do not address the adviser's committed timescale for the initial transfer and encashment actions recommended. The Scottish Widows policy info ([P10]) introduces a constraint on ongoing adviser charging for Money4Life Plans (ZU plans), which may affect adviser responsibility allocation but is not cross-referenced in the report's implementation section. The report asserts Adviser responsibility uniformly across all six recommendations, including a Rebalance action, but the evidence does not confirm who is responsible for initiating rebalancing or whether this is delegated to a discretionary investment model (DIM). The next steps regarding beneficiaries nomination and cancellation review are stated in the report, but the evidence does not confirm the timing relative to transfer completion or the allocation of responsibility between adviser and provider. The out-of-market risk disclosure references client comfort based on liquid cash, but no evidence in the retrieved file demonstrates that the client was specifically questioned on, or explicitly agreed to, this risk. The report's language ('You are comfortable with this concept') is assertive but not corroborated by client confirmation in the evidence. These gaps mean the report states what action will be taken and by whom, but the evidence does not systematically corroborate these assertions or demonstrate that they were agreed with the client.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Fund charge basis ambiguity: Report states fund charges separately (0.18% and 0.29%) but provides a weighted average of 0.23% in the illustration [P8], yet the total charge table states 0.44% which should equal 0.21% + 0.22% + weighted fund charges; arithmetic does not reconcile to single fund allocation. Charge calculation mismatch: Platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value of £110,185.71; investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45; solution total £484.81 at 0.44% implies £110,184.09. None match the existing arrangements total value of £116,998.47 or stated transfer value of £110,185, creating arithmetic impossibility. Transfer value discrepancy: Derived figures computed from canonical model show existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47, but charges are calculated against approximately £110,185, a difference of £6,813.47 that is not explained in the report. 17 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report discloses platform charge 0.21%, investment management charge 0.22%, fund charges of 0.18% (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc) and 0.29% (CT Universal MAP Balanced C), with a total ongoing charge of 0.44% pa. The report states an initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55). Transaction costs of underlying funds are excluded with the explanation that they are variable, subject to change based on market conditions and trading activity, and are not fees paid to asset manager but include broker commissions, taxes and cost of executing trades. The report asserts these costs are already accounted for in fund performance figures presented net of all charges. An aggregated total ongoing charge of 0.44% is disclosed. The report includes a cumulative effect disclosure in a personal illustration showing how charges reduce investment returns over time.

    File:     [P3] shows the Aviva Platform Personal Pension with platform 0.21% (£231.39), investment management 0.22% (£253.43), and total charge 0.44% (£484.81). The report states transaction costs are excluded with reasons. [P8] Aviva Illustration shows platform charge 0.21%, investment charges for CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc at 0.29% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 0.18%, with a weighted average charge of 0.23%. [P1] and [P37] KIID documents for the funds show HSBC at 0.18% ongoing charge and CT Universal MAP at 0.29% ongoing charge. [P17] states initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) payable directly by pension schemes on transfer. [P3] contains the same transaction cost exclusion language as the report. [P33] Aviva Illustration shows the cumulative effect of charges on projected values. Derived figures show platform charge 0.21% on £231.39 implies fund value £110,185.71; investment management 0.22% on £253.43 implies £115,195.45; total 0.44% implies £110,184.09. None of these match the existing arrangements totals (£116,998.47) nor the transfer value stated (£110,185).

    Discrepancies:

      - Fund charge basis ambiguity: Report states fund charges separately (0.18% and 0.29%) but provides a weighted average of 0.23% in the illustration [P8], yet the total charge table states 0.44% which should equal 0.21% + 0.22% + weighted fund charges; arithmetic does not reconcile to single fund allocation

      - Charge calculation mismatch: Platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value of £110,185.71; investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45; solution total £484.81 at 0.44% implies £110,184.09. None match the existing arrangements total value of £116,998.47 or stated transfer value of £110,185, creating arithmetic impossibility

      - Transfer value discrepancy: Derived figures computed from canonical model show existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47, but charges are calculated against approximately £110,185, a difference of £6,813.47 that is not explained in the report

      - Fund allocation and charge reconciliation: The report table [P3] shows total charge 0.44% but this should equal 0.21% + 0.22% + blended fund charges; the individual fund charges (0.18% and 0.29% at 50/50 allocation = 0.235%) sum to 0.21% + 0.22% + 0.235% = 0.665%, not 0.44%

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Clarification of the fund value used to calculate the platform (£231.39) and investment management (£253.43) charges, as these do not reconcile to any stated arrangement value or portfolio total, Explanation of why the total charge is stated as 0.44% when the component charges (platform 0.21% + investment management 0.22% + fund charges averaging 0.235%) should total 0.665%, Confirmation of whether charges disclosed in the report are calculated on the £110,185 transfer value or the £116,998.47 total existing fund value, and reconciliation of the £6,813.47 discrepancy, Explicit confirmation of the fund allocation percentages (50% HSBC at 0.18% and 50% CT Universal MAP at 0.29%) and how these were used to derive the stated 0.44% total

    The report discloses material charge layers (platform, investment management, fund, and initial advice fee) and states an exclusion for transaction costs with reasons. However, a critical arithmetic inconsistency prevents validation of the disclosure completeness and accuracy. The canonical model shows platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% and investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22%, which mathematically imply fund values of £110,186 and £115,195 respectively—neither of which matches the existing arrangements total (£116,998.47) or any other stated figure. The stated total charge of 0.44% cannot be reconciled: if fund charges are 0.18% and 0.29% on a 50/50 allocation, the blended fund charge is 0.235%, making the true total 0.21% + 0.22% + 0.235% = 0.665%. The report's assertion of a 0.44% all-in charge is arithmetically impossible given the component percentages disclosed. This is not a rounding difference—it is a structural contradiction. The charges appear to have been calculated on different fund values at different times, or the fund allocation differs from what is stated. The cumulative effect is disclosed in illustrations [P8] and [P33], but the underlying charge figures on which those illustrations rest are internally inconsistent. No guard (such as different calculation methods between report and illustration) has been disclosed to explain this discrepancy. The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the figures the report asserts do not internally cohere. The evidence from KIIDs [P1] and [P37] supports the individual fund charges (0.18% and 0.29%), and [P3] confirms the platform (0.21%) and investment management (0.22%) figures, but the reconciliation to a stated total of 0.44% fails.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Initial fee: 3% of amount invested, stated as £3,305.55 with current transfer values, paid directly by pension schemes on transfer. Ongoing fee: 1% per annum of fund value, stated as circa £1,101.85 for the first year, paid via Aviva Platform pension scheme.

    File:     Client Agreement [P12] states 'The fees you are required to pay for our advisory services are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation.' For investments, 'A fee based on the work we do to arrange the initial investment, and a fee based upon any ongoing reviews.' Standard terms [P13] state initial advice on pensions is '3% of the value of the funds that we advise on' with minimum £1,350, and for regular contributions '30% of the first year's payments'. Ongoing service [P3] states 'we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1%' (with minimum £100 per annum). Suitability report [P9] confirms 'initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested' and [P5] confirms 'The costs for this service are 1% per annum.'

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states initial fee of £3,305.55 but does not reference the minimum fee of £1,350 stated in the standard terms [P13], creating ambiguity about whether the minimum applies or has been exceeded

      - Report calculates ongoing fee as £1,101.85 based on £110,184.09 implied fund value at 1%, but the derived figures show this fund value does not match any existing arrangement value (total existing arrangements are £116,998.47) and the comparison is therefore made on an unstated and unverified basis

      - The ongoing fee of 1% is stated to apply but the report does not disclose the £100 per annum minimum stated in [P3] and [P13]

    The report asserts an initial fee of 3% and an ongoing fee of 1%, both of which match the percentage rates stated in the client agreement and standard terms [P3], [P12], [P13]. The percentages are consistent. However, there are material concerns about completeness and arithmetic integrity. First, the initial fee amount of £3,305.55 is stated as 'with current transfer values' but the underlying fund value is not disclosed in the suitability report itself. The derived figures show the 3% fee implies a base of £110,185, which does not match the total existing transfer value of £116,998.47. This 6.5% shortfall is material and unexplained in the report. Second, the ongoing fee calculation similarly rests on a £110,184.09 fund value that does not correspond to any stated arrangement value. The report does not disclose or explain why the fee is calculated on this value rather than the total transfer value. Third, the standard terms [P13] establish a minimum initial fee of £1,350 for pension advice; the report does not disclose this floor or confirm it does not apply. Fourth, the ongoing charge is subject to a £100 per annum minimum per [P3]; the report does not state whether this applies. The report satisfies the percentage rates in the agreement but fails to disclose key conditions (minimums), fails to reconcile the fee bases to stated plan values, and does not explain the arithmetic underpinning the monetary amounts. The client agreement [P12] requires confirmation 'by approving them expressly or by written consent', and the report presents the fees as though agreed, but without explaining the basis on which they are calculated, the client cannot meaningfully confirm them. This is a structural gap in the disclosure of fees 'consistent with the client agreement'.

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P3)

        "For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1% (with a minimum fee of £100 per annum)."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P5)

        "The costs for this service are 1% per annum. Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P9)

        "For researching, recommending and arranging these investments, we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement. In monetary terms this is £3,305.55 with current transfer values, this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values. This will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer."

      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md [A] (P12)

        "The fees you are required to pay for our advisory services are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation."

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P13)

        "There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on. In order to ensure we are able to consistently provide a high standard of professional service, our minimum initial advice fee for Pensions & Investment related advice is £1,350."

 

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report asserts Reduction in Yield for recommended solution at: Low growth 2.1%, Mid growth 2.2%, High growth 2.3%. Critical Yield at: Low 1.9%, Mid 4.8%, High 7.8%. Effect of switching amounts to: Low £10,400 (9.6%), Mid £12,000 (9.4%), High £15,000 (10.0%). These represent cumulative impact at age 75. Report states charges on Aviva Platform Personal Pension are 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total, equating to £484.81 annually. Report includes ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum and initial adviser charge of £3,305. Report states in mid-growth scenario: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan.' Report claims recommended funds have past performance supporting achievability of this critical yield.

    File:     Evidence [P1] corroborates the RIY and effect of switching figures exactly: Low 2.1% RIY, 1.9% critical yield, £10,400 (9.6%) effect; Mid 2.2% RIY, 4.8% critical yield, £12,000 (9.4%) effect; High 2.3% RIY, 7.8% critical yield, £15,000 (10.0%) effect. Evidence [P3] Aviva illustration confirms projected values at mid growth (2.9%): £115,000 recommended vs £127,000 existing. Evidence [P18] confirms Aviva platform charge 0.21%, investment charges shown as CT Universal MAP 0.29% and HSBC 0.18%, with weighted average 0.23% (not the 0.22% stated). Evidence [P11] confirms mid-growth rate 2.94% and critical yield 4.8%. Evidence [P17] confirms adviser charges: 1% ongoing ('circa £1,101.85 for first year') and initial fee paid via platform. Evidence [P19] documents the charge comparison table from suitability report showing Aviva 0.44% total (0.21% + 0.23% rounded, not 0.22%). Evidence [P22] pension switch report repeats the same RIY, critical yield and effect of switching figures.

    Discrepancies:

      - Investment management charge stated as 0.22% in report [P19] but evidence [P18] shows weighted average fund charges of 0.23% (CT Universal MAP 0.29% + HSBC 0.18% blended 50/50). Discrepancy of 0.01 percentage point.

      - Report [P19] calculates total charge as 0.44% (0.21% + 0.22%), but if investment charge is 0.23% then blended total should be 0.44% (0.21% + 0.23%), making the component breakdown inconsistent with the stated total.

      - Report provides cumulative impact via RIY and effect of switching figures but does not quantify or explain the adviser charges' contribution separately to the overall cumulative cost impact at different growth rates.

      - No document reconciles the effect of switching (£10,400–£15,000) with the combined ongoing platform, investment, and adviser charges over the accumulation period to age 75, leaving the constituent elements of cumulative cost unclear.

    The report's disclosure of cumulative impact through RIY, critical yield and effect of switching is well-supported by evidence. All three growth scenarios match [P1] exactly. The mid-growth critical yield of 4.8% is corroborated by [P11] and supported by performance analysis in [P9]. The charge component breakdown (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment = 0.44% total) is materially accurate: evidence [P18] shows the weighted average of stated fund charges is 0.23%, not 0.22%, a minor discrepancy of 1 basis point within rounding tolerance for blended funds. The report's statement that the recommendation 'includes our advice fees' in the 4.8% critical yield is supported by [P11] and [P17]. However, the report does not explicitly break down how much of the effect of switching (£10,400–£15,000) is attributable to adviser charges (initial £3,305 plus ongoing 1%) versus ongoing product and platform charges. Evidence [P1] and [P22] show the effect of switching is calculated on a consolidated basis assuming the same growth rate, but the adviser charges are noted separately in [P11] and [P17] as having reduced the initial investment and affecting ongoing projections. The suitability report does not explain the specific benefits expected in exchange for the higher costs incurred by switching. Evidence [P9] provides performance comparison showing the recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio has outperformed the existing Aviva plan over 5 years, which implicitly supports the achievability claim in [P11], but no document explicitly links the cost increase to expected benefits in terms of fund manager expertise, platform functionality, or consolidation advantages. The cumulative impact disclosure is complete but the value proposition supporting the cost increase is implied rather than explicitly stated and justified.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states on page 11: 'our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'This means that your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year (in monetary terms).' The report presents a per-arrangement comparison table showing: Aviva (EA3) existing charge 0.50%, new impact -0.06%, Zurich (EA4) existing 0.93%, impact -0.49%, and Standard Life (EA5) existing 0.18%, impact +0.26%. The report also states on page 23 an initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) will be paid directly by pension schemes on transfer, and describes Aviva benefits including financial strength, choice of funds, expertise, value with transparent charges, and convenience.

    File:     [P1] shows the existing arrangements table with five pensions: People's Pension 0.50% fund charge, Scottish Widows 0.19% fund and 0.10% platform, Aviva 0.50% fund charge, Zurich 0.18% fund charge, and Standard Life 0.52% fund charge. [P3] shows the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension with platform charge 0.21% (£231.39) and investment management 0.22% (£253.43), totalling 0.44% (£484.81). [P3] also shows a 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' table comparing existing to recommended charges. [P24] shows fund research for CT Universal MAP Balanced at 0.29% ongoing costs and 0.26% transaction costs (total 0.55%), and HSBC at implied costs. [P21] shows projected values at age 75: existing pension £127,000 vs new solution £115,000 at mid growth, a £12,000 decrease (9.4%). [P13] confirms the pension switch report shows effect of switching £3,000 or 2.1% decrease. [P35] shows the Aviva illustration with platform charge 0.21% and investment charges 0.29% (CT Universal MAP) and 0.18% (HSBC), weighted average 0.23%.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states overall comparison -0.24% on page 11; derived figures show solution total ongoing at 0.44% on £110,185 basis, but existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47, making any like-for-like percentage comparison on different fund values arithmetically impossible.

      - Report states Aviva (EA3) existing charge 0.50% with amount £18.72; this £18.72 at 0.50% implies fund value £3,744.00, which is the Aviva arrangement value, not EA3 which should be applied to the correct plan.

      - Report states EA5 (Standard Life) existing charge 0.18% with amount £186.19; this £186.19 at 0.18% implies fund value £103,438.89, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002, showing the row is computed on the wrong plan.

      - Report states EA5 impact +0.26% with amount £268.94; this £268.94 at 0.26% implies fund value £103,438.46, again Zurich's value, not Standard Life's, confirming misaligned computation.

      - Report's stated overall comparison difference of -0.24% does not reconcile with per-arrangement impacts: EA3 -0.06%, EA4 -0.49%, EA5 +0.26%; sum of percentage impacts on different bases cannot produce a single -0.24% for the whole portfolio without weighting, which is not disclosed.

      - Report states monetary difference £264 is 'in the first year'; derived figure shows £484.81 ongoing charge for recommended solution but no corresponding aggregated existing charge is stated, making the £264 figure's basis unclear.

      - Report claims Aviva plan is 'less expensive' (direction RecommendedCheaper) yet [P21] pension switch report shows projected value at age 75 is £12,000 lower (9.4% decrease) for the new solution, indicating the cost advantage does not translate to projected benefit.

      - Report provides initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) but does not aggregate this with ongoing charges in the cost comparison, potentially understating the true cost of the recommendation.

      - Report's research cites fund charges (CT Universal MAP 0.29%, HSBC 0.18% from [P24] and [P35]) but the comparison table on page 11 does not show how individual fund charges contribute to the 0.44% total platform charge stated.

      - Report states on page 11 comparison table that Zurich existing charge is 0.93%, but [P1] shows only 0.18% fund charge; the 0.93% does not appear in the existing arrangements table, creating an internal inconsistency.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: No aggregated total existing charge across all five arrangements is stated or calculated in the report, making the basis of the -0.24% overall comparison indeterminate., The fund-level charges from the investment illustrations are not mapped to the recommended Aviva solution charges; no documentation links the 0.29% and 0.18% fund charges to the 0.44% platform total., No breakdown of how the recommended platform charge (0.21%) and investment management charge (0.22%) relate to the underlying fund charges is provided., The per-arrangement comparison rows (EA3, EA4, EA5) use charge percentages that do not appear in [P1] existing arrangements table (0.93% for Zurich, 0.52% for Standard Life shown in report but not in evidence), and the Standard Life row (EA5) is demonstrably computed on Zurich's fund value, making those rows unreliable., Evidence of the service proposition attached to ongoing adviser charges or the value received for the 0.21% platform charge is absent., No client agreement or fee schedule is provided to verify the 3% initial advice fee or ongoing service fee.

    The cost comparison cannot be performed reliably. Three critical failures prevent validation: (1) Arithmetic failure: rows EA5 (existing and impact) are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438), not Standard Life's (£3,002), as proven by the arithmetic. Derived figures confirm £186.19 at 0.18% = £103,438.89 and £268.94 at 0.26% = £103,438.46, both Zurich, not Standard Life. This makes the entire comparison table unreliable because one of three per-arrangement rows is demonstrably wrong. (2) Basis mismatch: the report's stated overall charge of -0.24% appears to be a like-for-like comparison, but existing arrangements total £116,998.47 while the recommended solution uses £110,185 as its basis (per derived figures), a £6,813.47 difference that makes any single percentage meaningful only if both sides are on the same fund value. The report does not disclose that it is comparing charges on different portfolio values. (3) Aggregation absent: no document provided shows how the five existing arrangements' charges aggregate to produce a single starting percentage against which -0.24% is the difference. The comparison states it is on a 'LikeForLike' basis but provides no evidence that this basis was established. The fee structure is incomplete: the 3% initial fee (£3,305.55) is disclosed but not added to the ongoing costs in the comparison. The projected value data in [P21] shows the new solution at £115,000 vs existing at £127,000 (£12,000 or 9.4% lower), which directly contradicts the claim that the Aviva plan is 'less expensive' in any meaningful sense for the client, as lower ongoing charges have not translated to better outcomes. The vulnerability overlay: no special communication needs or vulnerability factors are recorded in the evidence that would affect the clarity or complexity of this cost comparison, so the standard expectation for clear, aggregated cost disclosure applies.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts seven material benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower overall cost structure compared to existing pension, meaning more savings remain invested for growth [P1, page 7]; (2) Lifestyling funds automatically reduce investment risk as approaching retirement, eventually moving portfolio outside agreed risk range [P1, page 7]; (3) Greater Investment Choice allowing more tailored strategy suited to risk profile with scope for future adjustments [P1, page 7]; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation allowing direct deduction of charges from plan without separate payments [P1, page 7]; (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments by adviser to ensure alignment with changing needs and market conditions [P1, page 7]; (6) Multi-asset fund selection providing professionally managed diversified investment solution aligned with financial goals and risk profile [P1, page 12]; (7) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility providing more flexible retirement options for tax-efficient withdrawal strategies [P1, page 7]. The report states recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings [P1, page 13], and that Aviva platform funds align with agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility [P1, page 13]. The report claims based on past performance of recommended funds, the plan is believed achievable to outperform by critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 [P1, page 11], stated as 'I do believe that this is achievable' [P1, page 13].

    File:     Evidence shows: (1) Fund recommendation table [P6] lists HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc at 50% with 0.18% annual charge and CT Universal MAP Balanced C at 50% with 0.29% annual charge. Research report [P3] confirms these funds' performance data. (2) Existing Aviva arrangement shows 0.50% fund charge [P1, page 5 table]. Performance comparison chart [P1, page 13] shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) achieved 37.25% over 3 years and 15.58% over 5 years as at 13/10/2025, versus existing Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation (A) at 22.89% over 3 years and 15.58% over 5 years. (3) Zurich Managed Pn AP (D) achieved 34.56% over 3 years and 44.32% over 5 years, outperforming recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) which achieved 37.25% over 3 years and 15.58% over 5 years. (4) Report states 'However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' [P1, page 13]. (5) Volatility analysis [P1, page 13] shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) volatility of 7.56% versus Zurich Managed Pn AP (A) volatility of 7.89%. (6) Discrete performance table for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio shows 2024 return of 10.82% and YTD 7.7% [P3, P5]. CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund shows 2024 return of 11.27% and YTD 8.04% [P3, P5]. (7) Fact Find [P10, P11] shows client net monthly income £1,430 against monthly expenditure £1,718, creating deficit of £288 [P14]. (8) Intelliflo proposed scenario [P23] lists People's Pension at £6,601, Scottish Widows at £211, Aviva at £3,744.36, Zurich at £103,439.24, Standard Life at £3,002. (9) Report page 5 table [P1] shows five existing arrangements totalling £116,998.47. Report page 9 shows recommended transfer value of £110,185. Shortfall of £6,813.47 is unexplained.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts 'Lower Costs' as key benefit but existing Aviva charge is 0.50% and recommended Aviva Platform shows 0.44% combined charge (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management per charge arithmetic). Text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which contradicts the stated cost saving benefit.

      - Report claims recommended Aviva advised funds 'have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings' [P1, page 13] but cumulative performance at 13/10/2025 shows Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) and existing Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation (A) both at 15.58% over 5 years (identical, not stronger).

      - Report states recommended funds 'have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' [P1, page 13], yet recommends consolidating Zurich (£103,439.24) into lower-performing Aviva. Zurich achieved 44.32% over 5 years versus recommended Aviva at 15.58%.

      - Report claims benefit of outperformance at critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 [P1, page 11] as 'achievable' based on 'past performance of your recommended funds' but provides no performance data reconciling to this specific rate. Charge arithmetic implies fund value of £110,188.68 at 4.24% but no arrangement equals this amount.

      - Report transfer value shows £110,185 on page 9 but sum of five existing arrangements equals £116,998.47 (£6,813.47 shortfall unexplained).

      - Report page 5 table shows Zurich arrangement with 0.18% fund charge plus £2.12 per month admin, but charge arithmetic for EA5 and EA5 impact rows compute on Zurich Managed Pn AP (£103,438.46) not on SL at retirement universal pension (£3,002), creating unreliable comparison basis.

    The report's core claim of 'Lower Costs' as a benefit is contradicted by its own charge figures. The recommended Aviva platform combined charge of 0.44% is lower than the existing Aviva 0.50%, supporting a modest cost saving only for that arrangement. However, the consolidation includes Zurich at 0.93% (£961.98 on £103,438.71), Scottish Widows at 0.29%, and Aviva at 0.50%, with a blended existing charge substantially higher than the flat recommended 0.44%. The report's statement that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' directly contradicts the stated benefit. The performance justification fails on two grounds: (1) the recommended Aviva Risk 5 funds matched (not exceeded) the existing Aviva Pension My Future Focus performance at 15.58% over 5 years, contradicting the claim of 'stronger performance'; (2) the Zurich Managed Pn AP at 44.32% over 5 years substantially outperformed both, yet consolidation into lower-performing Aviva is recommended. The report acknowledges this: 'However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.' The critical yield assertion of 4.8% annually is stated as 'achievable' without supporting analysis. The passage cited [P1, page 13] contains only the statement 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' — a claim unsupported by performance reconciliation to the 4.8% rate. Per the guard 'An assertion that required outperformance is achievable is a claim, not an analysis; require supporting performance data before accepting it.' The transfer value discrepancy (£110,185 recommended vs £116,998.47 sum of existing) is material and unexplained, suggesting either missing funds or undisclosed reductions. The charge arithmetic for EA5 rows uses Zurich Managed Pn AP fund value (£103,438.46) rather than the SL fund value (£3,002), making any comparison resting on those rows unreliable. The client's financial position [P14] shows monthly deficit of £288, yet the report does not address how ongoing adviser charging (implied by 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' benefit) will be funded from this negative cashflow. Vulnerability: The client is an HGV driver earning £300 per week net, with variable income noted as 'typically' [P1, page 3] not deterministic. This income variability is not reflected in the suitability analysis, and ongoing adviser fees on a volatile income base are not addressed.
