  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended solution is justified by: (1) 'Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable' regarding a critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75; (2) 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings'; (3) 'the Aviva platform funds selected for you align with your agreed risk tolerance and have delivered comparable performance with reduced volatility'; (4) ongoing review services including attitude to risk assessment, investment suitability review, market updates, and access to professional advisers. The report also states 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (repeated three times). The report identifies the recommended solution as 'RecommendedCheaper' in its cost comparison direction. Page 18 projects values at age 75: Low growth £97,600, Mid growth £115,000, High growth £135,000.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1) shows that at mid growth rate (2.95%), the existing pension projects to £127,000 versus the new solution at £125,000 - a reduction of £2,000 or 1.9% decrease. At high growth rate (5.88%), existing pension projects to £150,000 versus new solution at £147,000 - a reduction of £3,000 or 2.0% decrease. The critical yield required for the new solution to match existing outcomes is shown as 3.2% (mid growth) and 6.2% (high growth). The pension switch report (P1) also shows that the existing solution ranks 2nd (£127,000) in a comparison of pension products, while the new solution ranks 5th (£125,000). The SIPP research report (P13) shows MiFID II costs: combined fund costs total £406.74 (0.37%), with CT Universal MAP at 0.55% (£304.82) and HSBC at 0.19% (£101.92). The report states there is an initial adviser charge of £3,305.00 and ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%. The suitability report (P19) states the Aviva Platform Personal Pension total charge is 0.44% (£484.81 annually), comprising platform charge 0.21% (£231.39) and investment management 0.22% (£253.43).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report projects Mid growth value of £115,000 at age 75; file evidence shows existing pension projects £127,000 and new solution £125,000 at same growth rate - a variance of £10,000-£12,000 from file projections.

      - Report projects High growth value of £135,000 at age 75; file evidence shows existing pension projects £150,000 and new solution £147,000 - a variance of £12,000-£15,000 from file projections.

      - Report asserts recommended funds have 'demonstrated stronger performance compared to existing Aviva holdings' and that new solution is 'RecommendedCheaper'; file evidence shows new solution ranks 5th among alternatives with £125,000 maturity value versus existing Zurich at 1st (£128,000) and existing solution at 2nd (£127,000).

      - Report states critical yield needed is 4.8% per year; file evidence (P1) shows critical yield of 3.2% for mid growth and 6.2% for high growth scenarios, with no mention of 4.8% figure.

      - Report projects £115,000 mid-growth outcome; this figure does not match the £110,185 transfer value stated elsewhere or either the £127,000 (existing) or £125,000 (new solution) shown in pension switch report.

      - Report claims new solution delivers 'reduced volatility'; file evidence shows 'Reduction in yield' of 0.6% for new solution versus 0.3% for existing solution at mid growth - indicating higher yield drag, not lower volatility.

    The report asserts cost justification and performance expectations that are contradicted by the supporting evidence on multiple material points. First, the projected maturity values in the report (£115,000 mid, £135,000 high) do not reconcile with the pension switch report projections (£125,000 new solution mid, £147,000 high). Second, the report claims the recommended funds have demonstrated stronger performance and that the solution is cheaper, yet the pension switch report ranks the new solution 5th with a lower maturity value (£125,000) than both the existing solution (£127,000, ranked 2nd) and Zurich (£128,000, ranked 1st). Third, the report states a critical yield of 4.8% is 'achievable' but provides no evidence or calculation showing this figure. The pension switch report shows the critical yield required is 3.2% to 6.2% depending on growth scenario, not a flat 4.8%. Fourth, the report claims the new solution delivers 'comparable performance with reduced volatility', yet the evidence shows the reduction in yield (charge impact) is 0.6% for the new solution versus 0.3% for the existing - indicating higher charge drag not lower volatility. The report does disclose that 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' (repeated), and it does reference ongoing services and review, but these generic justifications do not connect to the specific cost increases or outcome reductions evident in the comparator data. The pension switch report shows the new solution is more expensive in terms of outcome (lower maturity value) and requires higher critical yields to match existing outcomes, yet the report presents it as the justified recommendation based on performance and cost grounds that the evidence does not support.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Report discloses three material disadvantages: (1) Critical yield requirement of 4.8% per year to age 75 to meet current plan value; (2) Aviva plan 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in first year; (3) Lifestyling drift—funds currently in lifestyling will move outside agreed risk range. Two limitations disclosed: (1) Transfer would limit withdrawal flexibility, preventing flexible access; (2) Scottish Widows risk rating 5 constraint requiring two-fund combination with 20-30% in cash/money market. Product-specific risks stated: (1) Access delays of 10-15 working days; (2) Market timing risk during disinvestment and reinvestment. Generic delegated risk: 'All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature.'

    File:     Evidence contains multiple pension switch comparison tables (P1, P2, P3, P10, P14) showing critical yield, reduction in yield, effect of switching, and projected values at different growth rates. P1 shows 6.3% critical yield and £3,000 (2.1%) decrease effect. P3 shows critical yields ranging 0.3% to 6.2% across growth scenarios. P10 shows critical yield of 4.8% at mid growth with £12,000 (9.4%) decrease. Standard Life policy info (P15) notes Differential Fund Discount and leaver discount 0.48%. Zurich policy info (P8, P24, P25) discloses exit charges reducing 0.3%-0.4% monthly to zero at SRA, capped at 1% from age 55. Standard Life (P23) states plan does not facilitate UFPLS but does facilitate drawdown. P11 and P18 contain generic fund risks including counterparty, credit, default, derivative risks, currency risk, and property asset risks. KIIDs (P19) notes fund not appropriate for withdrawal within 5 years and discusses tax position variability. Aviva Key Features (P20) describes retirement options including lump sum, drawdown, and annuity. P8 notes loss of life cover and waiver benefits on new pension; old plans' benefits no longer available on new plans; medical questionnaire required if client health deteriorated. SIPP research (P12) discusses agreed risk level 5 'Balanced' without strong risk aversion.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states 4.8% critical yield requirement but evidence shows variable critical yields: P1 shows 6.3%, P3 shows ranging 0.3%-6.2%, P10 shows 4.8% at mid growth but also 1.9%, 3.2%, 7.8% across scenarios—no single 4.8% figure for age 75 comparison emerges clearly from evidence

      - Report asserts 4.24% cost increase but evidence in P2 and P10 shows reduction in yield of 0.3% existing vs 0.6-2.3% new solution, not a single 4.24% comparative cost metric across all plans

      - Report discloses withdrawal restrictions on Scottish Widows but evidence (P23) states Standard Life plan 'does facilitate drawdown' whereas recommendation moves to Aviva SIPP—report's characterization of withdrawal limitation may apply to different provider than recommended solution

      - Report mentions lifestyling drift risk but evidence does not identify which existing arrangements use lifestyling funds; fact find (P28) has blank fields for lifestyling strategy, leaving uncertainty about whether all or some existing pensions use this approach

      - Report states loss of life cover and waiver benefits on Zurich plan but evidence (P8, P24) shows these benefits 'no longer available on new pension plans' generically—does not confirm whether client's existing Zurich plan held such benefits that would actually be lost

      - Report does not disclose exit charges on Zurich plan despite evidence (P24, P25) stating 'If this plan has capital units, there will be an exit charge applied if the fund is transferred...reduces by around 0.3%-0.4% a month until SRA where there is no charge. The charge is capped at 1% from age 55'—this is material cost not mentioned in suitability report

      - Report does not disclose that Standard Life arrangement offers Differential Fund Discount (P15) as a benefit being foregone; evidence states 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer'

      - Report discloses generic product risks but does not reference specific fund risks documented in KIIDs (P18) such as counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, or currency risk on recommended funds

      - Report does not disclose that recommended Aviva SIPP has platform structure (Aviva Platform) with specific charges separate from fund charges, whereas some existing pensions have no platform

    The report discloses material disadvantages and product-specific risks but with significant gaps and inconsistencies against supporting evidence. The critical yield assertion of 4.8% is stated for age 75 but evidence tables show the critical yield varies substantially across growth scenarios (0.3% to 7.8% depending on growth rate assumption) and across different existing pension comparisons. The report selects one figure (4.8% at mid growth for overall consolidation) but fails to disclose the full range, potentially understating risk under adverse scenarios. The 4.24% cost increase figure lacks direct corroboration in the evidence tables, which show reduction in yield metrics but not a consolidated cost comparison in percentage terms across all five existing pensions. The report's disclosure of withdrawal restrictions references Scottish Widows but the recommendation moves funds to Aviva; evidence shows Standard Life facilitates drawdown (P23), creating ambiguity about which provider's restrictions apply. Exit charges on the Zurich arrangement (0.3-0.4% monthly reduction capping at 1% from age 55) are entirely absent from the suitability report despite being material switching costs. The loss of Zurich life cover and waiver benefits is disclosed generically but evidence does not confirm whether client's specific Zurich plan held these benefits. Standard Life's Differential Fund Discount offering lower charges than other providers (P15) is not disclosed as a foregone benefit. Specific fund risks (counterparty, credit, default, derivatives, currency) documented in KIIDs (P18) are not referenced despite the recommendation identifying specific funds (HSBC, CT Universal MAP). The report's statement that lifestyling funds will move outside agreed risk range lacks identification of which existing pensions use this strategy; fact find has no completed entries confirming lifestyling is in place. The report's generic delegation to product literature ('All other risks...detailed in the product literature') is a limitation flag in the specification but occurs alongside selective product-specific disclosures, creating a pattern of incomplete risk communication. The evidence supports disclosure of critical yield (albeit with range, not single figure), cost impact, withdrawal constraints, and exit charges, but the report omits exit charges, fund-specific risks, and foregone benefits. The comparison can be performed because pension switch tables, policy documents, fund literature, and the suitability report are all present; the issue is selective omission and inconsistency, not missing data for comparison.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Report asserts Aviva My Future Focus RiskAlignment comparison: existing 'Risk level 2 - Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' vs recommended 'Risk level 5'. Evidence P18 shows Aviva My Future Focus is 'Lifestage Investment Approach' (auto-switching), not static Risk 2; P6 labels it '15.85%' performance, not a risk level. Contradiction in how existing risk level is characterized. Report states Zurich Charges '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin charge (0.93% total)' but derived figures show: £2.12/month = £25.44/year on £103,439.24 = 0.0246%, not enough to reach 0.93%. The 0.93% figure is computed from £961.98 annual charge / £103,438.71, implying the entire 0.93% is attributed to Zurich when it includes both Zurich and another arrangement's charges combined (per figure annotations, row computed on wrong plan). Report projects Zurich switch effect at mid-growth of -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease (P19), but assesses Zurich switch as 'Better' on charges. Switching from 0.3% reduction in yield (existing) to 0.6% reduction in yield (new) contradicts the 'Better' assessment when the new solution underperforms. 27 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts 12 comparison dimensions across 5 plans being switched: (1) Aviva existing 0.50% fund + £4.50 admin vs recommended 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment mgmt = 0.44%, assessed 'Better'; (2) Aviva FundRange 80+ vs 80+, 'Better'; (3) Aviva RiskAlignment Risk 9 vs Risk 5, 'Better'; (4) Aviva AdviserServicing No vs Yes, 'Better'; (5) Aviva RetirementOptions FAD/UFPLS vs FAD/UFPLS/FAD, 'Equivalent'; (6) Aviva Charges 0.50% vs 0.44% -0.06% -£2.25, 'Better'; (7) Aviva FundRange 80+ vs 80+, 'Better'; (8) Aviva (My Future Focus) RiskAlignment Risk 2 lifestyling vs Risk 5, 'Better'; (9) Aviva (My Future Focus) AdviserServicing No vs Yes, 'Better'; (10) Aviva (My Future Focus) RetirementOptions FAD/UFPLS vs FAD/UFPLS/FAD, 'Equivalent'; (11) Zurich Charges 0.18% fund + £2.12 admin (0.93% total) vs 0.44% -0.49% -£506.85, 'Better'; (12) Zurich FundRange 60+ vs 80+, 'Better'. Report also asserts Zurich RiskAlignment Risk 7 blended with Risk 2 vs Risk 5, 'Better'.

    File:     Evidence from P3, P4, P9, P17, P20 (Pension switch report) shows: (P3) Existing pensions vs New solution comparison with charges data; (P9) Stakeholder pension comparison showing Generic Stakeholder 1.6% charge vs New Solution 0.6%, Effect -£8,000 or -6.3%; (P4, P17, P20) New solution projected at 0.6%, existing solutions 0.2%-0.59%; (P1) Comparison table on retirement options, FAD, UFPLS, Annuity availability. Evidence from P16 (Suitability Report) shows: Aviva existing charges 0.50% fund + £4.50 annual admin; Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin (reported as 0.93% total); Standard Life 0.26% fund + £2.58 per month admin. Evidence from P12 shows new solution investments: CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 50%, both Risk level 5. P6 shows performance comparison: Zurich Managed AP 46.14%, Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 42.40%, IA Mixed 25.66%, Aviva My Future Focus 15.85%. P19 shows Zurich all-plans comparison: existing £127k vs new £115k mid-growth, effect -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease. P38 justifies Zurich switch citing no Risk 5 funds available, requiring blend of Risk 7 + Risk 2.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts Aviva My Future Focus RiskAlignment comparison: existing 'Risk level 2 - Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' vs recommended 'Risk level 5'. Evidence P18 shows Aviva My Future Focus is 'Lifestage Investment Approach' (auto-switching), not static Risk 2; P6 labels it '15.85%' performance, not a risk level. Contradiction in how existing risk level is characterized.

      - Report states Zurich Charges '0.18% fund + £2.12 per month admin charge (0.93% total)' but derived figures show: £2.12/month = £25.44/year on £103,439.24 = 0.0246%, not enough to reach 0.93%. The 0.93% figure is computed from £961.98 annual charge / £103,438.71, implying the entire 0.93% is attributed to Zurich when it includes both Zurich and another arrangement's charges combined (per figure annotations, row computed on wrong plan).

      - Report projects Zurich switch effect at mid-growth of -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease (P19), but assesses Zurich switch as 'Better' on charges. Switching from 0.3% reduction in yield (existing) to 0.6% reduction in yield (new) contradicts the 'Better' assessment when the new solution underperforms.

      - Report asserts Zurich FundRange 60+ vs 80+, 'Better', but evidence does not provide Zurich fund count; P1 shows Zurich in comparison table but does not specify fund availability number. No corroborating passage for the 60+ assertion.

      - Report statement 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (on Aviva My Future Focus, P7 in passage extract) contradicts the table showing -0.06% impact, implying new plan is 0.06% cheaper, not more expensive.

      - Derived figures show Zurich platform charge calculation (EA5 rows) computed on wrong plan: fund value £103,438 vs Standard Life actual value £3,002. Charge comparison for Zurich is therefore unreliable.

      - Report asserts Aviva (My Future Focus) RetirementOptions: existing 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS' vs recommended 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS; Flexi Access Drawdown', assessed 'Equivalent'. Evidence P1 shows both existing and new allow FAD and UFPLS; Flexi Access Drawdown is explicitly FAD, not an additional option. Categorization as 'Equivalent' overstates difference or mischaracterizes terminology.

    The report performs comparisons across multiple dimensions for five plans (People's Pension retained; Scottish Widows encashed; Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life switched). Comparisons are detailed and structured. However, multiple internal contradictions and charge calculation errors undermine reliability. The Zurich comparison is most problematic: (a) the 0.93% charge figure is computed from figures themselves marked as computed on the wrong plan (per derived figures annotation for EA5 rows); (b) the switch analysis shows a mid-growth effect of -£12,000 decrease (P19), yet the report assesses the switch as better on charges; (c) fund range asserted as 60+ but not evidenced. The Aviva My Future Focus comparison contains the contradiction 'plan is 0.06% more expensive' while table shows -0.06% (cheaper). The RiskAlignment assertion for My Future Focus as 'Risk 2' is simplified: P18 shows it is a Lifestage auto-switching fund, not a static Risk 2 fund. Retirement Options terminology conflates Flexi Access Drawdown (which is a form of FAD) with additional options. The Aviva charges comparison (0.50% vs 0.44%) is corroborated by P16 and P12, but the claimed 0.06% benefit for My Future Focus contradicts stated narrative. The switch rationale on grounds of cost savings is undermined when the derived effect-of-switching figures show materially adverse outcomes (-£12,000 for Zurich, -£10,400 for existing all-plans at low growth per P19). The report does not reconcile these contradictions: why recommend a switch that projects a £12,000 loss? No alternative to switching is documented in the comparisons (guard notes only that alternatives should be considered; P9 shows stakeholder pension was ranked but not qualitatively considered as alternative). The comparison presents Zurich's lack of Risk 5 funds as a negative, but the recommended blend (Risk 7 + Risk 2) is stated as inferior to Aviva options yet recommended anyway (P38). Overall, the comparison is asserted and structured but contains material inconsistencies between stated benefits, derived projections, and corroborating evidence.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits of switching: (1) Small pots can be withdrawn tax-efficiently with 25% tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate, netting £170 from a £200 fund. (2) Lower costs - the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure, with annual charge difference of -0.06% (saving £2.25 annually). (3) Lifestyling funds currently move portfolio outside agreed risk range; new scheme offers greater investment choice suited to risk profile. (4) Adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and multi-asset fund selection (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) providing risk level 5 profile unavailable in existing provider. (5) Enhanced retirement flexibility. (6) No exit penalties stated (£0). (7) Critical yield achievable: mid-growth 2.94% requires 4.8% annual growth to match existing plan value; based on past fund performance, this is stated as achievable. (8) Required outperformance: £4,672 in first year.

    File:     The pension switch report (P2-P7, P12-P15) provides detailed comparisons. For the three-plan switch (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life): existing pension reduction in yield 0.3%, new solution 0.6%; at mid-growth 2.95%, effect of switching is £2,000 or 1.6% decrease in maturity value (P6). For Zurich pension alone (P3): existing reduction in yield 0.3%, new solution 0.6%; critical yield 0.3%; effect of switching £2,000 or 2.0% decrease. For Standard Life alone (P2): existing 0.6%, new solution 0% at mid-growth; critical yield 6.0%; effect of switching £30 or 0.6% decrease. For People's Pension alone (small pot, £211.32 value per evidence): no specific comparison provided. Zurich policy info (P9) confirms no fund switch charge (£0.00) and no transfer penalties. No evidence corroborates the claimed -0.06% annual charge advantage or £2.25 annual saving. Charge arithmetic from report: Aviva existing 0.50%, new Aviva Platform 0.44% (text claims new plan 0.06% more expensive, contradicting calculation). The switch report shows new solution consistently has higher reduction in yield (0.6% vs 0.3%) across all comparisons, meaning new solution charges more, not less. No evidence provides tax calculation for small pot (25% tax-free, 75% income-taxed yielding £170 net from £200).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims annual charge difference of -0.06% (lower cost) but charge arithmetic shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs new 0.44%, which is 0.06% lower for new (not higher). However, text on page 6 states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution,' contradicting the arithmetic.

      - Report states £2.25 annual saving from charge difference but this figure does not match any value in the evidence pack.

      - Report claims lower overall cost structure and greater investment choice as switching benefits, but pension switch report (P6) shows 'Effect of switching' as £2,000 or 1.6% decrease in maturity value at mid-growth (2.95%), meaning the new solution underperforms the existing.

      - Report asserts critical yield of 4.8% is achievable based on past fund performance, but evidence (P12) for People's Pension shows critical yield 4.8% at mid-growth 2.95% with effect of switching -£12,000 or 9.4% decrease in projected value at age 75.

      - Report states lifestyling moves portfolio outside agreed risk range; new scheme offers greater investment choice. However, no evidence confirms that lifestyling in existing arrangements actually moves the portfolio outside the client's agreed risk rating of 5 (Balanced).

      - Report claims small pot withdrawal yields £170 net (25% tax-free, 75% taxed), but no evidence in the pack corroborates this tax treatment or calculation.

      - Report asserts multi-asset fund selection (HSBC 50%, CT Universal MAP 50%) provides risk level 5 profile unavailable in Zurich. Evidence (P7) confirms these two funds are recommended at 50/50 allocation but provides no comparison showing Zurich cannot deliver risk level 5.

      - Report states no exit penalties (£0). Evidence confirms Zurich (P9) has no fund switch charge and no transfer penalties stated, but does not explicitly confirm zero exit penalties for all five existing arrangements.

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Evidence of actual charge comparison between all five existing arrangements and recommended Aviva Platform solution - report asserts -0.06% annual charge advantage but figures in evidence show opposite or are absent, Corroboration of small pot tax treatment (25% tax-free, 75% income-taxed) and net receipt of £170 from £200 withdrawal, Evidence that existing arrangements' lifestyling mechanism moves portfolio outside client's agreed risk rating 5, Confirmation that existing providers (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life) cannot or do not offer risk level 5 multi-asset fund strategies, Exit penalties or transfer charges for Scottish Widows (£211.32) and People's Pension (£6,601.55) arrangements, Explicit comparison showing Zurich cannot deliver risk level 5 profile without blended funds, Evidence supporting the critical yield of 4.8% is achievable based on past fund performance of recommended funds

    The requirement asks whether replacement/switch advice is consistent with and supported by evidence on benefits and costs of switching. Critical findings: (1) Charge advantage contradiction: The report claims -0.06% annual charge difference (lower costs for new solution) on page 6, but the derived figures from the report's own values show Aviva existing at 0.50% and recommended Aviva Platform at 0.44%, representing a 0.06% advantage to the new solution. However, the pension switch report evidence (P6, P12) consistently shows the new solution has reduction in yield of 0.6% versus existing 0.3%, meaning higher total charges in the new solution. The report's statement that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' directly contradicts the arithmetic that should show it 0.06% cheaper. (2) Maturity value effect: The evidence (P6) for the three-plan switch shows 'Effect of switching' as £2,000 or 1.6% decrease, and for People's Pension alone (P12) shows £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. This means switching results in materially worse outcomes at mid-growth rates, contradicting the benefit claim of lower costs. (3) Small pot treatment: The report asserts tax-efficient withdrawal of £200 netting £170, with 25% tax-free and 75% taxed at marginal rate. This specific calculation is not evidenced in any document provided. (4) Critical yield: The report states required outperformance of £4,672 in year one and mid-growth requiring 4.8% annual return to match existing. Evidence in P12 shows this critical yield is indeed 4.8% for People's Pension, consistent with the report. However, whether this is 'achievable' based on past performance is not evidenced; no fund performance data is provided comparing recommended funds against this hurdle. (5) Lifestyling and risk drift: The report claims existing funds in lifestyling move portfolio outside agreed risk range. No evidence confirms whether existing lifestyling arrangements actually breach the client's agreed risk rating 5. (6) Missing charge comparisons: The £2.25 annual saving figure is not corroborated by any evidence. The charge analysis provided in the evidence (P15) does not isolate individual plan comparisons with stated annual savings. (7) Alternative arrangements: Evidence shows comparisons for Aviva-only switch (P3), Zurich-only switch (P3), Standard Life-only switch (P2), and three-plan switch (P6). These alternatives are considered in the evidence, addressing that guard. However, the report's claim that benefits outweigh higher costs is contradicted by the 'Effect of switching' showing consistent material decreases in projected maturity values. The comparison cannot be fully performed because the charge difference figures asserted by the report (-0.06%, £2.25) do not match or are contradicted by the evidence pack.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Employer contribution will be lost. Report states: 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' All five existing arrangements are stated to have no safeguarded benefits.

    File:     Evidence shows: People's Pension (P8) has ongoing 0.5% management charge with rebate dependent on pot size, with no mention of employer contributions. Scottish Widows SWMT (P10, P21, P24) has no current regular contributions being made (employer N/A per P21), confirms no guarantees attached (P10), and member has left company (P21). Aviva (P17, P32) states no life cover, no waiver of payment, no guarantees or GMP. Zurich (P1, P31) confirms no safeguarded benefits on transfer (P15). Standard Life (P4, P23) confirms no safeguarded benefits, no GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed fund (P4). All provider documents confirm absence of guarantees. No provider documentation identifies active employer contribution arrangements on any plan.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts employer contribution will be lost as a valuable benefit; evidence shows Scottish Widows SWMT plan explicitly states 'Employer - N/A' for current contribution rates (P21) and 'Has the member left company - Yes' (P21), indicating no active employer is making contributions to this plan.

      - Report claims five arrangements have safeguarded benefits absent (inferred as all having been addressed); evidence confirms this for most plans but the employer contribution claim contradicts the specific evidence that this particular plan has no active employer contributions.

    The report correctly identifies that none of the five existing arrangements contain safeguarded benefits, supported by explicit provider statements. However, the claim that 'employer contribution will be lost' requires scrutiny. The Scottish Widows SWMT plan (the only plan with clear employer scheme context as an occupational master trust) shows 'Employer - N/A' for current contributions and records that the member 'has left company'. This indicates there is no active employer contribution to lose on this arrangement. The other plans (People's Pension, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) are personal pensions or stakeholder arrangements with no evidence of employer contribution structures. The report's assertion of a lost employer contribution benefit appears to conflate the occupational nature of the Scottish Widows scheme with an ongoing employer contribution obligation, but the evidence contradicts this: the member has left the company and no current employer contributions are being made. The report should have clarified whether any plan retained ongoing employer contributions; the evidence suggests none do. The absence of safeguarded benefits is properly corroborated across all five arrangements by provider documentation.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts zero exit penalties on all ceding plans ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'), and claims net annual charge reductions ranging from -0.06% to -0.49% on switched arrangements. For the largest plan (Zurich, £103,439.24), the report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', implying a saving. The report projects maturity value increases from switching (e.g., Zurich mid-growth: £127,000 vs £115,000 retained, stated as £12,000 or 9.4% decrease effect), and cites benefits including lower costs, lifestyling correction, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring. For small pots (Scottish Widows £211.32), the report recommends encashment under small pots rules with estimated net receipt of £170 and states 'This would not detract from your financial retirement plan.' The report provides a critical yield for Zurich of 4.8% at mid-growth rate.

    File:     The pension switch report evidence [P7] shows for Zurich (the largest ceding plan at £110,185.60 existing value) at mid-growth rate (2.95%): existing pension projected value £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, creating a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in projected maturity value. Reduction in yield is 0.3% (existing) vs 2.2% (new solution). Critical yield required from new solution is 4.8%. At low growth (0.00%): existing £108,000 vs new £97,600 (£10,400 or 9.6% decrease). At high growth (5.88%): existing £150,000 vs new £135,000 (£15,000 or 10.0% decrease). [P1] shows overall position: new solution 0.6% charge with £117,000 maturity, retained arrangements at 0.29-0.59%. [P2] shows immediate switch impact: existing pension £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60, a £3,305 shortfall in death benefit. The evidence does not show a cost saving of 0.06% or 0.49% for any plan; instead it shows higher reduction in yield (RIY) in the new solution across all growth scenarios for the major plans switched.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims annual charge difference of -0.06% (saving) for largest plan (Zurich); evidence shows new solution RIY of 2.2% vs existing 0.3%, a net increase of 1.9% in the cost impact, not a reduction.

      - Report asserts charge saving of -0.49% on another plan; evidence does not corroborate this figure in any comparative table.

      - Report projects £12,000 improvement ('effect of switching') for Zurich at mid-growth; evidence shows £12,000 decrease in projected maturity value, not an improvement.

      - Report states small pots withdrawal would net £170 after tax; evidence [P5] does not confirm the £170 figure or the 25%/75% tax split claimed.

      - Report claims 'Lower costs - receiving scheme has lower overall cost structure'; evidence shows new solution carries higher RIY across all growth scenarios for the major transferred plans.

      - Report asserts benefits justify the switch (lifestyling correction, investment choice, adviser monitoring); evidence does not independently corroborate these claimed non-financial benefits as material advantages justifying a net cost increase and maturity value reduction.

      - Immediate capital impact: report does not disclose the £3,305 shortfall in death benefit immediately following the Zurich switch, as evidenced in [P2].

    The core issue is that the report's assertion that switching costs are justified by benefits cannot be reconciled with the evidence. The report claims cost savings (-0.06%, -0.49%) but the switch analysis tables in [P7] and [P1] show the new solution has materially higher reduction in yield (RIY) than the existing arrangements across all growth scenarios. For Zurich (the largest plan), RIY increases from 0.3% to 2.2%, a significant increase in the cost drag, not a saving. The report's statement that 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' contradicts the evidence that the new solution's RIY is 1.9 percentage points higher. The projected maturity values in [P7] show the new solution underperforms at all growth rates: low growth -9.6%, mid-growth -9.4%, high growth -10.0%. These are material cost-to-benefit mismatches. The report cites non-financial benefits (lifestyling, investment choice, adviser monitoring) but provides no independent evidence from categories E (costs/charges) or G (switching/transfer analysis) to support that these benefits outweigh the projected £10,400–£15,000 maturity value reductions. [P2] shows an immediate £3,305 death benefit loss following the switch, which the report does not disclose. The small pots encashment claim of £170 net receipt is not corroborated by the evidence, which discusses Standard Life small pots but does not confirm the tax treatment or receipt amount stated. The extraction note flagged ambiguity on whether charge comparisons were correctly computed. The derived figures confirm that the report's charge differences (-0.06%, -0.49%) are not supported by the fund values they reference: the -0.06% and £268.94 figures compute to fund values in the range of £103,438, which is the Zurich arrangement, not the arrangement label assigned to those rows. This indicates the charge comparison rows are computed on the wrong plan, rendering any switching cost analysis based on them unreliable. The evidence demonstrates that costs do exceed benefits: the projected maturity value reductions of 9–10% are not justified by the non-evidenced claims of lower costs or service improvements.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client

    Report:   The report asserts that all five existing arrangements have 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' (stated on page 5 for all arrangements).     Discrepancies:

      - For four of the five arrangements (People's Pension, Aviva pension my future focus consolidation, Zurich Managed Pn AP, Standard Life pension), no provider documentation is in the evidence pack confirming whether safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, terminal bonuses, or other valuable features exist.

    The requirement mandates that where a switch or transfer is recommended, safeguarded benefits, guarantees or other valuable features that may be lost must be identified and addressed. The pension switch report focuses entirely on charges and projected maturity values under different growth scenarios and does not address safeguarded benefits at all. A comparison cannot be performed because: (1) the report contains no assertion about safeguarded benefits for most arrangements, (2) the evidence pack does not contain provider documentation for four of the five arrangements confirming what safeguarded benefits exist, and (3) the method specified in the comparison guidance is 'Guard: Only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up', but G9.3 has not been evaluated in this pack. Without confirmation of what safeguarded benefits exist in each arrangement, and without the report's own narrative addressing which benefits (if any) are being given up and how that is justified, a meaningful comparison between report assertion and file evidence cannot be performed.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report considers five main options: (1) Leave existing pension untouched; (2) Leave existing pension and switch investment funds; (3) Switch into another existing pension arrangement; (4) Switch into employer's pension arrangement; (5) Set up new pension plan (recommended). For the employer scheme (People's Pension), the report rejects internal fund switches citing no multi-asset funds and limited options, and states funds do not meet risk profile. For Zurich, it rejects internal switch because Zurich offers no risk level 5 funds and blended approach reduces diversification, plus blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options. For Scottish Widows, the report presents both encashment and retention as options then recommends encashment. The report states the main priority is consolidation, control, and drawdown flexibility. For Standard Life, it rejects retention due to lifestyling funds moving outside agreed risk range and recommends switch. The report recommends switching four plans (Scottish Widows encash, Aviva switch, Zurich switch, Standard Life switch) while retaining People's Pension workplace scheme due to ongoing employer contributions.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1-P8, P11-P14) contains projections comparing existing arrangements against new solution. For the overall position, it shows existing pensions projected value of £141,000 versus new solution £138,000 (P1), a reduction of £3,000 or 2.1% at mid-growth rate. For Zurich alone (P3), existing £102,000 vs new solution £100,000 at low growth (0.0%), with critical yield of 0.3% required. At mid-growth 2.94%, Zurich existing £127,000 vs new £125,000, critical yield 3.2%, effect of switch £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. For Aviva existing alone (P5), at mid-growth, existing £4,280 vs new solution £4,260, critical yield 2.9%, effect of switch £20 or 0.5% decrease. For Standard Life (P8), at mid-growth, existing £127,000 vs new £125,000, critical yield 3.2%, effect of switch £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. Provider information (P9, P20, P21, P22) confirms Zurich has no guarantees or safeguarded benefits listed. The suitability report (P10) states for Zurich: 'blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' and recommends switch for consolidation and drawdown flexibility. For Standard Life (P17), report states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' and lifestyling funds move outside agreed risk range. Policy information shows People's Pension is current workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions (P19, P22-23).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report claims for Zurich that 'the blended funds have not performed as well as the options available through Aviva, albeit marginally' (P10) yet the switch comparison table (P3) shows at mid-growth rate 2.94%, Zurich existing yields £127,000 while new solution yields only £125,000, a reduction of £2,000 or 1.6%. This contradicts the narrative claim of better performance in Aviva options.

      - The report recommends switching Zurich citing that blended approach 'reduces diversification' and recommends multi-asset funds (P10), but the proposed solution (P4) invests 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced and 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced — both are balanced/diversified multi-asset funds, not a different diversification profile than the Zurich blended approach described.

      - For Standard Life, the report states (P17) 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but no evidence is provided in the pension switch report (P1-P14, P16) showing a Standard Life-specific switch analysis. The cost comparison cannot be verified from the file.

      - The report prioritizes consolidation as a primary objective (P10: 'your main priority is to consolidate your pensions') yet People's Pension is retained unchanged, contradicting the stated consolidation objective. The report does not explain why consolidation applies to four schemes but not to the workplace scheme.

      - For People's Pension, the report rejects internal fund switch citing 'no multi-asset funds available within the plan and limited range of other options' (P10) but provides no evidence (policy document, fund list, or comparison) confirming People's Pension lacks multi-asset funds or has a limited range compared to the new solution.

      - For Scottish Widows, the report presents encashment as a considered alternative (canonical model states 'Leave pension invested and allow to grow' was inferred as considered with no rejection reasons, then recommends encashment) but does not disclose the tax treatment, regulatory conditions, or why encashment is better than retaining a small pension (£211.32).

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Standard Life-specific switch analysis table with cost and benefit comparison — the report asserts cost difference of 0.08% but no switch comparison table is provided in the evidence., People's Pension fund list and multi-asset fund availability confirmation — the report rejects internal switch citing no multi-asset funds but no plan documentation is evidenced., Tax and regulatory analysis for Scottish Widows encashment — the report recommends encashment of small pension but provides no disclosure of tax treatment or small pots rules compliance., Performance comparison detail for Zurich blended funds vs Aviva multi-asset funds — the report claims Aviva performs better 'albeit marginally' but the switch table shows existing Zurich value is higher than new solution., Employer contribution analysis for People's Pension — while retention is justified by ongoing contributions, no quantification of the value of contributions is provided to support that retention is more beneficial than switching.

    A full comparison cannot be performed because critical evidence is absent. The report asserts alternatives were considered but key alternatives lack supporting analysis in the retrieved documents. Most significantly: (1) For Zurich, the report's narrative claim that Aviva performs better contradicts the switch projection table, which shows the existing Zurich arrangement yields a higher maturity value (£127,000 vs £125,000 at mid-growth), yet the report still recommends switching. This is not explained. (2) For Standard Life, the report claims a cost saving of 0.08% but no Standard Life-specific switch comparison is provided in the pension switch report; the cost claim is unsubstantiated. (3) For People's Pension, the report rejects an in-situ fund switch on grounds of no multi-asset funds, but no plan documentation confirms this limitation, and the stated objective of consolidation is not explained as inapplicable to this plan. (4) The overall position projection (P1) shows existing pensions at £141,000 vs new solution at £138,000, a net loss of £3,000 or 2.1% — yet the recommendation proceeds without addressing why switching results in a lower projected value. (5) For Scottish Widows, encashment is recommended without explanation of tax treatment, regulatory small pots conditions, or comparison to alternative retention. The report does reference consolidation and drawdown flexibility as drivers, but these are not quantified against the projected value loss shown in the switch report. The extracted data notes an inconsistency: transfer value stated as £110,185 but individual arrangements total £116,998.47, a shortfall of £6,813.47, which may affect whether comparisons are reliable. The guard on consolidation is noted: if CHK-002 confirms consolidation was a client-stated objective, then consolidation-as-driver is valid; however, the application is inconsistent (four plans consolidated, one retained with no explanation beyond employer contributions, which are not quantified). The performance comparison for Zurich fails the materiality test: the projection shows existing Zurich performs better, making the recommendation harder to justify on performance grounds alone, yet the narrative emphasizes performance. This is a material discrepancy between what the report asserts and what the projections show.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report claims multiple benefits of switching that justify switching costs: lower costs (0.06-0.49% saving depending on arrangement), greater investment choice and multi-asset fund selection, lifestyling fund drift correction, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and consolidation benefits. For the Zurich arrangement specifically, the report states 'Despite higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year), the benefits of proper risk alignment, consolidation, and active management justify the switch' and 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for one arrangement while claiming cost savings elsewhere.

    File:     The pension switch report (P1-P13, P14) shows: (1) For Aviva plan: existing charge 0.50% vs new solution 0.44% = -0.06% saving (P14); (2) For Zurich plan: existing 0.93% vs new 0.44% = -0.49% saving (P14); (3) For Standard Life: existing 0.26% vs new 0.44% = +0.18% cost increase (P14, P15); (4) Critical yield analysis shows switching requires funds to outperform by 4.8% per year for mid-growth scenario (P18); (5) Immediate post-switch fund value drops from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60 creating £3,305 shortfall in death benefit (P2, P4); (6) Effect of switching shows value decreases under all scenarios: Zurich only comparison shows -£2,000 (2.0% decrease) at low growth, -£12,000 (9.4% decrease) at mid-growth (P4); Alternative comparisons show new solution ranks 5th in maturity value (position) vs existing solution rank 2 (P8).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report claims 'lower costs' as benefit but Standard Life arrangement shows +0.18% cost increase (higher cost), not lower (P15, P14)

      - Report states for Aviva 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution' but charge table shows 0.50% existing vs 0.44% new = 0.06% saving, not cost (P15, P14)

      - Report asserts Zurich switch justified despite 'higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year)' but charge analysis shows -0.49% saving = approximately -£510 annually on £103,438 fund value, not +£268.94 (P8, P14, P19)

      - Report claims benefits justify switching but critical yield shows new solution must outperform existing by 4.8% annually to match maturity value, not exceed it (P18)

      - Report claims consolidation and flexibility benefits but switching projections show value reductions: Aviva Aviva-only £30 decrease (0.6%), Zurich-only £2,000-£15,000 decrease (2-10%) depending on growth rate (P3, P4, P7, P11)

      - Report claims 'lifestyling fund drift' correction but evidence shows client's existing Zurich fund allocation and states funds are blended (Managed AP fund plus Interest Deposits), with no confirmation lifestyling automatic de-risking is actually occurring (P19, P12)

      - Report claims 'adviser charging facilitation' as new benefit but pre-switch arrangement already contemplated adviser charges (1% annual fee paid via provider noted in recommendations section) (P17)

    The guard 'where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite' applies to some arrangements (Aviva -0.06%, Zurich -0.49%) but NOT to Standard Life (+0.18% higher cost). For Standard Life, the report must justify why higher costs are acceptable through benefits. The report's claim of 'lower costs' as a universal benefit contradicts the charge data for Standard Life. The critical yield requirement (4.8% outperformance needed) is not a benefit; it is a cost that must be overcome. The 'Greater Investment Choice' benefit lacks specificity—no fund count or named missing option on existing plans is provided. The 'Lifestyling' benefit claim (portfolio drifting outside risk range) is asserted but the evidence shows existing Zurich arrangement uses blended funds and does not confirm automatic lifestyling is in place. The 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' is claimed as new but the report itself shows adviser charging (1% per annum) was already contemplated in the existing setup. The consolidation benefit is legitimate but the switching analysis shows value reductions across all growth scenarios, contradicting any assertion that consolidation alone justifies the switch. The report's claim that benefits justify switching contradicts its own charge analysis showing either savings (Aviva, Zurich) where the guard applies, or cost increases (Standard Life) without adequate benefit substantiation.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts five replacement actions: (1) Retain People's Pension workplace scheme citing ongoing employer contributions that would be forfeited on transfer, with recommendation to switch investments to Global Investments fund to align with risk rating 5. (2) Encash Scottish Widows plan because it lacks a single multi-asset fund aligned with risk rating 5; combining two funds would leave 20-30% out of market. Plan does not allow adviser management and limits withdrawal flexibility. (3) Switch Aviva plan to better align with risk rating 5, move away from lifestyling fund, lower costs, and enable ongoing adviser servicing. (4) Switch Zurich plan for greater investment choice, multi-asset fund selection properly aligned with risk rating 5, enhanced retirement flexibility, lower annual charges (0.49% saving), and adviser servicing. (5) Switch Standard Life plan away from lifestyling fund that will drift outside agreed risk range, provide fixed risk level 5 portfolio, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, and ongoing adviser oversight. Report acknowledges for Standard Life that despite higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year), benefits justify the switch.

    File:     Provider documentation confirms: (P3, P4) Zurich plan has no guarantees, bonuses, or safeguarded benefits.     Discrepancies:

      - Report states Aviva switch justified by 'lower costs (0.49% saving)' but charges comparison shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs new solution 0.44%, a difference of 0.06%, not 0.49%

      - Report states Standard Life switch justified by 'lower annual charges (0.49% saving)' but derived figures show charge arithmetic on Standard Life row (EA5) computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's actual value (£3,002), making this comparison unreliable

      - Report states Standard Life switch to lower costs but also acknowledges 'higher annual charges (£268.94 more per year)', creating internal contradiction about cost justification

      - Pension switching report (P5, P20) ranks New Solution 5th by projected maturity value (£125,000) below Existing Solution (£127,000), Zurich (£128,000), Aviva (£126,000), and Standard Life (£126,000), contradicting premise that switch produces better outcome

      - Report states Zurich switch justified by better risk alignment but file notes (P16) indicate Zurich requires blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 funds (not risk level 5) and the blend 'have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva'

      - Report recommends Aviva alternative was 'Discounted' because 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR' (P16), yet report recommends switching to Aviva Platform - this appears contradictory unless alternative internal Aviva fund differs from recommended Aviva Platform

      - Report states Scottish Widows lacks single multi-asset fund and requires combining two funds leaving 20-30% out of market, but provider literature (P10, P14) states 'The member can invest into as many of the available funds as they wish' without stating fund unavailability or market mismatch

      - Report justifies retention of People's Pension on employer contributions but does not provide evidence of forfeiture or detail the quantum of ongoing contributions to support materiality claim

    Comparison performed: The report's replacement rationale can be compared against provider literature, file notes, and charges schedules. Material contradictions exist. (1) Cost justification fails: Report claims 0.49% charge savings for both Aviva and Standard Life switches. Aviva shows only 0.06% saving (0.50% to 0.44%). Standard Life charge row (EA5) is computed on Zurich's fund value per derived figures, rendering comparison unreliable. The report also contradicts itself by stating Standard Life switch justified by lower costs while simultaneously disclosing £268.94 higher annual charges. (2) Investment performance contradicts replacement premise: The pension switching report (P5, P20) ranks investment outcomes and shows the New Solution projects to £125,000 (5th rank), below the Existing Solution (£127,000, 2nd rank) and three of four plans being switched from (Zurich £128,000, Aviva £126,000, Standard Life £126,000). This directly undermines the stated objective to consolidate and improve retirement readiness. (3) Risk alignment claims unsupported: Report claims Zurich switch addresses lifestyling drift and achieves risk level 5 alignment, but file notes (P16) confirm Zurich has no risk level 5 funds and requires a blend of risk level 7 and risk level 2 funds. The blend performs worse than Aviva alternatives per adviser notes. (4) Aviva alternative discounting contradicts recommendation: File notes discard Aviva as alternative because 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4' which does not meet ATR, yet the recommendation is to switch to Aviva Platform SIPP. Unless the Platform offers different funds than the rejected alternative fund, the rationale contradicts the recommendation. (5) Scottish Widows encashment rationale not directly evidenced: Report claims plan lacks single multi-asset fund and requires combining funds, but provider confirms member can invest into multiple funds without restriction. Provider literature does not confirm fund unavailability or the 20-30% out-of-market claim. (6) Employer contributions retention: Report justifies People's Pension retention due to ongoing employer contributions but does not quantify contributions or analyse forfeiture materiality. The workplace scheme recommendation is embedded in consolidation objective (OBJ3) to consolidate pensions, creating inconsistency: if consolidation is an objective, retaining one scheme contradicts it. (7) Charges arithmetic failure (derived figures): Standard Life and potentially Zurich charge comparisons are computed on incorrect fund values per the derived figures analysis, rendering the cost-benefit analysis on these plans unreliable. The report's own internal inconsistencies flag that charge analysis lacks a sound foundation. (8) Guard application: File notes document that alternatives were considered and reasons given for discounting them; however, the rationales applied to discard alternatives (e.g., 'not risk rated 4', 'no servicing', 'more expensive') differ from or contradict the rationales used to recommend the solution (e.g., Aviva Platform alternative discounted for risk rating 4, but Aviva Platform recommended despite risk alignment claimed via different mechanism). No evidence contradicts that Scottish Widows encashment was offered advice; the gap is that the rationale (fund unavailability) is not directly corroborated by provider literature, which permits multi-fund investment.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts that employer contribution will be lost if the client transfers away from People's Pension, quoting: 'If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.' The canonical model records employerContributionsLost as true for arrangement 0 (People's Pension) and false for the other four arrangements. The report recommends retaining People's Pension but switching four other plans (Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) to Aviva Platform. Exit penalties are stated as absent across all five arrangements ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'). The report does not explicitly state whether employer contributions are active on the other arrangements or whether they would be lost on those transfers.

    File:     Evidence P1-P8 from People's Pension Policy Info shows a consistent pattern of both Employee Contributions and Employer Contributions being paid into the People's Pension from 2021 through 2023, confirming active employer participation. P11 (Zurich Policy Info) states 'Currently, no payments are being made to this plan' under Current regular payments, with total employer payments shown as £0.00. P14 (SW MT Plan Schedule) and P16 (Standard_Life_Policy_Info) contain no evidence of active employer contributions. P9 shows Standard Life current value of £3,002.02 with 'We've assumed no further payments will be paid.' The evidence confirms People's Pension has active employer contributions but does not clearly establish whether the other four arrangements had active employer contributions at the time of the recommendation.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report addresses loss of employer contributions only for People's Pension (retained) but does not state whether the three switching arrangements (Scottish Widows, Aviva existing, Standard Life) have active employer contributions that would be forfeited by the transfer. The file shows Zurich has no active employer contributions ('Currently, no payments are being made to this plan'), but the report does not disclose this for any arrangement.

      - The extraction notes an internal inconsistency: the recommendation states 'Retain your people's pension plan' but also recommends consolidating pensions (objective OBJ3) and switching four other plans, creating ambiguity about the complete strategy.

      - The report does not provide a detailed plan-by-plan comparison table showing which arrangements had active employer contributions, the amount/frequency of those contributions, and the financial impact of loss on each switching arrangement.

    The rule guard applies: 'An employer contribution that stops on leaving service is not lost by the transfer; check whether the client is still an active member.' The evidence shows the client is actively employed (P3-P8 show contributions through 2023, P14 notes payroll integration possible). The People's Pension evidence (P1-P8) conclusively demonstrates ongoing active employer contributions. The report correctly identifies this risk for People's Pension (retained). However, the report's failure to address whether the other four arrangements have active employer contributions is a material omission. P11 explicitly states Zurich has no current regular employer payments, so no loss there. For Scottish Widows and Standard Life, the evidence is ambiguous but suggestive: P14 contains no evidence of active payments, and P9 (Standard Life) states no further payments will be paid, suggesting the arrangement is not receiving active contributions. The report does not explicitly state this; it merely lists exit penalties as absent. The canonical extraction expected but found absent 'featuresOrGuaranteesLost' for all non-People's arrangements, confirming the report did not analyse safeguarded features or lost contributions on a plan-by-plan basis. The charge analysis on page 6 claims cost savings but does not reconcile this against lost contributions, which would be a material dimension of the switching justification. The derived figures show arithmetic errors in the charge calculations (EA5 and EA5 impact computed on wrong plan), undermining reliability of the cost-benefit comparison that should justify the switches. The report's assertion that switching is appropriate lacks documented analysis of whether the projected fund growth will offset the loss of any employer contributions on the ceding plans.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report identifies six specific foreseeable harms: (1) market volatility and fund fluctuations, mitigated by adviser monitoring and multi-asset funds; (2) underperformance of new funds versus existing, mitigated by critical yield analysis; (3) pension switching costs reducing fund value, mitigated by no exit penalties identified and charge comparison; (4) illiquidity and administration delays, mitigated by liquid cash reserves of £6,000 and 10-15 working day access timeframe; (5) loss of employer contributions if People's Pension is left, mitigated by retaining that scheme; and (6) Inheritance Tax impact from April 2027, mitigated by IHT analysis and monitoring. The report states the client has 'limited experience' but 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed' and that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living.' Risk rating was upgraded from 4 (questionnaire) to 5 (Balanced) on adviser assessment and client agreement. The model shows `/vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[]` is absent — this path was not extracted, meaning the report does not explicitly state how identified vulnerabilities materially shaped the recommendation itself.

    File:     The Risk Profile [P1] shows the client answered 'No understanding/knowledge' to three knowledge questions: stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning and retirement income. The client has no investment experience in unit trusts, stocks, EIS/VCTs or pensions (all answered 'No' to 5-year history). The client does check valuations annually by themselves. Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses are recorded [P1] but the specific notes state 'No additional notes were recorded' [P2]. The agreed risk level is 5 Balanced [P3], justified by the fact-find showing the client 'Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' [P6]. The suitability report [P14] confirms the client was initially rated 4 (Cautious Balanced) by questionnaire but the adviser felt this 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' and 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4.' Page 4 also states 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5.' The report [P14] notes the client is 'in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net' with 'additional £20 per week from your role with the Sea Cadets' and partner Kim contributes £1,100 per month. A fact-find [P7] shows current monthly net income of £1,430 against expenditure of £1,718, creating a monthly deficit of £288. The cashflow model [P4] shows state pension will 'cover your bills in retirement' allowing the pension 'to keep growing for large ad hoc spends.' File notes [P22] record 'Zurich current risk – 7' and alternative options for other arrangements. The report [P20] recommends Power of Attorney be set up: 'It would also be prudent to set up and register Power of Attorney for your financial affairs / health and welfare, to ensure that in the event of your incapacity, your spouse or relative could act on your behalf.' There is no record in the case that the client has done this or that it was executed. No formal vulnerability assessment document exists. No health, mental capacity, or capability assessment appears in the file. No communication support adjustments are recorded. No third-party authority is documented as in force.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts client has 'limited experience' but file shows 'No understanding/knowledge' on three specific investment knowledge areas (stocks/shares, EIS/VCTs, pension planning); this is a material difference in degree, not merely 'limited' but zero recorded knowledge in core domains

      - Report states client will 'continue to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net' but Fact Find [P7] shows employment as 'CDH Recruitment' at £1,200 per month basic income, creating ambiguity about actual employment status and income stability

      - Report upgrades risk rating from 4 to 5 based on adviser assessment that questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite,' but client has zero understanding of pension planning and retirement income and no investment experience; the justification for upgrade to Balanced (risk 5) when knowledge is absent is not explained

      - Report identifies six foreseeable harms but does not state whether or how each harm materially affects the suitability of the recommendation to a client with zero pension knowledge and no investment experience

      - Report recommends 'prudent' setup of Power of Attorney [P20] but file contains no evidence this was completed, executed or registered; no third-party authority or trusted contact is documented as in force

      - Report does not explicitly address whether the client's current monthly deficit of £288 [P7/P12] and reliance on pension drawdown in early retirement affects suitability of the risk rating or recommendation

      - Report states client 'are not reliant upon your pension funds' [P14] but Fact Find shows client needs £1,700 net monthly in retirement and will be drawing from pension to meet shortfall; this contradicts the assertion of non-reliance

      - Fact Find [P6] records consolidation as the client's own objective ('Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has') but this is not framed as a vulnerability or support need in the report's vulnerability section; it may indicate cognitive or comprehension challenge not explicitly assessed

    The check requires evidence that vulnerability information in the report is 'fully supported' and that 'material clarification or additional evidence' is not needed. Several gaps emerge. First, the client's knowledge level: the Risk Profile [P1] documents zero understanding of three core investment domains (stocks, EIS/VCTs, pension planning), yet the report characterises this as 'limited experience' [P14] and upgrades risk from 4 to 5. The file shows no assessment of why zero pension knowledge supports a Balanced (higher-risk) rating, nor any evidence the client comprehends what 'Balanced' entails. Second, the Power of Attorney recommendation [P20] is stated as prudent but no evidence exists that it was completed, discussed in detail or registered. This is a gap between advice given and action recorded. Third, the cash flow: the Fact Find [P12] shows a monthly deficit of £288, yet the report asserts the client 'are not reliant upon your pension funds' [P14]. The cashflow model [P4] shows pension drawdown is planned from age 69. This is internally inconsistent — if income does not cover expenditure and pension draws are forecast, the client is reliant. Fourth, consolidation as a support need: the Fact Find [P6] records the client 'Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has,' which may signal a comprehension or complexity challenge, but the report does not flag this as a vulnerability or support need; it is treated as an objective. Fifth, the foreseeable harms section [vulnerability/foreseeableHarms[]] lists six harms with mitigations, but does not state how they materially affected the recommendation for this particular client. For a client with zero pension knowledge, market volatility and underperformance risk take on heightened significance, yet the report does not address this explicitly. Sixth, the risk rating upgrade: the questionnaire indicated 4 (Cautious Balanced), the client answered zero for pension knowledge, yet the report (and Risk Profile [P14, P3]) record an upgrade to 5 (Balanced) on the grounds that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' [P14]. The file contains no evidence — such as a detailed discussion record, a comprehension test, or a reassessment of capacity for loss in light of zero pension knowledge — to support why a higher risk rating is appropriate for a novice investor. The check is an overlay on appropriateness: a recommendation appropriate for a resilient, experienced client may not be appropriate for one with zero knowledge and a current cash flow deficit. The report asserts suitability but does not demonstrate that the identified vulnerabilities (knowledge gaps, cash flow strain, need for simplification) were material to shaping the advice. The absence of the canonical path `/vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[]` is telling: the report identifies harms but does not state their material impact on the recommendation. A Potential Concern arises because: (1) zero pension knowledge is not explicitly linked to the choice of risk rating; (2) foreseeable harms are listed but not tied to material changes in the recommendation; (3) the Power of Attorney recommendation lacks follow-up or evidence of completion; (4) the assertion of non-reliance contradicts the cash flow forecast; and (5) no formal vulnerability assessment appears in the file to show whether the overlay was applied at all.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts /vulnerability/perClient[]/hasVulnerability = 'No', indicating no vulnerabilities were identified or recorded in relation to the client.

    File:     The case file contains extensive evidence of characteristics and support needs that may constitute vulnerability indicators under FG21/1: (1) Client age 69 with state pension reliance at £230/week and volatile income as HGV driver at £300/week net (P1); (2) Limited investment experience confined to workplace pension arrangements (P1); (3) Negative monthly cashflow of -£288 per month with total expenditure £1,718 exceeding net income £1,430 (P9); (4) Modest liquid savings of only £6,000 against pension assets of £116,997 (P8, P19); (5) Capacity for loss assessment describes potential underperformance would not have 'detrimental impact on day-to-day living' but this sits against cashflow deficit (P1); (6) Risk rating was initially 4 per questionnaire but upgraded to 5, with notation that adviser 'felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' suggesting initial risk assessment may not have captured true position (P1); (7) Client works as HGV driver with income described as 'typically' £300/week, indicating variability not reflected in deterministic cashflow (P1, P14); (8) Fact Find shows 'No Requirement' for accessible format requirement (P10) and no additional notes recorded on investor experience or capacity for loss questionnaire (P13); (9) File notes indicate extensive alternative option analysis and fund substitution decisions (P5, P6), suggesting complexity requiring explanation.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report asserts no vulnerability (hasVulnerability='No') while file records multiple financial and demographic characteristics consistent with vulnerability indicators under FG21/1: age 69, low financial resilience (negative cashflow), limited investment experience, modest liquid assets relative to pension holdings, and volatile employment income.

      - Risk rating was upgraded from questionnaire result of 4 to agreed rating of 5, with adviser statement that initial rating 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite', suggesting potential misalignment between initial assessment and final position that may indicate vulnerability not fully captured.

      - Cashflow shows -£288 monthly deficit (expenditure £1,718 vs income £1,430) while capacity for loss assessment states underperformance would not have 'detrimental impact on day-to-day living', presenting apparent tension with actual financial position.

      - Client income described as 'typically earning around £300 per week net' (word 'typically' indicating variability) but cashflow model appears to use deterministic £300/week assumption without qualification for income volatility.

      - Fact Find records 'No additional notes were recorded' on investor experience and capacity for loss questionnaire (P13), indicating no narrative capture of client circumstances, yet file notes contain extensive commentary on alternatives analysis and fund considerations.

    The report's assertion that hasVulnerability='No' cannot be reconciled with the evidence. The distinction between reporting hasVulnerability='No' and the absence of vulnerability data extraction must be addressed: the canonical model explicitly states the value is 'No', not absent. This is a substantive assertion that contradicts multiple indicators in the file. Under FCA FG21/1, vulnerability drivers include low financial resilience, limited capability, life events, and health/capability concerns. The file evidences: (1) Age 69 with imminent retirement decision; (2) Financial position showing negative monthly cashflow of -£288 despite capacity for loss assessment; (3) Limited investment experience restricted to workplace pensions; (4) Volatile employment income as HGV driver; (5) Modest liquid reserves (£6,000) against pension consolidation of £116,997; (6) Risk rating revision from questionnaire result of 4 to agreed 5, with adviser noting initial rating did not reflect risk appetite—this revision process itself suggests initial assessment captured misalignment. The file notes contain no evidence of vulnerability assessment being explicitly performed and rejected; instead, there is silence in the formal assessment sections (P13 records no notes) combined with operational handling of complexity (extensive alternatives analysis, fund selection complexity). The guards permit that a vulnerability client asked not to record may still have been handled correctly, and that historic vulnerabilities may not apply. However, there is no evidence the client requested non-recording of any vulnerability. The cashflow deficit and modest liquid resources are current-state financial facts recorded in the Fact Find dated 19/09/2025, not historical. The lack of any recorded discussion of vulnerability assessment—neither affirming one occurred and found none, nor documenting why characteristics were not treated as vulnerability—represents a gap in the requirement that vulnerabilities be assessed and, if found, reflected consistently throughout the report. The revision of risk rating suggests the adviser recognised initial assessment inadequacy, yet this recognition does not appear in any formal vulnerability assessment narrative. The requirement asks whether report information is 'fully supported by the evidence provided'. The report's 'No' is not supported; it is contradicted by multiple financial and demographic characteristics in the evidence.
